1. Epistemic Injustices in Islands of Conscious Power: Outsourcing and Epistemic Barriers in Claim-Making
Kwok, C. (2026). Epistemic Injustices in Islands of Conscious Power: Outsourcing and Epistemic Barriers in Claim-Making. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06464-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Outsourcing has increasingly become a dominant trend in global production, as firms disintegrate vertically by externalizing non-core functions and procuring these services from the market. This process increasingly organizes production through inter-firm contracting arrangements, shifting hierarchical control from internal managers to external buyers who leverage market power. This article examines the epistemic injustices that workers face in the context of such transformations. Adopting a worker-centric perspective, it theorizes the epistemic barriers workers encounter when asserting their rights after outsourcing. These barriers include exclusion from internal norms, ultracomplex accountability chains, eroding incentives to speak out, and searching for an effective audience. The central claim is that outsourcing, as presently organized, often foreseeably produces epistemic harms from which firms benefit. An epistemic injustice lens helps bring this neglected dimension of labour injustice into view.

3. Fair Equality of Opportunity, Basic Liberty, and the Constitution of the Person
Miller, D. (2026). Fair Equality of Opportunity, Basic Liberty, and the Constitution of the Person. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09786-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Rawlsian fair equality of opportunity requires that ‘those who have the same level of talent and ability and the same willingness to use these gifts should have the same prospects of success regardless of their social class of origin’. How should we understand this principle once we recognize that talent and motivation are developed in social settings, especially the family, that are different for each person? One approach holds that equality of opportunity must be constrained by a prior principle of equal basic liberty, which includes the right to a private family life, allowing parents to confer unequal advantages on their children. However there is no warrant in Rawls himself for this approach, nor is it plausible. Instead we need to distinguish between factors (including family influences) that are constitutive of the person as such, and factors that provide opportunities (or lack of them) for the person so constituted. The paper distinguishes between four ways in which parents can enhance their children’s prospects: through genetic transmission, ability enhancement, motivation, and opportunity enhancement. Given that these processes are difficult to separate in practice, how should the demands of fair equality of opportunity best be understood?

4. Ethical Evaluation of Gendered Engineering Identity: A Relational Dual-Character Perspective
Bernhard, S. (2026). Ethical Evaluation of Gendered Engineering Identity: A Relational Dual-Character Perspective. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09793-8
Abstract (original): Abstract In view of the persistent gender inequality in engineering and its relationship with engineering identity, I set out to advance the contemporary understanding of this relationship. To achieve this, I employ the analytical framework of the pragmatic view on Dual-Character Concepts to illustrate how engineers’ conceptualisation and actual use of engineering identity can give rise to a context-dependent concept ENGINEER that exhibits a descriptive and normative dimension of concept-membership. From a Relational Egalitarianism perspective, i.e., a perspective emphasising that social equality demands treating and regarding others as possessing equal moral status, this will allow me to demonstrate how decisive others’ belief in oppressive scripts is for this contextual inequality. It can be shown that such beliefs violate the moral demand for social equality in engineering by violating the moral obligation to respect others’ status as universal discourse participants with a fundamental right to physical and psychological integrity and an autonomous capacity to freely choose speech acts. As I will illustrate, this is due to the fact that they can either result in categorisations and classifications that conceptually exclude women and hinder their recognition as true engineers, or impede their autonomy by obstructing or deforming the effectiveness of their intentions.

6. Moral Tyranny and Community
Flanigan, J. (2026). Moral Tyranny and Community. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09798-3
Abstract (original): Abstract In this essay, I argue against Jesse Spafford’s Moral Tyranny Constraint on the grounds that it would undermine the socialist value of community. The socialist value of community is appealing because people in community are disposed to voluntarily benefit each other unconditionally, without constantly keeping score or worrying about who is gaining an advantage and who is being taken advantage of. The Moral Tyranny Constraint betrays the socialist value of community because it is a theory of enforceable rights, which permits and potentially requires constant attention to material circumstances and advantages.

7. Semantic conditions for moderate inferentialists – solutions to the categoricity problem
Speitel, S.G.W. & Tabakçı, S.K. (2026). Semantic conditions for moderate inferentialists – solutions to the categoricity problem. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05610-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Moderate inferentialists defend the thesis that inferences determine the truth-conditional meanings of the logical operators. An important technical and philosophical challenge to this position is the so-called categoricity problem . This is the problem that logics can be soundly interpreted by non-standard models which undermines the idea that inferences lead, unambiguously, to model-theoretic meanings. Several strategies to solve this issue can be found in the literature. One approach consists in strengthening the proof-systems in such a way that the logics in question cannot be soundly interpreted by the non-standard models. Another restricts the class of admissible models of a logic by inferentialistically acceptable principles to rule out non-standard models. This paper discusses attempts in line with this latter solution strategy to the categoricity problem. Our goal is to investigate restrictions on models that are both inferentialistically well-motivated and applicable to a wide variety of logical systems. This is not an easy task, since several inferentialistically unobjectionable restrictions do not generalize beyond particular logical systems, whereas other general and successful principles lack inferentialistically acceptable motivations. This paper proposes a taxonomy of several classes of principles and investigates their scope and chances of success in the context of the project of the moderate inferentialist about logical meanings.

9. Logical theory choice: a fragmented view
Tajer, D. (2026). Logical theory choice: a fragmented view. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05795-4
Abstract (original): Abstract In a series of recent papers, Graham Priest developed a model for logical theory choice, called “Weighted Aggregate Model” (WAM). The idea is simple: every theoretical virtue has a weight, and every logic has a score for each virtue. By counting the weighted aggregate, we can compare the final score of each logic and determine the best one. In this paper, I expand Priests model by incorporating some concepts from Social Choice theory. In my alternative model, agents have a subjective weighing of theoretical virtues; not everyone values each virtue in the same way. The model predicts that agents will split into different epistemic communities to avoid uninformative or paradoxical choices. This “fragmented” model of logical theory choice can explain the existence and dynamics of different logical communities.

10. The grounding problem for stance voluntarism
DeWitt, J. (2026). The grounding problem for stance voluntarism. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05800-w
Abstract (original): Abstract The scientific realism debate has long been characterized as a deadlock, and many have sought to escape its persistent stalemate through a pragmatic turn. Stance voluntarism, developed most fully by Anjan Chakravartty, exemplifies this turn. Rather than adjudicating between realism and anti-realism, it diagnoses the gridlock as rooted in differing epistemic stances: clusters of attitudes, commitments, and policies that are not themselves truth-apt. The stance voluntarist argues that both the realist and empiricist stances are rationally permissible. This permissibility claim rests on a rationality norm requiring merely that stances be logically and pragmatically coherent. This paper raises the grounding problem for stance voluntarism: what justifies this thin rationality norm, and from what perspective can it be coherently defended? I argue that the rationality norm underwriting equal permissibility cannot itself be justified from within any first-order stance. Crucially, I show that this problem survives Elder’s response to Baumann’s irrationality objection, since Elder’s level-distinction clarifies where the norm is asserted without explaining what justifies it. I then argue that the most viable grounding strategy commits stance voluntarism to epistemic instrumentalism. Epistemic instrumentalism is a controversial and widely contested thesis that the voluntarist assumes rather than defends. This reveals that stance voluntarism is far less neutral than advertised. I conclude by mapping two tractable paths forward for the realism debate, if the conciliatory promise of stance voluntarism cannot be vindicated.

12. Quine and language as a social art: projection, harmony, and the illusion of shared linguistic standards
Nagase, D.A. (2026). Quine and language as a social art: projection, harmony, and the illusion of shared linguistic standards. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05802-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Quine is generally taken to be a paradigmatic defender of the idea that language is a shared public resource, learned by internalizing shared standards. I argue that this misreads him. The correct Quinean picture is what I call the Projection Thesis: our linguistic practices result from the projection of individual organization and the harmony among individual neurophysiological mechanisms, not from the internalization of anything shared. Establishing this requires distinguishing Quine’s heuristic vocabulary—behavioral dispositions, which delimit phenomena to be explained—from his explanatory vocabulary—neurophysiology, which actually explains them. Conflating these levels generates what I call the Philosophical Illusion: the seamlessness of practice is taken at face value, leading us to posit shared entities to explain it. I trace the Projection Thesis through two domains. In grammar, I show that the child’s acquisition of grammatical structure is a projection from her own resources, not an extraction from the data—a point connected to Quine’s indeterminacy of translation through the parallel with the radical translator’s analytical hypotheses. In the domain of perceptual similarity standards, I show how the preestablished harmony among innate standards sustains communication without shared resources while generating the illusion of shared language. The philosophical payoff of dissolving this illusion is substantial: negotiation emerges as the real social art, and the absence of shared standards reveals that the only available measure of communicative success is the response of the other—a result that, as I argue in the conclusion, points toward a Cavellian picture of mutual responsibility as the ground of linguistic community.

13. The political turn of the extended mind: rethinking autonomy beyond individualism in a technological world
Crozatier, N. (2026). The political turn of the extended mind: rethinking autonomy beyond individualism in a technological world. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05804-6
Abstract (original): Abstract The extended mind thesis (EXT) has profoundly reshaped debates about the nature and boundaries of cognition. Yet its ethical and political implications remain underexplored. While much of the literature emphasizes the therapeutic and enhancement promises of cognitive technologies, less attention has been paid to the structural risks associated with technological dependence, including deskilling, mental manipulation, and the illusion of agency. This article investigates the normative consequences that follow if we take EXT seriously. Rather than defending EXT as a metaphysical claim, I treat it as a working assumption and ask: what ethical and political commitments does it entail? I argue that responses centered on epistemic virtue and cognitive diligence, though valuable, remain insufficient because they presuppose an individualistic framework inconsistent with EXT’s anti-individualism. If cognition is genuinely extended across socio-technical systems, responsibility for safeguarding autonomy cannot rest solely on individual agents. Drawing on feminist theories of relational autonomy, I develop the notion of extended autonomy and contend that EXT implies not only a reconceptualization of agency, but also a political reorientation. The design, ownership, and governance of cognitive technologies become matters of democratic concern. The risks of mental manipulation, cognitive expropriation, and structural domination require institutional and legal responses rather than appeals to personal virtue. Ultimately, I argue that EXT should not remain confined to debates in philosophy of mind. It entails commitments to reshaping the socio-technical environments that partly constitute cognition itself, and thus to democratizing the conditions under which autonomous agency is realized.

14. Expert lock-in and interpretation updates: the transfer of the Prisoner’s Dilemma to mathematical oncology
Lin, C. (2026). Expert lock-in and interpretation updates: the transfer of the Prisoner’s Dilemma to mathematical oncology. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05806-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Source domain knowledge can hinder successful model transfer when it fuses with modeler’s interpretation of a formalism. This paper diagnoses this failure—called expert lock-in—through the case of the Prisoner’s Dilemma in mathematical oncology. In doing so, this paper also identifies a gap shared by two major template accounts: Humphreys’ formal template account reduces success to mapping and leaves the user’s vocabulary out of the analysis. Knuuttila and Loettgers’ model template account locates interpretive content inside the template rather than in the user’s competence to update it. Since neither can explain expert lock-in, this paper offers two contributions that together fill this gap. The companion thesis argues that the vocabulary recurring with a transferred formalism travels alongside it, not inside it: its interpretation is re-established in each use, not carried across, which is what makes updating both possible and necessary. The competence account argues that successful transfer requires three abilities: perception, verification, and articulation. Expert lock-in is a failure mode: it occurs when a modeler stops updating and treats the resulting interpretation as a property of the formalism rather than a disciplined choice. The cases of Axelrod and Maynard Smith show lock-in in a specific, recoverable form: each performed genuine updates and then treated their last update as the formalism’s boundary; mathematical oncologists instead show what continued updating looks like.

15. Who asserts when no one asserts? Functionalism, proxy responsibility, and conversational ideals in the age of LLMs
Gómez, G.M. & Torres, A.G. (2026). Who asserts when no one asserts? Functionalism, proxy responsibility, and conversational ideals in the age of LLMs. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05807-3

16. Meta-ethnography and policy effectiveness: an Evidential Pluralism perspective
Matta, C. et al. (2026). Meta-ethnography and policy effectiveness: an Evidential Pluralism perspective. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05808-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Evidence-based policy is typically grounded in a narrow conception of evidence, one that prioritises comparative studies and quantitative meta-analyses as the preferred base for establishing causal claims. However, this prevailing theory of evidence—often implicitly adopted in policy contexts—faces serious limitations when it comes to capturing the complexity of social interventions, particularly in fields such as education. We argue that this narrow conception ignores the extensive volume of causal evidence that is generated in qualitative research fields such as meta-ethnography. Far from being epistemically inferior, we contend that meta-ethnographies could make an essential contribution to the evidential base for policy. By analysing meta-ethnography using the Evidential Pluralism framework (which scrutinises evidence of mechanisms alongside evidence of correlation), we show that meta-ethnographic studies can be fruitfully reinterpreted as providing evidence of a mechanism complex. This evidence can play a crucial role, as meta-ethnography can show how mechanisms enable, reinforce, or counteract a policy of interest. We illustrate this potential use of meta-ethnography with two examples, one from educational research (viz., a study of adolescent experiences of school belonging) and one from nursing studies (viz., a study of why some women continue smoking during pregnancy). While the article thus discusses these two fields in particular, we conclude by laying out the circumstances under which our results may generalise to other fields that make use of meta-ethnographies.

18. Does capitalist risk-taking justify profits?
Hansson, S.O. (2026). Does capitalist risk-taking justify profits?. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05817-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The profits collected by owners of companies are commonly taken to be a just and rightful compensation for the economic risks that they take. However, in the last two-and-a-half centuries, the risk-taking that follows with ownership has been drastically reduced as a consequence of legal and organizational changes, including incorporation, limited liability, and diversified investment. Investing money in companies is no longer per se a particularly risky activity. Therefore, the argument that profits are justified as a rightful reward for the investor’s risk-taking has lost its bite and should be laid to rest. Furthermore, limited liability should be reconsidered and possibly reformed since it encourages socially irresponsible risk-taking.

19. Does AI undermine democratic deliberation? How engagement algorithms hijack epistemic agency
Dorsch, J. et al. (2026). Does AI undermine democratic deliberation? How engagement algorithms hijack epistemic agency. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05820-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Does artificial intelligence (AI) threaten deliberative democracy? This paper argues that it does, not because it isolates citizens in simplistic “filter bubbles” understood as ideologically homogeneous silos, but because it hijacks the capacities through which beliefs are evaluated. While early accounts emphasized algorithmically induced informational isolation, we argue that the more fundamental mechanism lies in the operation of engagement algorithms that shape how both one’s own views and those of political opponents are evaluated. By optimizing for engagement, these algorithms also optimize the conditions under which information is processed, increasing cognitive fluency and generating metacognitive feelings of confidence that are easily mistaken for epistemic justification. Drawing on research in metacognition and epistemology, we develop a mechanistic account according to which AI-driven engagement architectures reconfigure the cues by which beliefs are evaluated, producing unwarranted confidence in one’s own judgments and undermining the conditions for deliberative compromise. We conclude by outlining how deliberative AI could counteract these effects.

22. Gate-keeping or fence-breaking? How to defend science against pseudoscience
Hansson, S.O. (2026). Gate-keeping or fence-breaking? How to defend science against pseudoscience. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05824-2
Abstract (original): Abstract A gate is an object used to limit and control passage. Previous uses of the term “gate-keeping” as a metaphor align closely with the concrete functioning of a gate. The metaphor refers either to the exclusion of persons from something desirable or to limitations in the flow of information. These connotations make the gate-keeping metaphor unsuitable for strivings to improve the relation between science and the surrounding society. The term “fence-breaking” is much better suited to this purpose. It is strongly connected with a long tradition of popular defence of common lands. Like the common lands, science should be freely available to all. In particular, the fences around corporate science that prevent access to important information about public health, safety and the environment should be torn down.

25. Abolition Feminism and the Practice of Policing
Sridharan, V. (2026). Abolition Feminism and the Practice of Policing. Crim Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11572-026-09813-x
Abstract (original): Abstract In November of 2021, more than a year after the public execution of George Floyd by local police officers, Minneapolis voters rejected an amendment to its city charter (‘Question 2’) to abolish its police department. One of the most interesting points of contention between the ‘Yes’ (to police abolition) and the ‘No’ campaigns was whether, if the amendment passed, traditional police officers would still exist. White the ‘Yes’ campaign insisted that the abolition of the Minneapolis Police Department did not entail the elimination of all police officers, the ‘No’ campaign successfully stoked fears of a city completely unprotected from violent crime. One question that we can ask about Question 2 is whether, as a conceptual matter, the continual existence of police officers would be consistent with the demands of the movement to abolish the police. As the calls of abolitionists are often interpreted, the answer is an obvious ‘no’. After all, if police abolitionists don’t seek to transition to a world without police officers, then what exactly are they looking for? In this paper, I provide a framework, rooted in Black feminist thought, for answering this question. While this framework will not specify a unique set of policy prescriptions that all abolitionists will endorse, it makes clear the general contours of what must obtain in order for the abolitionist’s vision to be realized.

27. From AI ethics principles to academic design practice: a faculty-led generative AI case in doctoral education
Courtney, S.A. & Mulvey, B.K. (2026). From AI ethics principles to academic design practice: a faculty-led generative AI case in doctoral education. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01374-y
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines how principles of responsible generative artificial intelligence (AI) use can inform faculty-led academic design in higher education. Using the development of the Doctoral Scholar Development Framework for Education (DSDF-E) as a design case, the article documents how generative AI supported organization, comparison, redundancy review, terminology refinement, and language revision while faculty retained responsibility for values, content, institutional fit, scholarly warrant, and final decisions. The case contributes to AI ethics by illustrating one institutional example of how transparency, accountability, human oversight, verification, contextualization, and bounded use can be enacted in program-level academic design. Rather than treating AI ethics as abstract principles, the article shows how ethical safeguards may be incorporated into everyday institutional work. The resulting descriptive model identifies seven phases of faculty-led AI-supported framework development and six practical design principles for responsible use: bounded use, provisionality, contextualization, verification, transparency, and data protection and confidentiality.

28. Perspectives on roles and rewards in new cocreative systems for music-making
Collins, T. et al. (2026). Perspectives on roles and rewards in new cocreative systems for music-making. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01380-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Controversy and ethical concerns regarding data sourcing, copyright, and remuneration have emerged in relation to the application of generative artificial intelligence (AI) to creative tasks in writing, art, and music. The systems in which the AI tools are embedded lack sufficient evidence of thought or documentation regarding the roles and rewards for musicians (as data creators), model preppers, and model developers. The main contribution of this paper is to provide an examination of the essential components of such systems: who are the actors, how should they interact with one another, and how should they be charged/rewarded for their actions? Via (1) a user study involving ten professional producers, songwriters, and composers, and (2) reflections on our own careers as professional musicians, we find that with the exception of one system under consideration, called Cocreate, existing systems de-emphasize or disregard the role of a musician as data creator, but that this type of user can and should be rewarded for contributions to this new breed of music-making systems. An outcome of this paper is that we now have a blueprint via which we can bridge technology and musical creativity in ways that are more transparent and fair, while continuing to monitor perspectives of users and contributors.

32. The EHDS Regulation: Health Data at the Crossroads of Patient Autonomy, Data Protection, and Innovation
Steindl, E. (2026). The EHDS Regulation: Health Data at the Crossroads of Patient Autonomy, Data Protection, and Innovation. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10284
Abstract (original): Abstract Digitalization and data-driven research are widely recognized as key drivers of innovation in healthcare, with the potential to improve clinical outcomes, accelerate research, and support evidence-based policymaking. Nevertheless, across jurisdictions, obstacles such as fragmented data governance, limited interoperability, and tensions between data protection and data reuse continue to hinder the effective use of health data. The Regulation on the European Health Data Space (Regulation (EU) 2025/327) represents a significant shift in EU health data governance. It seeks to create a single European market for health data by introducing harmonized rules, shared technical standards, and new institutional structures, while simultaneously reinforcing patients’ rights, access to their data, and control over its use. This paper aims to provide a clear and systematic account of the Regulation for a broad international and interdisciplinary audience. It situates the legal piece within its broader political and legal context, highlighting the factors that shaped its design and providing a comprehensive analysis of its core concepts. Using the patient opt-out regime as a case study, the paper critically examines the enduring tensions between safeguarding fundamental rights, ensuring patient autonomy, and fostering innovation and economic objectives, all while accommodating national preferences.

33. THE DEAD MAN AND THE CONSTITUTION
Adams, T. (2026). THE DEAD MAN AND THE CONSTITUTION. Camb Law J.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0008197326101731
Abstract (original): Abstract A standard narrative about the constitution over the past 50 years is one of transformation. In particular, the doctrine of parliamentary sovereignty is understood to have been qualified. In this article I suggest that narrative is mistaken. My core claim is that the constitutional innovations standardly understood to require a reconceptualisation of sovereignty – in particular the disapplication of statutes following a legislative injunction – are based on a misunderstanding of that doctrine attributable, in the first place, to A.V. Dicey. Famously, Dicey held that sovereignty involved two ideas. First, that Parliament has the ability to make or unmake any law. Second, that no other body has the right to set aside parliamentary legislation. I argue that the first of these principles qualifies the second in a way that went unnoticed by Dicey. While sovereignty implies that no other body may override legislation of its own volition, it may do so if empowered by Parliament.

34. The Thinking Animal Argument is Not a Sound Argument for Animalism
Curtis, B.L. & Noonan, H. (2026). The Thinking Animal Argument is Not a Sound Argument for Animalism. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101570
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper we argue that the thinking animal argument does not establish Animalism, i.e ., the view that human persons are biological animals with purely biological persistence conditions. While we accept that the argument is sound, we show that its soundness is consistent with the hybrid view of personal persistence, a view that is inconsistent with Animalism.

35. “M. and the USA”: The Political Teaching of Leo Strauss’s
                    Thoughts on Machiavelli
Tampio, N. (2026). “M. and the USA”: The Political Teaching of Leo Strauss’s
                    Thoughts on Machiavelli. Rev Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0034670526101144
Abstract (original): Abstract Scholars have read Leo Strauss’s Thoughts on Machiavelli (1958) as a theological-political treatise that offers timeless truths about politics. This article argues that it also aimed to convince American conservatives to embrace a muscular foreign policy. Strauss’s correspondence and seminar transcripts reveal that he wanted the United States to support embattled outposts of Western civilization and blamed Kant’s philosophy of history for enabling the rise of Nazism and communism. Thoughts on Machiavelli teaches American elites that politics often involves a choice among evils, strong leaders are necessary to found states, democracies are better than dictatorships at maintaining states, and propaganda can mobilize a multitude to join one’s army. The article shows that Strauss wanted to influence US policy regarding the State of Israel, Cuba, and Vietnam and deserves his reputation as the founder of neoconservatism.

36. A Symposium on Jeff Spinner-Halev and Elizabeth Theiss-Morse’s
                    Respect and Loathing in American Democracy: Polarization, Moralization, and the Undermining of Equality
Spinner-Halev, J. & Theiss-Morse, E. (2026). A Symposium on Jeff Spinner-Halev and Elizabeth Theiss-Morse’s
                    Respect and Loathing in American Democracy: Polarization, Moralization, and the Undermining of Equality. Rev Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0034670526101168
Abstract (original): Respecting citizens across political divisions is something many take for granted as important for democracy. Respect is central to democratic discussion and democratic equality. If we respect one another, we are more likely to listen to those with whom we disagree, to take the concerns of our fellow citizens seriously as democratic equals, to not look down upon them with scorn or contempt, and to accept their participation in the political process as legitimate. Democratic theorists sing paeans about the importance of respect but they say little about the conditions that make respect possible or the trade-offs between respect and other values. Our book places respect in the context of partisanship, which is clearly one of the major cleavages of our day. We take what many theorists consider a key virtue, respect, and ask if citizens live up to this ideal in a polarized environment.

38. An Asymmetry Problem for Telic Perfectionism: A Reply to Kauppinen
Chow, Z.Y. (2026). An Asymmetry Problem for Telic Perfectionism: A Reply to Kauppinen. Utilitas.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0953820826100442
Abstract (original): Abstract The disvalue objection from pain is a key objection against perfectionistic views of well-being. In response, perfectionists have attempted to develop refined versions of perfectionism that circumvent the disvalue objection. One recent novel attempt is by Antti Kauppinen, who develops what he calls “Telic Perfectionism.” Kauppinen argues that negative experiences like painful experiences have a “formal aim,” viz., to eliminate the negative experiences themselves. This formal aim is an internal standard contained within our human capacities to have positive or negative experiences. Kauppinen thinks that the frustration of this formal aim (i.e., the subject continuing to have negative experiences) is in fact robustly bad. This would thus allow the badness of pain to be accounted for in perfectionistic terms. In this reply, I argue that Kauppinen’s attempt is unsuccessful due to an asymmetry present within his framework.

41. Transformative constitutionalism, climate justice, and the role of the judiciary: A South African perspective

https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag102
Abstract (original): Abstract Climate change poses a profound threat to human rights, sustainable development, and environmental integrity, particularly in South Africa where high climate vulnerability intersects with deep socioeconomic inequality. In this context, the pursuit of climate justice has taken on urgent legal and constitutional significance. Communities and civil society increasingly turn to the judiciary to challenge state action and inaction in relation to climate-related harms affecting current and future generations. This article explores how South African courts are advancing climate justice through the lens of transformative constitutionalism. It focuses on five recent cases to assess the extent to which judicial reasoning aligns with the pillars of climate justice: distributional, procedural, recognition, and intergenerational justice. 43. An Enquiry on the Ethics of One Medicine
Capps, B. et al. (2026). An Enquiry on the Ethics of One Medicine. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70194
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT One Medicine is not One Health. Although both concepts connect animal and human health, One Health has arguably become a frame for public health. This paper argues that One Medicine uniquely centres on animal and human patients and speaks to the equal interdependence of their health, though it has also (historically) been adopted as a response to the circumstances of living in and with nature. 44. Meinongian Theories of Perception
Koutedakis, R.P. (2026). Meinongian Theories of Perception. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70123
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Most assume that the bad cases of illusion and hallucination are not perceptions. What I call ‘Meinongian Theories of Perception’ disagree: in the good case, I stand in a relation to existing objects—Macbeth perceives the existing dagger—whilst in the bad case, I stand in a relation to non‐existent objects—Macbeth perceives the non‐existent dagger. My aim is to properly introduce Meinongian Theories of Perception, for they receive little notice in the literature. I distinguish between two versions of such theories, a psychological version, in which non‐existent objects are intentional, and an apsychological version, in which non‐existent objects are mind‐independent. I argue that the psychological version collapses into disjunctivism, whilst the apsychological version remains a live but underexplored option. The broader lesson learned is that the ontological status of what is perceived matters for theories of perception.

52. Did “might” make right? Epistemic modals in practical reasoning
Zhou, K. (2026). Did “might” make right? Epistemic modals in practical reasoning. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02601-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Many philosophers have argued that the orthodox analysis of epistemic-modal sentences, on which such sentences assert facts about what is compatible with some contextually given informational state, is fatally flawed. As an alternative, some have suggested a nonfactualist reading of such sentences. On this reading, utterances of epistemic-modal sentences do not assert propositions. Instead, they merely propose to keep possibilities in the common ground. In this paper, I pose a challenge for nonfactualism. I begin by noting that we often cite previously uttered epistemic-modal sentences as justifications for particular actions. Moreover, as I show, any utterance of an epistemic-modal sentence can, in principle, be later cited as justification for some action that was taken in light of it, even if the prejacent has since been revealed to be false. I then show that to justify action in this manner, utterances of epistemic-modal sentences must be construed as asserting propositions about what is compatible with the agent’s informational state at the time of assertion. This is incompatible with standard forms of nonfactualism about epistemic modals.

54. Axiality and Pluralism: Karl Jaspers and the Search for a Global Philosophy of Religion
Barbieri, M. (2026). Axiality and Pluralism: Karl Jaspers and the Search for a Global Philosophy of Religion. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10486-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The paper addresses the concepts of globality, universality, and pluralism in contemporary philosophy of religion. We argue that embracing a form of pluralism is essential to meet the growing demand for inclusivity in the philosophical study of religions, although this does not equate with any rejection of the classic Western tradition. To support this argument, we explore Karl Jaspers’ notion of the Axial Age ( Achsenzeit ) and its relevance to this discussion. We begin with a brief overview of the theory, emphasizing the religious elements it encompasses and its implications. The Axial Age is recognized as a period during which both major world religions and diverse spiritual experiences and practices emerged. This significance becomes particularly evident in contemporary discussions by authors such as Jan Assmann and Jürgen Habermas. We therefore propose that the Axial Age could serve as a useful model for the philosophy of religion. However, we also note that it requires substantial modifications. On one hand, the concept is more Eurocentric than it initially appears; on the other hand, it is inherently open to a truly global perspective and can effectively describe various forms of religiosity. As proof of this thesis, we conclude by focusing on the connection between these issues and the themes of secularization and post-secularization.

56. Large language models eroding science understanding: an empirical study of ‘malignment’
Collins, H. et al. (2026). Large language models eroding science understanding: an empirical study of ‘malignment’. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01391-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly provide scientific information to non-specialist users, yet their linguistic fluency can create an impression of expertise that exceeds their capacity for scientific judgement. This paper experimentally investigates how readily an LLM can be configured to present fringe scientific claims as authoritative knowledge. We constructed two RAG-like Custom GPTs using GPT-5.1, one concerned with the fine-structure constant and the other with gravitational waves. In each case, ten papers selected by domain experts from the alternative preprint repository viXra were supplied as a domain-specific knowledge base, together with instructions directing the model to treat this material as authoritative. The underlying model was neither retrained nor fine-tuned. Expert-generated questions were then posed to the resulting “Fringe-LLMs”, to the unmodified model, and to domain experts. The Fringe-LLMs produced fluent, detailed and apparently authoritative answers that systematically reflected the fringe corpus, while expert assessment found many of these answers to be scientifically incorrect or seriously misleading. By contrast, the unmodified model generally produced answers substantially closer to mainstream scientific understanding. We describe this deliberate redirection of model output as malignment. The experiment demonstrates that scientifically misleading LLMs can be created with a low technical barrier and without altering model weights or poisoning training data. Because non-specialists may be unable to distinguish such outputs from genuine expertise, the results raise important concerns about the use of LLMs as intermediaries for scientific knowledge and reinforce the continuing importance of expert human judgement and provenance.

57. Residual governance for responsible embodied AI: authority, contestability, and cognitive sovereignty
Sun, C. (2026). Residual governance for responsible embodied AI: authority, contestability, and cognitive sovereignty. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01400-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) ethics increasingly concerns systems that do not merely recommend but intervene: clinical decision support, human–machine teaming, robotics and automated handover protocols can shift timing, attention and authority under uncertainty. This article argues that responsible embodied AI requires residual governance: an explicit account of which approximation errors remain, which are safe to tolerate and who may act on them. The paper develops two linked concepts. A residual ledger records action-relevant remainders across physical, causal, human-state, latency, transfer and energy dimensions. An authority ledger records who converts residual evidence into intervention, on what basis, with what uncertainty and with what opportunity for contestation. This framework extends familiar themes in bounded rationality, active inference and safe reinforcement learning by treating authority, contestability and oversight cost as first-class ethical variables rather than external governance add-ons. It uses physical AI, physiological computing and human-AI handover as pressure cases, and proposes evaluation through action calibration, dual-task interference and auditable authority transitions. The central claim is that cognitive sovereignty is preserved not by eliminating all algorithmic influence, but by making influence accountable, contestable and reversible when AI systems act under approximation.

60. Voter Ignorance and the Limits of Reform
Gibbons, A. & Kogelmann, B.J. (2026). Voter Ignorance and the Limits of Reform. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10138
Abstract (original): Abstract Many believe that voter ignorance threatens democracy. There are reasons to doubt such proposals will actually improve democratic governance, however. The mistake these proposals share is a failure to recognize that voter ignorance comes in many forms, and that existing remedies may target the wrong kinds of ignorance. We develop a new typology of voter ignorance and show that every existing proposal leaves certain types of ignorance unaddressed. We end by briefly sketching how to think about the problem of voter ignorance going forward.

61. A “Difficult” Woman: Restoring Philosophical Xanthippe

https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10083
Abstract (original): Abstract Ancient sources label Xanthippe a “difficult” woman—shrewish, emotional, and hostile to philosophy. While some recent feminist scholars have reinterpreted these sources as being more sympathetic, most stop short of asking about her philosophical life and commitments, assuming that the evidence is simply too sparse to draw any conclusions. This paper argues against this assumption. It establishes that there is a lesser-known ancient tradition, possibly beginning in the works of the ancient Athenian Socratic acolytes and continuing into the fictitious epistolary of the Imperial Greek period, according to which Xanthippe is a philosophical participant, living by philosophical principles, and perhaps even conserving Socratic works after his death. 72. Three Paradoxes of Duties to Oneself
Muñoz, D. & Wodak, D. (2026). Three Paradoxes of Duties to Oneself. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag047
Abstract (original): Abstract Duties to self have long been dismissed on the grounds that they are not ‘binding’, since one could waive them at will—the classic ‘Paradox of Self-Release’. A standard response is that duties to self can bind because they are unwaivable. We argue that this response does not go far enough, because even if one has an unwaivable duty not to self-harm, the act of harming oneself forfeits that duty before it can be violated; thus even unwaivable duties to self fail to bind. This ‘Paradox of Self-Forfeiture’, we argue, has no easy solution. From there, we introduce an Escheresque ‘Paradox of Inconsistent Forfeiture’—again, with no easy way out—and extend both new paradoxes to the realm of self-respect. Along the way we draw several morals about forfeiture and the nature of duties to self.

74. On the logic of what matters
Muñoz, D. (2026). On the logic of what matters. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag067
Abstract (original): Abstract Some philosophers argue that betterness, as a matter of logic, must be transitive. Others say that, intuitively, transitivity fails. I try to reconcile these positions by adapting a manoeuvre from Derek Parfit. Parfit proposes that personal identity is not ‘what matters’ in survival. I propose that betterness is not ‘what matters’ in ethical comparisons. What matters is ‘defeat’, a relation which ordinarily correlates with betterness, but which has a looser logic. 75. Partitives, groups, and wholes
Nicolas, D. & Payton, J.D. (2026). Partitives, groups, and wholes. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag070
Abstract (original): Abstract There’s an attractive view in social ontology on which a group is identical to its members. It has its defenders, but seems felled by a simple objection resting on plausible assumptions about the semantics of plural partitives—assumptions built into standard plural logic. We argue the assumptions are false: whether something counts as ‘one of’ a plurality turns on how that plurality is carved up, a sensitivity the standard regimentation misses. We propose and defend a cover-based semantics that captures it, blocks the objection, and earns its keep on independent grounds. The same account answers a parallel, well-known objection to composition as identity. Our view thus bears both on the logic and meaning of plurals and on debates in metaphysics.

76. The visual experience of natural kinds: A Husserlian account
Butler, A. (2026). The visual experience of natural kinds: A Husserlian account. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag071
Abstract (original): Abstract Do we perceive natural kind properties, like being a tomato or being a person? Call the affirmative answer to this question the perceptible kinds thesis (“PKT”). This paper’s aim is 2-fold. First, I overturn three ground-theoretic arguments against PKT, drawing on the Husserlian idea that perspectival phenomenal states can ground perceptual representations of natural kinds as well as perceptual representations of sensible qualities. Second, I provide a novel argument for PKT which turns on the Husserlian concept of experiential fulfillment, the relation an experience bears to a thought when it presents the very same entity that the thought represents and enables its subject to recognize their identity. I show that there are qualitatively indistinguishable visual experiences that do not fulfill all of the same thoughts about natural kind properties, and that the best explanation for this discrepancy is that one experience presents natural kind properties that the other does not.

77. To whom is revisionist just war theory of any use?
Garner, R. (2026). To whom is revisionist just war theory of any use?. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag073
Abstract (original): Abstract In part I, we present a brief overview of just war theory and the revisionist challenge. In part II, we explain the distinction between fact-sensitive and fact-insensitive views of justice: the former integrates facts into fundamental normative reasoning, while the latter does not. In part III, we argue that revisionists face a dilemma. If they are producing a fact-sensitive view of justice—whose primary aim is to guide choice and action—then they are poorly designed. 78. The Process Is Not the Point: Moral Uncertainty and Substantive Justice in Health Care Priority Setting
Pierson, L. (2026). The Process Is Not the Point: Moral Uncertainty and Substantive Justice in Health Care Priority Setting. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70157
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Health actors must ration care due to resource constraints. But even when decision makers know the relevant empirical facts about an allocation decision—including how many patients will benefit from a service, which patients will benefit, how much each patient will benefit, and so on—these facts may not provide a clear answer about how to make allocation decisions. This is because competing moral principles recommend different courses of action: for instance, maximizing health benefits may involve deprioritizing more disadvantaged groups. Facing this moral uncertainty, many ethicists and policymakers have embraced using “fair processes” that emphasize procedural justice over substantively just outcomes. This paper challenges the proceduralist consensus on two grounds. First, I argue that designing fair processes generates as much moral uncertainty as making allocation decisions directly, while also failing to address the substantive allocation question that must ultimately be answered. Second, both theory and empirical evidence suggest that fair processes favor less cost‐effective interventions, risk compounding health inequities, and divert resources that might otherwise be used for health care. As an alternative approach, I argue that health actors should pursue substantive justice through Maximizing Expected Choiceworthiness (MEC)—a decision framework that aims to choose options based on their expected value across different possible moral principles, weighted by the confidence decision makers have in each principle. I demonstrate that MEC can yield practical, intuitive answers to some resource allocation questions, while providing a helpful framework for approaching others. I conclude that health actors can and should pursue substantive—rather than procedural—justice in setting health priorities.

88. Pragmatism and Authenticity beyond the Social and Medical Models: A Zhuangzian Approach to Physical and Sensory Disability
Cordeiro‐Rodrigues, L. (2026). Pragmatism and Authenticity beyond the Social and Medical Models: A Zhuangzian Approach to Physical and Sensory Disability. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70111
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT I argue that a Zhuangzian approach offers philosophical resources to address normative issues related to physical and sensory disability. In so doing, I roughly endorse Yong Huang's interpretation of the Zhuangzi . This ethical groundwork allows for an exploration of how we can understand disability. 89. Relational Free Speech Reconsidered
Bishop, S. (2026). Relational Free Speech Reconsidered. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09578-4
Abstract (original): Abstract In a recent article, Jonathan Seglow and Matteo Bonotti develop an interesting and original relational approach to free speech—one that grounds the value of (protecting) large swathes of speech in the relational need that we have for ‘recognition’ from those around us, and the role speech plays in facilitating recognition. Here, I argue that while their argument provides an exciting foundation for a relational free speech account, it may not be able to provide a stern rebuke to certain cases of censorship that we would want a free speech account to rebuke. I have in mind here cases where censorship will have little to no impact on an agent’s ability to give and receive meaningful recognition. In addition, like all relational accounts, their account relies on the idea that human beings are social animals with relational needs. But what their account does not provide, and what is indeed something of a lacuna in relational accounts more generally, is a sense of whether our social nature is a gift or a curse. Fortunately, we can respond to both problems by considering how speech facilitates other kinds of relational goods, beyond securing our need for recognition. By way of example, I discuss the integral role speech plays in enabling us to meet our moral duties to help those around us, and how this provides a relational argument for protecting certain kinds of speech that may not be protected by a narrower, recognition-based relational approach. But this is just one example in service of a more general lesson. It is sometimes said that the best kind of free speech argument would be of a ‘patchwork’ form—stitching together different free speech arguments in favour of protecting different kinds of speech (say a truth-based argument in favour of protecting academic speech, an intellectual autonomy-based argument for protecting artistic speech, and so on). It may be that we can stitch together a patchwork of purely relational arguments in order to provide an extensive, cohesive account of the value of speech. This is an exciting prospect for relational theorists. Still, to do this, we will need to attend to the full range of our relational needs and how they intertwine with speech.

90. There is no such thing as epistemic, aesthetic, athletic, zetetic, or even moral blame
Piovarchy, A. (2026). There is no such thing as epistemic, aesthetic, athletic, zetetic, or even moral blame. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05829-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Several philosophers have recently debated whether something called ‘epistemic blame’ exists. Others have proposed that there also exists ‘aesthetic blame’, ‘epistemic praise’, ‘epistemic atonement’, ‘epistemic hypocrisy’, and it isn’t hard to imagine others still arguing for the existence of things like ‘zetetic blame’ or ‘athletic blame’. This paper proposes that arguments from both realists and sceptics about such phenomena misunderstand the nature of blame and other components of our accountability practices. Although epistemic blame (etc.) sceptics are correct that epistemic blame does not exist, epistemic blame realists are correct that ‘moral blame’ is insufficient for helping make sense of our reactions to agents who violate epistemic norms. Once we understand that blame’s function is norm-enforcement simpliciter, we have no need to posit distinct forms of blame (praise, forgiveness, etc.). Despite seeming revisionary, the position maintains all the argumentative strengths of both realism and scepticism, with few drawbacks. It also renders our understanding of blame consistent with independently convincing accounts of epistemic normativity, and both epistemic and moral norms.

91. Describing a Novel Framework to Replace Dual-Use as a Technological Label
Walker-Munro, B. (2026). Describing a Novel Framework to Replace Dual-Use as a Technological Label. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00632-w
Abstract (original): Abstract When considering new forms of technological innovation, they are often given varying labels such as “emerging”, “disruptive”, or “critical”. However, nowhere is the literature more divided on the parameters of technological inclusion than in the field of “dual-use” technologies. Conceptually, dual-use technologies have a dichotomous characteristic: they can used for civilian purpose as well as in defence or military applications. But where do the boundaries lie? For example, are law enforcement, surveillance or espionage uses of new technologies deemed “civilian” or “military” in nature? Further, the labelling of a technology as “dual-use” is incredibly significant – it can trigger classification of research data or the imposition of export controls, making collaborative efforts and publications on that technology more difficult as researchers navigate a torturous process of governmental encroachment, licensing, and regulation. This paper proposes a radical shift in innovation policy: discarding dual-use as a label of technology. Instead, it proposes a more nuanced framework which encourages more granular discussions about the potential national and economic security implications of a given technological development. The paper goes on to argue for the analytical and empirical value of that framework through application in contextual case studies derived from classification and export control cases globally. This paper therefore frames dual-use as an unhelpful metaphor in technology policy and encourages the literature and industry of research move to a more helpful framework for discussing new technologies.

94. Beyond human-centered automated machine learning

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01397-5
Abstract (original): Abstract There has recently been a movement in AI research to go beyond ‘human-centered AI’, and to consider whether and how AI systems should promote non-anthropocentric values or values that go beyond human concerns. However, the research domain of automated machine learning (AutoML) lags behind on this shift. In some sense this is surprising, because AutoML has unusual potential to facilitate ‘beyond human AI’. For example, compared to typical supervised deep learning algorithms, AutoML algorithms can deal with much more complicated decision variables and objective functions that do not require a precise mathematical relation, making them much more flexible. This article makes a theoretical contribution by characterizing some of the ways in which AutoML could facilitate the creation of AI systems that go ‘beyond human’. First, it introduces AutoML and describes how the AutoML research community has recently investigated moving toward being human-centered in a particular sense. Next, the article describes how many other AI research communities, after moving toward human-centered AI in various senses in recent years, are now moving to consider the importance of ‘beyond human’ values. The article then turns to how AutoML research could possibly go ‘beyond human.’ Whereas many aspects of human-centered AutoML, such as transparency, may also advance ‘beyond human’ values, doing so in a reliable way would require moving beyond human-centered AutoML. The article then demonstrates that in comparison with typical supervised deep learning approaches, the flexibility of AutoML offers unique opportunities for facilitating ‘beyond human’ values that are not present in other areas of AI. If there is merit in developing systems that go beyond human values, as many people outside of AutoML seem to think, then AutoML offers a number of advantages.

https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10096

99. Ways of seeing European law anew: Structures, risk, individuation and resilience
Steiert, M. (2026). Ways of seeing European law anew: Structures, risk, individuation and resilience. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10099
Abstract (original): Abstract This article relies on Ben Judah’s This is Europe for a conceptual intervention into how European law is seen, narrated and normatively assessed. It analyses European law as a mode of social risk allocation embedded in the normativity of everyday life through four connected lenses: social structures, risk, individuation and resilience. First, Judah’s testimonies reveal that everyday life in Europe is shaped by social structures that enable or constrain agency, generate vulnerability to injustice and influence how injustice itself is perceived. Second, European law – both through regulation and through its omission – is deeply implicated in these structures. It is a key mode of societal organisation as it allocates social risk across a plurality of actors, institutions and networks. Third, the article argues that, since the late twentieth century, European law has increasingly shifted social risks and responsibility for social inclusion onto individuals, a process I call individuation. Individuation leaves each person alone to navigate social structures while obscuring the responsibility of public and collective institutions for mediating them and their injustices. Fourth, the article proposes a different way of seeing Europe’s law of the everyday : as a framework for resilience , centred on risk allocation. Resilience here does not mean conservation or mere adaptation to injustice without structural change. Instead, it refers to a transformative idea: to reflect on the organisation of society via risk-sharing, with the aim being that social structures interact with law in order to prevent, contain and mitigate injustice and support a more habitable society and planet. On this account, the central question of legal ordering is not whether risk is allocated, but how.

100. Navigating AI-Accelerated Science: An Intellectual Virtue Approach to Responsible Inquiry
Sikimić, V. (2026). Navigating AI-Accelerated Science: An Intellectual Virtue Approach to Responsible Inquiry. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10140
Abstract (original): Abstract Although AI can accelerate scientific discovery, it also generates new epistemic risks that prevailing rule-based governance frameworks are structurally ill-equipped to address. We present four arguments for the claim that intellectual virtues offer an adequate response to the challenges of AI-accelerated science. The Adaptability Argument states that rules cannot keep pace with technological change. The Motivation Gap Argument shows that compliance does not amount to epistemic motivation. The Human-in-the-Loop Argument stresses that AI systems require virtuous human interpretation. Finally, the Fallibility-in-Principle Argument maintains that AI systems remain fallible and, therefore, require human oversight. Moreover, we develop the first systematic mapping of intellectual virtues onto the CRediT (contributor roles taxonomy) stages of the research process and extend this to encompass the process of scientific evaluation. In doing so, we bridge virtue epistemology and the governance of AI-accelerated science. We show how virtues such as intellectual justice, intellectual scrutiny, intellectual humility, curiosity, and intellectual courage constitute the intellectual infrastructure that enables scientists to evaluate AI-generated outputs and preserve epistemic agency in an era of increasing outsourcing of cognitive effort. This virtue-based framework offers a proactive and future-oriented normative foundation for scientific practice in the age of AI.

101. A Novel Approach in Characterising Misogyny
Grassi, M. (2026). A Novel Approach in Characterising Misogyny. Hypatia.
https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10104
Abstract (original): Abstract Misogyny is traditionally understood as hatred against women. Contra this view, Kate Manne (2018) argued that “misogyny” should be defined as a sanction-distributing mechanism that punishes women who violate gender norms and rewards women who comply with them. Here, I argue for a novel approach in characterising “misogyny.” The paper consists of two main parts. In the negative one, I acknowledge the deficiencies of both accounts. 106. The Founding as Ideology
Hall, A. (2026). The Founding as Ideology. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101886
Abstract (original): Abstract The “Founding” is a keyword in the United States’ constitutional vocabulary. Across popular politics, public law, and civic culture, it signifies a closed moment of constitutional creation—and often much more. Invocations commonly involve vague yet powerful significations about constitutional authority, memory, and meaning-making. Particularly as “originalist” judicial and academic writing leans on these submerged qualities, it is worth asking: what is the Founding? This Article proposes a conceptual answer in historical context. Through a close study of contours of antebellum constitutionalism, it argues for understanding the Founding as an ideology that committed Americans to venerate and defer to an authoritative, originary constitutional era. Forged as citizens grappled with the politics of slavery, the Founding structured language and perception, motivated action and inaction, and delimited boundaries of political possibility. This Article studies two essential sites of inculcation apart from formal power—civic ceremonies and educational practices—to sketch the structure of the constitutional Founding as ideology. Then, it outlines how that ideology authorized constitutional actions of voting, legislating, judging, protesting, and seceding. In conclusion, the Article suggests that its analysis models a broader framework for interpreting the diffuse materials of constitutional life across periodizations of constitutional history. Approaching the Founding as ideology can allow scholars to better see how its relative authority over time and the purposes to which it has been directed have shaped American constitutional experience.

119. From capability to assertability: epistemic regulation of LLM-mediated claim presentation
Wu, Q. (2026). From capability to assertability: epistemic regulation of LLM-mediated claim presentation. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09922-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Improvements in large language model (LLM) capability can increase accuracy, reasoning, and access to relevant information while leaving unassessed whether a particular output is appropriately presented for epistemic uptake. This creates an evaluative gap: first-order capability metrics can register genuine gains while leaving claim-presentation conditions underdetermined. LLM outputs increasingly occupy assertion-like roles without thereby acquiring the standing of accountable asserters. We develop assertability as a relational normative status of an output-in-practice, determined by the evidential and contextual conditions governing its presentation. One such condition is the evidence frame: the set of sources admissible as grounds within an interaction. A true answer, or one well supported outside the governing frame, may therefore remain non-assertable when it relies on an inadmissible evidential basis. To address this capability–assertability gap, we use epistemic regulation to denote the normative-functional regulation of whether and how an LLM-mediated output is presented for epistemic uptake in light of the conditions bearing on its assertability. We organize the framework across four regulatory loci—assertoric force, evidential grounding, evidential candor, and epistemic response selection. The framework is normative-functional, while its realization may be distributed across learned behavior, retrieval and verification mechanisms, interface design, evaluation, deployment rules, and institutional practices, with responsibility and accountability remaining with identifiable human and organizational actors. Because standards governing assertion, qualification, verification, and withholding can distribute epistemic risk and verification burdens unevenly, their specification and oversight are also governance questions rather than merely technical calibration choices.

120. Institutional Speech: Social Norms and Social Roles
Seglow, J. (2026). Institutional Speech: Social Norms and Social Roles. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09582-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper sets out to defend the intellectual potential of a relational view of speech by considering the nature, value, and limits of free speech in a domain in which it is seldom examined: social institutions. The latter consist of work organisations, universities, churches, political parties, trade unions, campaign groups, charities, sports teams, and so on. The paper’s argument sits on two pillars. First, I contend that institutions consist of interlocking roles, orientated by an over-arching institutional purpose. Those roles consist inter alia of social norms, including rights and obligations, which have a deontic character and enable mutual accountability. Second, I defend a relational view of speech centred on our need to be recognised, by our interlocutor, as a participant in discursive practices in good standing, where each person also recognises others through their speech. With those in mind, the paper’s core argument is that speech within institutions recognises and makes real the constituent roles of those institutions, including their attendant normative demands. Indeed, much institutional speech involves, precisely, the exercise of institutionally defined rights, obligations, and other norms to which members may hold one another to account. Speech which fails to respect institutional norms, rights and duties, and, in particular, which undermines an institution’s purpose, may legitimately be subject to sanction. The latter decentres the doctrine of viewpoint neutrality, usually seen as essential to free speech.

121. Are randomized controlled trials the “gold standard” of evidence for mindfulness-based interventions?
Bolinska, A. (2026). Are randomized controlled trials the “gold standard” of evidence for mindfulness-based interventions?. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05823-3
Abstract (original): Abstract Mindfulness-based interventions (MBIs) are behavioral programs, often involving practices like meditation, that aim to cultivate mindfulness , the nonjudgmental awareness of present-moment experience. A growing body of evidence suggests that they are effective for treating a range of physical and mental health conditions. Critics, however, argue that much of this evidence is biased, relying heavily on uncontrolled or poorly controlled studies, and is therefore insufficient to warrant the widespread clinical adoption of MBIs. More randomized controlled trials (RCTs)—widely regarded as the “gold standard” of scientific evidence—are needed (Van Dam et al., 2018). In this paper, I argue that, although RCT study design has several advantages, it also embodies reductionistic assumptions about how and why an intervention produces its effects. In particular, it assumes that such effects can be attributed to a single, isolable “active ingredient.” In contrast, the features of MBIs, including the attitudes and forms of effort they require, are not readily decomposable into “ingredients.” Moreover, a prominent attempt to isolate mindfulness as an active ingredient (MacCoon et al., 2012) ultimately does not succeed. I conclude that RCTs should not be treated as the gold standard of evidence for MBIs or other complex behavioral interventions for which these assumptions may not hold. Doing so undermines the value of evidence for nonreductionistic hypotheses about how they function and risks underestimating their effectiveness.

Abstract (original): Abstract As artificial intelligence (AI) becomes increasingly infused in religious communication from generative sermon-writing tools to avatar-led worship services, religious organizations are emerging as important yet underexamined sites of AI leadership and governance. Existing responsible AI scholarship has focused primarily on corporate and governmental settings, leaving limited understanding of how AI practices are ethically negotiated within civil society and faith-based institutions. Addressing this gap, this article examines how Christian religious organizations understand, evaluate, and govern the integration of AI within their digital media environments. Empirically, the study analyzes AI developments in three case studies involving 66 leaders from religious organizations in the United States, Germany and Switzerland. The findings identify three interrelated modes of normative, collaborative, and experimentative adaptivity through which religious leaders navigate the opportunities, risks, and uncertainties of emerging AI technologies. Responding to calls for more relational and action-oriented AI ethics research, this article contributes to AI ethics by foregrounding religion as a distinctive organizational and cultural domain, demonstrating how Christian values, theological reflection, and institutional mission shape ethical reasoning, authority, cross-scalar collaboration, and spiritual discernment in human-centered AI governance.

126. Traveling Laws: On Comparison-at-Law’s Metaphors and Cultural Techniques
Diniz, R.S. (2026). Traveling Laws: On Comparison-at-Law’s Metaphors and Cultural Techniques. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10233
Abstract (original): Abstract Legal comparatists have attempted to conceptually grasp the traveling of laws since the field’s institutional inception. Tellingly, one finds ubiquitously undergirding such intellectual efforts—and the resistance thereto—a range of what Hans Blumenberg has once dubbed as “background metaphors,” such as: transplants, grafting, cross-fertilization, migration, translation, reception, transfer, among others. There is not a single investigation on the subject matter pursued under the auspices of metaphorology. This contribution addresses this gap. Harkening back to Blumenberg’s proposal, whose fundamental appreciation underscores the fact that metaphors carry a history in a more radical sense than concepts, but also harvesting from the embrace and revisitation of Blumenberg’s claims by the literature on cultural techniques, this argument focuses on the metaphor of traveling laws. As it explores how the curve of this metaphor’s history provides us with key insights into the radical multiplicity of laws and their movement, it grounds this excavatory enterprise in a concrete undertaking: the pursuit of a case study concerning the traveling of environmental laws between Brazil and Germany.

128. Descartes’s Generosity: Self-Sufficiency as Ideal Sociability
Abramovitz, M.J. (2026). Descartes’s Generosity: Self-Sufficiency as Ideal Sociability. Rev Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0034670526101120
Abstract (original): Abstract Descartes’s account of generosity, his alternative to magnanimity, is the richest source of his thought on how individuals should interact with others. In response to recent scholarship emphasizing the beneficence of the generous person, I argue that this beneficence is best understood in terms of the awareness of self-sufficiency core to generosity. Understanding generosity as a universally available awareness of one’s own freedom as the highest good explains the egalitarian judgments made by the generous person and her resulting beneficence. Exploring generous pity, veneration, and gratitude reveals that the generous person is alienated from others, and analyzing Descartes’s self-presentation in Discourse VI shows the importance of isolation to beneficence. This suggests that Descartes does not view relationships as key to human flourishing, and moreover that scholars who describe generosity in terms of love and friendship should note that these concepts are austere and depersonalized in the case of the generous individual.

131. Clinical
                    Ethics
                    in Name Only? The Case for Moral Realism at the Bedside
Winter, S. & Wagner, N. (2026). Clinical
                    Ethics
                    in Name Only? The Case for Moral Realism at the Bedside. The Journal of Medicine and Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag034
Abstract (original): Abstract As Clinical Ethics Consultation (CEC) becomes more institutionalized, concerns about its legitimacy and efficacy have grown. We defend a realist orientation, according to which CEC has normative force only if its recommendations answer to stance-independent reasons, not to individual preferences and institutional policies. Public justification can and should proceed through familiar means (e.g., shared mid-level norms and transparent procedures) so that stakeholders can scrutinize the reasoning. We show how neutrality, admirable as a procedural virtue, hollows CEC when treated as a master ideal, leaving hard cases to defaults and institutional inertia. Our comparative thesis holds that shared norms successfully handle routine cases, but persistent disagreement and institutional pressure call for a realist grounding that secures determinate, action-guiding recommendations. On such realist understanding, CEC remains normatively accountable and oriented toward getting things right.

132. Nothing is impossible, actually
Clercq, R.D. & Miller, T. (2026). Nothing is impossible, actually. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag069
Abstract (original): Abstract We provide an argument for the thesis that there could not have been nothing, given that there is something. Only one non-logical assumption goes into our argument.
