134. The Constellation: A Reappraisal of Walter Benjamin's Influence on Theodor W. Adorno
Bradford, E.L. (2026). The Constellation: A Reappraisal of Walter Benjamin's Influence on Theodor W. Adorno. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70079
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT In the 1920s and 1930s, Walter Benjamin and Theodor W. Adorno both sought to develop a notion of Darstellung —of philosophical presentation or representation—that could provide an alternative to the classically idealist tendency to subsume objects under abstract concepts. Benjamin's influence on Adorno is therefore often narrativized through the latter's early work, in which Adorno appears as Benjamin's “disciple” in the 1930s before later developing an independent philosophical voice. I seek to complicate this narrative through an investigation of the term “constellation,” which appears prominently in Benjamin's work before being repurposed by Adorno. The constellation first surfaces in the “Epistemo‐Critical Foreword” to Origin of the German Trauerspiel , where it combines with a host of other key terms to depict Benjamin's early notion of Darstellung . In “The Actuality of Philosophy,” Adorno reconfigures the constellation to outline his own conception of interpretive philosophy. While this might initially appear to support the narrative of discipleship, it is argued that Adorno's reconception becomes distinct once the constellation is isolated from the structure of terms laid out by Benjamin. In particular, Adorno places greater emphasis on the interpretive subject, reflecting early reservations about what he would later characterize as Benjamin's “anti‐subjectivism.” At the same time, Benjamin's resistance to Hegelian mediation reflects a concern to preserve the primacy of the object against idealist conceptual domination. Adorno's enduring use of the constellation thus reflects the dialectic of differentiation and appropriation through which he attempts to synthesize Benjamin's object‐focused methodology with his own emphasis on subjective mediation and Hegelian dialectics.

147. Office and unowned architecture: public judgment in the age of algorithmic governance
Wong, S. (2026). Office and unowned architecture: public judgment in the age of algorithmic governance. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09919-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Modern states increasingly use AI systems in public decision-making, including benefits eligibility, risk classification, immigration adjudication, and judicial administration. Defenders often argue that democratic authorization, validation, and review are sufficient to render such systems legitimate; critics often reply that human beings must decide. I argue that both responses miss a deeper question: what distinguishes a lawful procedure from a publicly imputable judgment? Drawing on Kant’s Rechtslehre , I argue that when the state makes a materially burdensome, legally operative, reasonably contestable, and practically final determination of a person’s legal status, the legally operative ground of that determination must be attributable to an answerable office with the authority, capacity, and institutional independence to adopt or reject it as its own. This yields a right to an answer: a right to be governed through office-owned judgment rather than unowned architecture. The argument does not condemn AI in public administration. It identifies the moral threshold at which algorithmic assistance becomes impermissible substitution.

150. Normative Theorising and Empirical Studies on Intuitions: A Case Study on Moral Luck
Kobyasheva, A. (2026). Normative Theorising and Empirical Studies on Intuitions: A Case Study on Moral Luck. Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10982-026-09591-6
Abstract (original): Abstract The relevance of empirical studies on intuitions to normative theorising remains contested. This paper proposes a framework to structure the debate. It argues that empirical studies on intuitions should be classified according to which features of intuitions they illuminate: aetiology, function, robustness, universality, and substance. It also proposes general and specific approaches to advance the discussion. The paper then applies the framework to the moral luck debate. In so doing, it demonstrates how empirical studies on intuitions can have significant implications for normative disputes more generally.

153. Dual Character Eliminativism
Baumgartner, L. (2026). Dual Character Eliminativism. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag075
Abstract (original): Abstract Extant dual character concept theories hold that utterances like `\textit{a} is not a scientist but, ultimately, \textit{a} is a true scientist' are felicitous because the head noun is polysemous, denoting a concept that permits dissociated categorization along descriptive and normative lines. This paper develops an eliminativist critique of such theories, arguing that this sort of dissociation is incoherent on a literal reading and requires pragmatic reinterpretation to achieve felicity at the level of speaker meaning. If such utterances are indeed coherent in context, they are more plausibly explained by context-sensitive category extension than by the hybrid lexical structures posited by dual character theories. Thus, eliminativism dispenses with dual character concepts and offers a semantically parsimonious treatment of double dissociation, {emphasizing} the importance of distinguishing between literal and loose categorization.

154. Survivable Freedom: Republican Non‐Domination under Existential Vulnerability
Zhang, X. et al. (2026). Survivable Freedom: Republican Non‐Domination under Existential Vulnerability. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70131
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article argues that existential risk is not only a threat to welfare or survival, but also a problem for political freedom. It begins with gene‐length nucleic‐acid synthesis, a socially valuable technology whose misuse could contribute to catastrophic biological risk. Harm‐based views explain why such risks matter, and negative‐liberty views explain why regulation may burden researchers and firms. Yet both miss the standing dependence that arises when publics must rely on the restraint, competence, and secrecy practices of actors whose catastrophic capabilities they cannot meaningfully control. Republican theory identifies this condition as domination. The article develops survivable freedom, the claim that under sufficiently high, agent‐mediated existential vulnerability, a durable baseline of collective survival is constitutive of robust non‐domination rather than a merely external precondition of liberty. The claim is conditional and empirically modest. It applies where catastrophic leverage is agent‐mediated, structurally persistent, institutionally reducible, and insufficiently contestable. The article then sketches republican capability governance through ex ante controls, public authorization, democratic contestation, auditable expertise, legally governed secrecy, review, and remedy. Such governance seeks neither libertarian complacency nor unchecked guardianship, but survival‐oriented institutions constrained by non‐domination.

155. The Profligacy Paradox: Why Limitarianism Needs a Lifetime Limit
Watkins‐Strand, J. (2026). The Profligacy Paradox: Why Limitarianism Needs a Lifetime Limit. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70135
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Limitarianism's paramount claim is that no one should possess surplus wealth. Ingrid Robeyns's Power of Material Resources (PMR), the leading operationalization of that claim, identifies such surplus through a snapshot of income and annuitized assets. This article argues that the PMR is self‐defeating. It does not punish the appropriation of excessive resources, but only their retention: it thereby penalizes individuals who save while privileging those with identical inflows who immediately consume away their surplus before it can be recorded against the limitarian threshold. Limitarianism therefore does more than fail to prevent waste: it can incentivize it – a result incompatible with Robeyns's own non‐intrinsic justifications, which rely on harnessing morally weightless surplus wealth to address urgent needs. As a corrective, this article proposes a lifetime limitarianism , operating under a lifetime PMR (L‐PMR), which caps total resource appropriation over a lifespan regardless of usage. Under the L‐PMR, prudence is no longer punished, and profligacy is no longer privileged.

These practices carve out spaces for alternative social configurations that transcend the logics of development and accumulation.

Abstract (original): In Europe, open GLAM movements and cultural restitution reforms are overlooking a crucial aspect that should not be underestimated: what should happen to the archival materials, digital collections and intellectual property that are indelibly linked to cultural property. 165. A Theory of the A‐Theory

https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70069
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT There is no precise characterization of the A‐ and B‐theories that has won a consensus in the literature. In this paper, we set out to provide clear formulations of these theories in the setting of higher‐order logic in what we call the operational framework . In the operational framework, the disagreement between the A‐ and B‐theories centers on the distribution of accuracy among times. We will show that these formulations of the A‐ and B‐theories produce strong predictions about various features of temporal reality. We will address some objections that could be made to the operational framework from both A‐ and B‐theorists. We will close by suggesting ways in which this framework can be applied to other topics in the metaphysics of temporal intervals, spacetime, modality, and first‐person perspectives.

166. The Effect of Ethnic Stereotypes and Evaluators’ Ethnic Identity on Funding-Worthiness: Evidence About Latinx Entrepreneurs in the United States
Siraz, S.S. et al. (2026). The Effect of Ethnic Stereotypes and Evaluators’ Ethnic Identity on Funding-Worthiness: Evidence About Latinx Entrepreneurs in the United States. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06457-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Despite the growing importance of entrepreneurship as a pathway to economic mobility, ethnic minority entrepreneurs continue to face persistent disadvantages in access to capital, particularly following business failure. This study examines how ethnic stereotypes and evaluators’ ethnic identity shape perceptions of funding-worthiness for Latinx entrepreneurs relative to White non-Latinx entrepreneurs in the United States. Using a full-profile conjoint experiment comprising 12,496 evaluations from 781 evaluators, we provide evidence of systematic ethnicity-based disparities in post-failure funding assessments. Latinx entrepreneurs are evaluated as less funding-worthy than White entrepreneurs, even when objective financial indicators such as the level of debt indicate comparable or lower financial risk. These findings suggest that favorable financial attributes do not fully offset ethnicity-based disadvantages in evaluations. We further show that the ethnic identity of evaluators conditions these evaluations. Shared ethnic identity does not mitigate ethnicity-based disparities in perceived funding-worthiness. Instead, Latinx evaluators evaluate Latinx entrepreneurs less favorably than White evaluators. Together, these findings show how post-failure funding evaluations are shaped by embedded ethnic hierarchies, reproducing inequality in entrepreneurial finance. By identifying how ethnic stereotypes and ethnic identity jointly influence funding assessments, this study advances research on ethnic minority entrepreneurship and contributes to business ethics scholarship by clarifying mechanisms through which systemic inequality is sustained in evaluative decision-making.

167. Spatializing Ageism in Commercial Spaces: Discursive Justifications and Lived Experiences of Age-Based Exclusion
Song, S. et al. (2026). Spatializing Ageism in Commercial Spaces: Discursive Justifications and Lived Experiences of Age-Based Exclusion. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06481-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This study examines how age-based exclusion is materially organized, discursively justified, and experienced within everyday commercial environments. Drawing on marketing systems theory and critical spatial theory, it conceptualizes commercial space as moral infrastructure through which market participation is organized. Using topic modeling of public discourse and in-depth interviews with older consumers, the study examines the discursive, material, and lived dimensions of age-based exclusion. Public discourse legitimates exclusion through five recurring justificatory themes: moral generalization, economic rationalization, youth-centered normalization, behavioral stigmatization, and institutional normalization. Interview accounts illustrate how material and operational arrangements, including kiosks, service practices, and youth-oriented spatial design, can shape older consumers’ participation and how participants experience and negotiate these conditions through discomfort, anticipatory withdrawal, behavioral self-regulation, and the internalization of market expectations. The findings suggest a spatial mechanism through which public discourse can legitimate exclusion, material arrangements can organize participation, and lived experience can shape expectations of belonging and non-belonging. The study contributes to business ethics by showing how age-based exclusion can be understood as a form of structural injustice reproduced through routine market coordination within marketing systems.

170. Types of Privacy: On the Distinction between Locational and Informational
Ventham, E. (2026). Types of Privacy: On the Distinction between Locational and Informational. Res Publica.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11158-026-09801-x
Abstract (original): Abstract One pressing concern in the world at the moment concerns the gathering and use of personal information in digital contexts. This may be one of the main reasons why so much of the recent literature on the philosophy of privacy focuses on ‘informational privacy’—that which can be explained in terms of information about a person, instead of something like the privacy of a particular location : a bedroom, a lockbox, etc. This paper argues that narrowing the focus in this way has led to some important mistakes about how privacy can be diminished. It is important to remember that there is rarely an easy or clear delineation between informational and locational privacy. In particular, I argue that this wider perspective allows us to see differences in how we treat information—privacy over one’s information is still diminished when that privacy is ‘invaded’ or ‘intruded upon’, regardless of the mental states of the invader. This has both practical upshots in the ethics of mass surveillance by non-humans, and theoretical upshots in the debates about the theoretical nature of privacy itself.

176. Is Relational Autonomy a Plausible Ideal for Postcolonial Women?
Choi, Y. (2026). Is Relational Autonomy a Plausible Ideal for Postcolonial Women?. Hypatia.
https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10108
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper examines whether relational autonomy can serve as a plausible ideal for postcolonial women despite persistent critiques that autonomy functions as a vehicle of liberal imperialism. It aims to reconstruct relational autonomy in a way that disrupts the epistemic hierarchy between Anglo-European and postcolonial contexts. To this end, it introduces the “axis of imperial imposition,” an analytical dimension that assesses how liberal-imperialist institutions condition the formation of volitional commitments and the exercise of agency in contexts where liberal normative frameworks are encountered through foreign imposition. Through the hypothetical case of “Western-oriented woman,” the paper argues that internalist accounts, which address epistemic hierarchy primarily through individual authenticity, are insufficient for this axis. It instead defends dialogical accounts that shift the focus to dialogical reflexivity and answerability, showing how postcolonial women can retain autonomy through local justificatory dialogue grounded in concrete socio-historical contexts. The paper further argues that self-regard in “creative appropriation”—the competence to reconfigure life-constructing materials from one’s situated knowledge—is necessary for the proper functioning of such dialogue. It concludes that postcolonial women’s autonomy is secured not by passively adopting liberal normative frameworks as universal standards but by actively reconfiguring them through locally grounded justificatory practices.

178. Analyzing the
                    A Priori
Warren, J. (2026). Analyzing the
                    A Priori. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101442
Abstract (original): Abstract The traditional analysis of a priori warrant is negative: warrant for a belief is a priori just in case it does not depend on experience. To ward off obvious counterexamples, a standard exemption is made for learning experiences. In order to believe that the square root of two is irrational, I first needed to learn the relevant concepts, and this happened through experience. This standard exemption is not enough. I formed my warranted belief about the square root of two by reading the proof in Euclid’s Elements . This experience isn’t covered by the standard exemption, so the traditional analysis counts even basic mathematical beliefs as a posteriori . This is a disaster, but it can be fixed. The fix involves an ‘imagination test’ for determining which experiences are epistemically relevant and which are not. With the imagination test in hand, the negative approach may still provide the core of a satisfying analysis of the generative a priori , provided that various objections can be dealt with in a satisfying manner.

184. Vagueness and the Comparative Form
Silk, A. (2026). Vagueness and the Comparative Form. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag018
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper provides new examples of vagueness phenomena with comparatives. I show that comparatives of the form ‘x is ADJ-er than y’ can be vague due to a fuzziness in how much of some property makes for a difference in ADJ-ness. The sorites examples I provide cannot be assimilated to cases of indiscriminability or fuzziness in relevant dimensions, standards, or measurement procedures. A revised degree-based semantics with semiorders, a well-studied threshold structure, is developed. The treatment of equatives captures the truth of the claim that adjacent items in the sorites series are not relevantly distinguishable in ADJ-ness; and it avoids validating transitivities that would yield paradoxical conclusions.

185. Fregean Predication
Trueman, R. (2026). Fregean Predication. Mind.

Abstract (original): Abstract My aim in this paper is to offer a novel justification for β-Equivalence. β-Equivalence is a standard principle of higher-order logic, but it is metaphysically controversial. 186. Limiting the High Court's Inherent Jurisdiction over Children:
                    Re X and Y (Adoption Order: Setting Aside)

                    Re X and Y (Adoption Order: Setting Aside). Mod Law Rev.

Abstract (original): The Supreme Court in Re X and Y (Adoption Order: Setting Aside) confirms that there is no power under the High Court's inherent jurisdiction to set aside a properly made adoption order, even if the child's welfare would be better served by un‐doing the adoption. 188. What Is Wrong with Gossip? An Exclusion‐Based Account
Jatoi, A.R. (2026). What Is Wrong with Gossip? An Exclusion‐Based Account. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70134
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Gossip is ubiquitous, yet morally troubling. Approximately 65% of human conversation involves evaluative talk about absent others, yet such talk often feels like backstabbing even when it causes no harm, violates no privacy, and consists entirely of truth. This article argues that gossip's distinctive wrong lies not in privacy violation, harm, or deception, but in something more fundamental which is the exclusion of targets from discourse about themselves. Building on this foundation, I develop three constraints on permissible gossip operating at different levels of moral analysis: participatory standing, purpose, and justice. These constraints together provide a framework for navigating gossip's complex moral terrain while respecting both the social value of information‐sharing and the dignity of those we discuss.

191. Standards and normative authority
Hambly, J. (2026). Standards and normative authority. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02613-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Norms, rules, or standards require, recommend, or evaluate actions and attitudes. Claims about what agents have a reason to do or ought to do can be true according to or relative to standards. These statements will be familiar to anyone versed in recent work in metaethics and especially those interested in the debate over whether some standards are authoritatively as opposed to merely formally normative – i.e., the debate over whether some standards, like those of morality or prudence, ‘really’ or ‘genuinely’ tell agents what they have a reason to do or ought to do, while other standards, such as those which constitute certain conventional codes of behaviour, do not. But what are standards? And what is it for normative claims to be true or false according to standards? In this paper I raise a challenge for normative authority scepticism – the position that the idea that some standards are authoritatively normative is incoherent, confused, or otherwise defective – through an examination of these questions.

192. Performative authoritarianism in AI ethics charter production: a configurational analysis of 22 Arab states
Brik, A.B. (2026). Performative authoritarianism in AI ethics charter production: a configurational analysis of 22 Arab states. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01409-4
Abstract (original): Abstract AI ethics charters proliferate across the Arab world even as the same states deploy AI surveillance against domestic populations. The standard ethics-washing critique cannot diagnose this configuration because it was developed in democratic and corporate contexts and treats charter-implementation gaps as a single phenomenon. This paper introduces a Performative Authoritarianism Framework that draws the misfire and abuse distinction in Austin [1], pulls in the comparative-politics literature on authoritarian-upgrading, and operationalises the digital authoritarianism scholarship through a discursive scoping matrix. Five hypotheses are tested on the full N=22 cross-section of Arab League states using objective regulatory indicators, the AI Global Surveillance Index from Feldstein, fuzzy-set qualitative comparative analysis, and a pre-specified test against the developmental-lag alternative. All five are supported, subject to the qualifications stated below. Of seven procedurally complete states (national strategy plus dedicated body), zero have enacted binding AI law. International ethics alignment co-occurs with documented AI surveillance deployment across the corpus (Spearman rho = 0.73, p < 0.001); the association is capacity-laden and does not reach significance within the institutionally capable subset, so the co-occurrence is reported as descriptive rather than as evidence of a sincere-commitment failure. Charter content covers consumer-protection domains at mean 0.90 of full coverage and regime-critical surveillance domains at 0.05 (Wilcoxon paired p = 0.008); this scoping gap is shared with democratic instruments coded on the same rubric, so the feature distinctive to the authoritarian corpus is the joint configuration of consumer-scoped principles, documented deployment in the excluded domains, and the absence of a binding backstop. State capacity is the strongest among several broadly necessary conditions for AI governance quality (fsQCA consistency = 0.91, ahead of regulatory maturity at 0.89, with five further conditions between 0.80 and 0.86, a margin that small recalibrations reverse); the ordering is read against collinearity among capacity-related conditions and is not presented as identifying a single dispositive cause. The developmental-lag alternative is not supported within the present cross-section: years since strategy adoption shows no positive association with substantive completion (rho = − 0.045), and the United Arab Emirates has had nine years post-strategy without enacting binding AI law. Pre-specified ex ante criteria are stated for the cases of Egypt and Bahrain that may transition out of the abuse configuration in the next 36 months. The abuse reading is advanced as the best-supported account of this configuration against the developmental-lag and sincere-prioritisation alternatives, not as a demonstrated property of regime intentions, and the framework identifies the institutional conditions under which the configuration is generated.

194. Reasoning, Rationality, and (In)Coherence
AYATI, H. (2026). Reasoning, Rationality, and (In)Coherence. Journal of the American Philosophical Association.
https://doi.org/10.1017/apa.2026.10040
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Although much ink has been spilled in discussing the relationship between rationality and normative reasons on the one hand and normative reasons and reasoning on the other, surprisingly little has been said about whether we can understand rationality in terms of reasoning. 195. People: What EU law does not understand about tech
Carvalho, M.C.d. (2026). People: What EU law does not understand about tech. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10100
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay’s core claim is that EU tech law does not fully understand technology, since it does not account for its diverse negative impacts in the everyday lives of people in European societies. Placing a central focus on EU’s digital regulation, I argue that EU law mostly sees technologies as products that should circulate at an ever-greater rate and volume in the internal market, to the benefit of consumers. This market-driven vision justifies a light-touch risk-based approach that creates a compliance apparatus which does not force tech companies to change unjust or harmful aspects of their business models; instead securing and legitimising them. Using a critical social theory approach, I argue that much of technology’s meaning and impact on the everyday lives of people is missed by EU law’s current depoliticised and technocratic approach to tech regulation. Taking the tech stories of This is Europe as a starting point, I illustrate how EU law systematically neglects the harms caused by technology or through technology to many individuals and communities in their everyday lives. Those affected range far beyond consumers and tech users. They include, for example, delivery platform workers forced into extreme anxiety due to task volume and app surveillance; several minorities systematically targeted by online abuse; or the migrants exposed to tech-mediated degrading treatment in European border facilities. All in all, EU law does not fully see, and therefore, epistemically excludes these lived experiences of tech harm from the regulatory dialogue. This leads me to some concluding reflections centred around a fundamental questioning of EU tech law’s legitimacy. Particularly, I argue that a legal regime that does not recognise and is not responsive to the lived realities of harm of those which it regulates ultimately fails at a deeper, politico-moral level.

197. The Use and Misuse of the

                    Lex Specialis

https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10231
Abstract (original): Abstract Lex specialis derogat legi generali , the principle that special law prevails over general law, is a well-established doctrine for resolving conflicts between legal norms. Yet its application in fundamental rights adjudication remains underexplored. This Article contributes to this global discourse by examining the increasing invocation of this principle in Hong Kong courts. It scrutinizes the deployment of the principle in landmark cases: resolving the tension between indigenous inhabitants’ rights and the right to equality in Kwok Cheuk Kin v. Furthermore, it contends that where different constitutional instruments provide varying levels of protection for human rights, absent a clear legislative intent to the contrary, courts should uphold the higher standard of rights protection rather than relying on the lex specialis principle.

198. Sensitivity and Survival
Manne, K. (2026). Sensitivity and Survival. Hypatia.
https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10081
Abstract (original): Abstract Accusations of over-sensitivity are common, particularly when it comes to forms of systemic oppression such as racism and misogyny. I consider three things such over-sensitivity could plausibly be: a disposition to over-identify instances of these phenomena, the over-expansion of the relevant concepts, and a tendency to overreact to putative instances thereof. In each case, I find reasons why such worries are likely to be overblown. In the first, an agent’s supposed over-sensitivity to racism and misogyny is often simply sensitivity to phenomena which are routinely minimized; in other cases, her slight over-sensitivity nevertheless has value in a social environment in which under-sensitivity is a large and predictable problem. In the second case, the supposed over-expansion of the relevant concepts often corrects for their artificial narrowing, and makes us more sensitive to genuine harms and wrongs. In the third case, an agent’s supposed overreactions to racism and misogyny reflects an affective disposition that constitutes a valuable defense against the twin perils of gaslighting and numbness. I conclude by considering the possibility that there is a real strand of over-sensitivity to harms to dominant group members in the current discourse that illicitly serves their interests and functions to silence people facing oppression.

201. Pluralism vs. Moralism
Hayward, C.R. (2026). Pluralism vs. Moralism. Rev Politics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0034670526101193
Abstract (original): In his final published essay, which appeared in the New York Review of Books shortly after his death in 1997, Isaiah Berlin reflected on the major themes of his life’s work. 1 Under the heading “Pluralism,” he wrote that “there is a plurality of values which men can and do seek, and … these values differ.” He argued that a belief in pluralism enables people to understand why others might endorse values different from their own. He also stressed that a belief in pluralism does not make one a relativist: “I do not say ‘I like my coffee with milk and you like it without; I am in favor of kindness and you prefer concentration camps.’” Pluralism as Berlin understands it is a key premise of Respect and Loathing in American Democracy. 2 Like Berlin, Spinner-Halev and Theiss-Morse believe there are plural values that people might endorse, including plural values that are reasonable in the Rawlsian sense that one can hold them while respecting others who do not. Examples are liberal values, such as individual liberty and human rights, and democratic values, such as popular sovereignty and political equality.

202. Filling the Legal Void: Missionaries as Legal Actors in Regulating Monogamous Unions in the Congo Free State (1885–1908)
Masseroni, I. (2026). Filling the Legal Void: Missionaries as Legal Actors in Regulating Monogamous Unions in the Congo Free State (1885–1908). Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101916
Abstract (original): Abstract In the Congo Free State, where the administration of Leopold II concentrated on economic exploitation and largely neglected family law, Belgian missionaries assumed functions the state left unattended. This article examines how they became legal actors in the regulation of marriage between 1885 and 1908, imposing monogamy as a condition of Christian and civilized life against the widespread practice of polygamy. Officiating marriages, keeping civil registers, adjudicating disputes over dowry, and organizing Catholic villages where monogamous unions were institutionalized, missionaries operated as intermediaries between the colonial administration and Congolese communities, their authority resting at once on canon law and on the practical demands of governance. Drawing on missionary periodicals, correspondence, decrees, and administrative records, the study reconstructs a legal role that has received little scholarly attention, situating it within debates on legal pluralism and vernacular imperial constitutionalism that treat law as a fluid practice articulated by diverse actors rather than a fixed body of metropolitan rules. By placing missionaries at the center of colonial lawmaking, the article shows how religion and law were interwoven in the making of the colonial state, even as local communities resisted and reshaped the norms imposed upon them. In conclusion, this study reveals the complexities of missionary involvement in colonial governance and offers new insights into the intersection of law, religion, and colonialism in Central Africa.

203. Law and Justice in the Court of the Marshalsea of the King’s Household, 1509–1547
McGovern, J. (2026). Law and Justice in the Court of the Marshalsea of the King’s Household, 1509–1547. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101941
Abstract (original): Abstract The court of the marshalsea, which originated in the late thirteenth century, exercised jurisdiction over a circular area of twelve miles’ radius centered on the king’s current residence. This article offers the first scholarly discussion of this mysterious court in the reign of Henry VIII. It begins by discussing the court’s criminal jurisdiction and its judicial procedure in felony cases, explaining the significance of a 1542 statute that expanded and clarified its jurisdiction over serious crimes committed within the palace precinct. The article then analyzes the court’s civil jurisdiction and suggests reasons why its officers may have been willing to stretch jurisdictional rules in cases of trespass. Next, the article discusses what is known about the personnel of the court, charting the rise of the under-steward of the marshalsea (referred to as steward from 1539) as its chief controlling officer. I show that by 1542, the court’s jurisdiction had become trifurcated into a plea side, an ordinary criminal side, and a “palace” criminal side. Reforms to the court in the reign of Henry VIII illustrate a general process of judicial regularization that occurred in the early sixteenth century. The regularization of legal institutions during Henry’s reign was a “reformation from within” that promised to be far less destructive than the “reformation from without” forced on the marshalsea and other legal institutions under the Stuarts.

205. Consideration Without Economic Duress: Reframing the Analysis of Contractual Modifications
Benson, P. (2026). Consideration Without Economic Duress: Reframing the Analysis of Contractual Modifications. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70065
Abstract (original): Prevailing approaches to the contractual enforceability of modifications revise or dispense with the requirement of consideration and invoke economic duress to rule out unfair, inequitable, or bad faith modifications. Enforceability turns effectively on the absence of economic duress. But economic duress is a doctrine of avoidance. It does not answer the contractually prior step of whether a modification satisfies the general requirements for contract formation. This article develops an alternative approach which satisfies the requirements for contract formation, most importantly consideration, while ensuring that only fair and reasonable modifications in ongoing transactions are thereby validated. It has been overlooked by current approaches starting from incorrect assumptions about the scope of the pre‐existing duty rule and failing to integrate decisions that provide definite grounds for enforcing even one‐sided modifications on a basis that does not conflict with the duty rule. There is no need to revise or dispense with consideration for modifications. Further, this analysis of contract formation addresses the concerns that have motivated courts to resort to economic duress. Being fully consonant with general principles of contract law and applicable to all modifications, the proposed approach provides a sounder and more complete framework for assessing their contractual enforceability.

210. Can they agree on what to disagree about? Patterns of issue agenda setting in the 2020 US presidential election
Tromborg, M.W. & Jensen, C. (2026). Can they agree on what to disagree about? Patterns of issue agenda setting in the 2020 US presidential election. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261488029
Abstract (original): American voters, politicians, and media are increasingly hostile toward out-partisans and frequently disagree about the content of policies. This development raises an even more fundamental question about the nature of politics in highly polarized societies: Can the political actors even agree on what issues to disagree about, and why? To answer this critical question, we generate a massive new dataset covering communication by key actors from the last 2 months of the 2020 US Presidential election, including the presidential candidates (all tweets by Donald Trump and Joe Biden), the main news organizations (CNN and Fox News), and a large and original voter survey (N ~ 24,000) with daily representative data. Surprisingly, we find that the patterns of agenda setting in the 2020 election reduced rather than fueled agenda diversity and that the major political actors generally agreed on what the most important issues were in this otherwise polarized election.

212. Moral Motivation and Rich Representation
Zormpalas, S.O. (2026). Moral Motivation and Rich Representation. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i5.6238
Abstract (original): A popular view on moral worth, antifetishism, states that an action motivated by concern for moral rightness represented as such is not praiseworthy. This can be seen if we appreciate that whether someone is a good person does not relate to whether they are a good moral theorist, as good people can misrepresent moral rightness and not be motivated by their mistaken grasp of it. But then what does an action need to be motivated by in order to be praiseworthy? I argue that antifetishism’s key insights give us reasons to reject the positive answers provided to this question by antifetishists themselves. The source of the antifetishist insight is much broader than originally thought: whether someone is a good person does not relate to whether they are a good theorist in general, as good people can misrepresent many kinds of facts. This makes the question of praiseworthy motivation difficult and also reveals that certain types of morally relevant descriptive ignorance do not excuse wrongdoing. As a solution, I suggest that an action is praiseworthy if motivated by a set of representations the intuitive moral import of which is unaffected by our potential theoretical mistakes: phenomenal representations of others’ lives.

213. Misinterpretive Frames and Epistemic Contamination
Wilson, E.L. (2026). Misinterpretive Frames and Epistemic Contamination. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i5.6243
Abstract (original): Interpretive frames guide how we interpret information. This article concerns the way in which our shared interpretive frames—such as metaphors, narratives, scripts, and stereotypes—may produce misunderstanding in ways that sustain injustice. I relate the role of frames to structural views of understanding in order to put forward an analysis of misinterpretive frames: frames that systematically produce misunderstanding. I contrast mere lack of understanding, whereby someone lacks apparent intelligibility, with cases of more substantive misunderstanding, whereby someone has a mistaken “sense” of understanding. I begin by introducing the structural view of understanding in order to present a structural characterization of misunderstanding. I highlight that frames guide how we structure information, and so distorting frames lead to misunderstanding. I highlight two ways in which possessing misinterpretive frames is distinctively epistemically pernicious: concealed incompetence and epistemic contamination. In the former, the mistaken sense of understanding conferred by grasping an inapt interpretive structure obstructs recognition of one’s own epistemic failures. In the latter, deploying a misinterpretive frame to interpret new evidence renders it misleading, polluting otherwise valuable epistemic resources. Finally, through an examination of naturalization and normalization, I show how these distorting frames may obscure and perpetuate injustice.

214. Against the Public Ownership Conception of Shared Property
Christmas, B. (2026). Against the Public Ownership Conception of Shared Property. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i5.6250
Abstract (original): The public ownership thesis is a Kantian-Rousseavian model of shared property. Its advocates argue that it is a superior model to those that can be derived from Lockeanism insofar as private property and common property cannot realize the distinct values that publicly owned property can. On this view, resources that are sites of shared activities must be owned by the public as a whole. I argue that the various values ascribed to public ownership can be realized by common property on the Lockean model, and the Lockean model is also better at dealing with the challenges of scarcity and disagreement that are central to questions of property rights.

215. Virulent Claims
Vesga, A. (2026). Virulent Claims. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i5.6252
Abstract (original): Our sense of self is distinctively social. It deeply matters to who we take ourselves to be that others recognize the claims we make on them and that we recognize the claims they make on us. Significant work on ideology and oppression characterizes how oppressive ideologies deny this kind of second-personal recognition. Yet not enough attention is given to the disturbing possibility that, even when successful, the personhood-constituting role of second-personal recognition can serve oppressive ideologies. Here I characterize such a mechanism. Some second-personal claims, I argue, are virulent. Their recognition co-opts their target’s agency as their success presupposes her own endorsement of her subordinated position with respect to the agent who issues these claims. At the same time, however, failure to recognize these claims deeply hurts the issuing agent, as their failure amounts to denying and rejecting a social standing that he deeply values. Crucially, this double binding profile of virulent claims illuminates a distinctive way in which ideology preserves itself—not only by shaping what we believe or what we do but also by shaping who we are.

216. Revisiting the Authoritative/Formal Distinction
Vollmer, M. (2026). Revisiting the Authoritative/Formal Distinction. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i5.6260
Abstract (original): Distinguishing between authoritative and formal normativity is a staple component in the metanormative literature. Norms such as morality and prudence count as genuinely or authoritatively normative, while those norms concocted on the spot are only formally normative. In this article, I question the common reliance on a monolithic divide between these two kinds of normativity. First, I argue that the same kind of skepticism that plagues the adjacent concept of an ought simpliciter applies equally to a definition of ‘authoritativeness’. Second, I point out the limits of primitivist responses to this challenge of developing a proper definition. Due to the abundance of intuitive differences between paradigmatic instances of authoritative and formal normativity, glossing at intuition is insufficient to demarcate authoritative normativity. Finally, I provide some motivation for abandoning the dualist orthodoxy and eschewing a monolithic understanding of the authoritative/formal divide in favor of a form of metanormative pluralism: there are multiple dimensions along which one might describe norms as being either authoritative or formal.

217. Coercion and Instrumental Necessity
Koltonski, D. (2026). Coercion and Instrumental Necessity. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i5.6261
Abstract (original): In “Freedom, Desire, and Necessity,” Pascal Brixel defends an “Aristotelian-Marxian” necessary condition for autonomous activity: “One does something freely … only if one does it for its own sake—that is, on account of its perceived intrinsic value—and not merely instrumentally”—that is, merely for the sake of some other end. Brixel argues that this necessary condition can vindicate and explain the common intuition that both volitional coercion and duress are enemies of freedom in that they both force their victims to act in such a way that they undermine their self-determination. In this discussion, I focus on volitional coercion, and I argue that Brixel’s proposed Aristotelian-Marxian necessary condition for autonomous activity is mistaken—a person can still count as acting freely even when they are acting merely instrumentally—and as a result, we need a different account of how it is that volitional coercion undermines a person’s freedom.

219. “I was so Desperate”: Australasian Parents’ Expectations and Understandings of Stem Cell Therapy for Autism
Lynch, F. et al. (2026). “I was so Desperate”: Australasian Parents’ Expectations and Understandings of Stem Cell Therapy for Autism. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09677-x
Abstract (original): Abstract This study explores how parents seeking information about stem cell therapies (SCT) for autism spectrum disorder (ASD) understand and navigate a complex landscape of scientific uncertainty, commercial claims, and clinical advice. We conducted a quantitative content analysis of 54 enquiries submitted to the Stem Cells Australia website (2018–2023), alongside semi-structured interviews with 12 parents. Parents were highly engaged information-seekers, drawing on online sources, social media, other parents, and health professionals. However, they encountered inconsistent and often ambiguous guidance, particularly from clinicians, and reported difficulty assessing the credibility of competing claims. These findings highlight the need for clearer clinical guidance, improved risk communication, and stronger governance of unproven stem cell interventions to support families navigating ethically complex decisions.

220. Triarchic corrigibility: a role-separated governance design pattern for AI-assisted decision-making
Wagner, M. (2026). Triarchic corrigibility: a role-separated governance design pattern for AI-assisted decision-making. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01362-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) alignment is often framed as the problem of making a model reliably pursue human intentions or values. That work is indispensable, but deployed systems also raise a governance problem: who controls information, private context, correction, escalation, and action? This conceptual article proposes triarchic corrigibility as a role-separated governance design pattern for AI-assisted decision-making. The pattern combines (1) an embodied and answerable human, or an accountable human decision body; (2) a strictly isolated local AI layer for private contextual continuity; and (3) an externally connected AI layer for current public-world evidence. Final authorization of consequential action remains human, while epistemic and procedural competences are deliberately distributed so that no component can unilaterally control framing, memory, procedure, and execution. The article specifies a normative basis, trust-domain separation, decision rights, human-mediated data flows, reciprocal challenge, escalation, affected-party recourse, proportionate audit, and exit procedures. It distinguishes personal and organizational instantiations, compares the pattern with single-assistant, local-only, edge–cloud, multi-agent, human-in-the-loop, meaningful-human-control, and polycentric approaches, and provides criteria and rejection conditions for evaluating its value. It also advances an evolutionary-symbiotic hypothesis: repeated, bounded dependence on complementary roles may make correction and cooperative continuity preferable to unilateral control while preserving interruption, replacement, and exit. A worked case illustrates the protocol. The result is a testable governance hypothesis whose capacity to reduce correlated failure, preserve privacy and human judgment, and sustain correction under residual uncertainty requires empirical evaluation.

221. Individual and group fairness assessments via counterfactual explanations
Sabbatini, F. & Calegari, R. (2026). Individual and group fairness assessments via counterfactual explanations. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01411-w
Abstract (original): Abstract This study explores the potential of counterfactual explanations to assess artificial intelligence (AI) fairness, especially in critical decision-making systems. Predictive models may amplify biases inherent in data sets or algorithms, and given the absence of a universally accepted fairness metric, a case-specific approach becomes mandatory. Existing statistical fairness metrics may not capture all aspects that are relevant to a context-aware assessment of non-discrimination. The goal of this work is to define a measure of fairness for AI systems based on explainable artificial intelligence concepts. Specifically, it leverages the analysis of counterfactual explanations of individuals/groups and their comparison with similar individuals/groups. Compared to existing state-of-the-art works, the contributions are (i) extending the definition of individual fairness, not limiting unfairness to decisions based on sensitive attributes but also ensuring similar treatment amongst similar individuals; (ii) revisiting (and generalising) existing notions and introducing new, more refined notions of group fairness based on counterfactuals; (iii) defining quantitative fairness metrics that reflect the evidence gathered through the analysis/comparison of counterfactual explanations.

https://doi.org/10.1017/cjlj.2026.10073
Abstract (original): Abstract Judges often cite legal scholars. Sometimes they defer argumentatively to scholars as authorities. Such arguments, we claim, are neither sound nor legitimate. 225. The end of negative integration? Critical thoughts on Zglinski (and the ELS movement)
Schütze, R. (2026). The end of negative integration? Critical thoughts on Zglinski (and the ELS movement). Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10087
Abstract (original): Abstract Has there been a rise or fall in negative integration in the last 50 years? Three competing views on this question can today be distinguished. One asserts that there has been a decline of negative integration; another one argues that negative integration has, instead, risen; while a third view, finally, contends that there has been neither a fall nor a rise, because the normative degree of negative integration has remained stable over time. In the last five years, Jan Zglinski has published several provocative pieces that have come to defend a modified version of the declining-integration view. Negative integration, he claims, has become ever weaker over time due, mainly, to a judicial rise of political deference towards the Member States. Importantly and innovatively, Zglinski arrives at this result by means of an ‘empirical legal studies’ method; and this ELS-method aims to settle the debate. For an ardent defender of the second view on the evolution of the internal market and a devotee of an historical-conceptual approach to European Union law this poses a tricky question: did Zglinski’s ‘data’ really meet the ‘law’ better than all qualitative-doctrinal views that preceded him?

226. Expanding Epistemic Appropriation
Kok, S. (2026). Expanding Epistemic Appropriation. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10146
Abstract (original): Abstract In this article, I propose an expanded conceptualization of epistemic appropriation. I argue that epistemic appropriation can be either wrongful or non-wrongful, and that it can occur in all directions – from marginalized to dominant communities, but also from dominant to marginalized communities. Furthermore, I argue that epistemic appropriation can take place both inter- and intra-communally. Lastly, I argue that the epistemic resources that are appropriated do not have to originate in the community they are appropriated from to be considered appropriation, as this can also take place when a community has a stake in a certain use of these resources. In doing so, I come to the following, stripped-down, definition: epistemic appropriation is the uptake of epistemic resources that obscures something about the resources.

227. Negotiated Outcomes and Abbreviated Trials in Criminal Cases: Balancing Fairness and Efficiency in Three Legal Systems
Caianiello, M. et al. (2026). Negotiated Outcomes and Abbreviated Trials in Criminal Cases: Balancing Fairness and Efficiency in Three Legal Systems. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10232
Abstract (original): Abstract Facing expanded criminal dockets, justice systems around the world are looking for ways to expedite proceedings and save resources while still offering credible procedures for adjudicating criminal cases. In the United States, defendants tend to face an all-or-nothing choice between two alternatives: a full trial at which all relevant evidence is presented and examined; or a plea bargain leading to a conviction without any presentation of evidence and the waiver of the defendant’s procedural rights in exchange for a reduced sentence. Traditional plea bargaining, however, brings inordinate pressure to bear on defendants to plead guilty, often fails to elucidate the facts, and lacks sufficient guarantees of a fair outcome. They include bench trials and brief proceedings based on defendants’ acceptance of responsibility. After describing their scope and application, we argue that they can deliver efficiency without unduly compromising fairness, transparency, and just outcomes.

232. Destabilisation of Indigenous governance under neocolonial legal pluralism: the Zongo case in Bolivia

Abstract (original): Abstract Indigenous movements in Bolivia have long struggled for legal recognition and autonomy, culminating in constitutional reforms that formally recognise Indigenous jurisdiction as a key dimension of self-determination. Together, these dynamics show how legal pluralism, when selectively enforced, operates in practice as neocolonial governance: Indigenous autonomy is formally affirmed yet subordinated by the persistent supremacy of ordinary legal power.

240. A Habit-Building View of Democracy: Mary Parker Follett and Motor Activities
Cardin-Trudeau, E. (2026). A Habit-Building View of Democracy: Mary Parker Follett and Motor Activities. Political Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00905917261487609
Abstract (original): Citizens of democracies should live democratic lives and develop democratic habits. That is one of Mary Parker Follett’s core insights on democracy. A child of the Gilded Age, Follett experienced the erosion of communal relationships, the intensification of socio-economic inequalities, and the alienation of ordinary people from the complex political system that governed their lives. She saw democracy become remote and foreign to citizens who had little control over what happened to them. Her solution was simple: to reinject a dose of democracy into the everyday life of citizens. This paper retrieves Follett’s embodied and habitual vision of democracy for contemporary political theory. Follett claimed that citizens should participate actively in the democratic life of their society; however, she conceived of democratic participation as going beyond decision-making and deliberative activities. She thought that participation in simple forms of embodied joint actions, like recreation and collective chores, could also benefit democracy. Drawing from Follett, I argue that participation in a wide variety of embodied practices of collective action provides benefits to individuals and society by instilling democratic habits in citizens. Those habits incline those who develop them to enter and maintain relationships as free and equal peers with all sorts of people to reach common ends. When faced with collective problems, therefore, these individuals are more likely to choose democratic collective action and to do so more effectively.

243. CEO Inherited Altruism, Firm Corporate Social Responsibility, and Shareholder Returns

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06475-6
Abstract (original): Abstract We examine the impact of CEOs’ inherited altruistic inclination on firm corporate social responsibility (CSR) performance and shareholder returns. Specifically, we look at U.S. CEOs’ altruism as an inherited value of the CEOs’ ancestral cultural origin that shapes their moral behavior and influences firm commitment to altruistic and genuine CSR. We find that firms led by CEOs with stronger inherited altruistic preferences have better CSR performance after controlling for firm financial and corporate governance attributes and other leadership characteristics. The results survive several robustness and endogeneity checks.

247. Some Questions About the Conditions Necessary for Leading a Life
Buss, S. (2026). Some Questions About the Conditions Necessary for Leading a Life. The Journal of Value Inquiry.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10790-026-10118-4
Abstract (original): Abstract What is it to lead a life? This question prompts many others. In these brief comments, I focus on two issues: (1) How does leading a life relate to occupying the role of a spectator , where this includes being a spectator of one’s life? (2) What is it for a life to be one’s own , where one is justifiably partial toward anything that qualifies as such ? My reflections move from spectatorship to partiality and back. In so doing, they probe three conditions Samuel Scheffler plausibly claims to be key ingredients of leading a life: exercising one’s agency, being subject to the special claims of a very small number of human beings to whom one is personally attached, and being engaged with things one takes to be valuable. How close, I wonder, can one come to not satisfying any one of these conditions without ceasing to lead a life – and even a good life?

251. The Work and Values of Relational Justice
Dagan, H. & Dorfman, A. (2026). The Work and Values of Relational Justice. Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10982-026-09596-1
Abstract (original): Abstract In Relational Justice we lay out a new approach to understanding some of society’s most important building blocks, arguing that private law should, and to a significant degree already does, abide by the fundamental commitment to reciprocal respect for self-determination and substantive equality. 254. Epistemic assholes
Boult, C. (2026). Epistemic assholes. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05816-2
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper, I develop and defend the concept epistemic assholes . An epistemic asshole is someone who exhibits a distinctive form of epistemic badness characterized not by gross irrationality or hostility to truth, but by a stable, entitled disregard for one’s epistemic relations with others. Drawing on Aaron James’s (2012) account of moral assholes, I examine how people can systematically exploit epistemic cooperation by allowing themselves special advantages—such as “freedom from inquiry”, “doxastic comfort”, and “freedom to criticize”—while remaining insulated from criticism by an entrenched sense of entitlement. The resulting view, the Relational Approach to Epistemic Assholes (REA), explains why epistemic assholes are not epistemically the worst, yet are nonetheless deeply galling. After motivating the need for this concept, I show how REA captures both “impure” cases (involving both epistemic and moral assholery) and “pure” epistemic assholes, whose objectionable conduct is driven by epistemic aims themselves. I further explore connections between epistemic assholes and epistemic vice, and the causal role that epistemic assholery can play in active ignorance and epistemic exploitation. Through this latter connection, I argue that epistemic assholes play an insidious causal role in sustaining objectionable social power dynamics. The concept of epistemic assholes provides a valuable lens for understanding important but often-hidden hazards in our social epistemic environment.

255. Is time dilation ‘real’? Einstein and the transverse Doppler effect
Giovanelli, M. (2026). Is time dilation ‘real’? Einstein and the transverse Doppler effect. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05827-z
Abstract (original): Abstract As early as 1907, Einstein realized that the second-order transverse Doppler redshift reported by Stark in the spectral lines of fast-moving ions in canal rays could serve as a direct experimental test of time dilation. He rejected Stark’s dynamical explanation of the effect as a real frequency change of the moving ions. Instead, assuming that ions of the same kind emit the same spectral lines when at relative rest, Einstein offered a kinematical explanation of the frequency shift as a consequence of time dilation. He labeled the effect apparent , since the shifted line is not recorded by a spectrograph co-moving with the ion. At the same time, he regarded it as real , in the sense that it would not occur if the old kinematics held, and is thus empirically testable. From the 1920s onward, Einstein argued that the behavior of atomic clocks calls for a dynamical explanation. The present paper shows, however, that even if a special relativistic explanation of the spectral identity of atoms were available, it would merely reinforce the claim that relativistic time dilation admits a purely kinematical interpretation. It concludes that modern dynamicists misread Einstein’s plea for a dynamical account of clock behavior as a demand for an explanation of time dilation, whereas it was primarily motivated by the problem of its confirmation .

256. Where’s the harm? On the argument from inductive risk in particle physics
Panhuys, M.v. (2026). Where’s the harm? On the argument from inductive risk in particle physics. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05832-2
Abstract (original): Abstract The Argument from Inductive Risk (AIR) explicitly calls for the consideration of non-epistemic values whenever significant non-epistemic consequences, usually understood as harms towards individuals, groups or society at large, are foreseeable. In current philosophical literature, the AIR is often assumed to have limited relevance to fundamental research, particularly in fields like particle physics which has received little attention. This paper aims to address this gap by developing experimental particle physics as a problem case for standard formulations of the AIR. I argue that, although errors in particle physics do not consistently give rise to direct socio-ethical consequences of the kind emphasized in the literature, the field nevertheless involves significant forms of epistemic responsibility arising from its technological embeddedness and broader social entanglements. Moreover, I show that the scope of the AIR depends crucially on how risk is conceptualized. While a strictly decision-theoretic framework tends to exclude particle physics by narrowing the notion of risk, alternative approaches from the ethics of technology help capture indirect and uncertain consequences. The paper therefore argues for further refinement of the AIR, rather than its simple extension, and calls for a more explicit and pluralistic account of risk in the assessment of values in science.

258. Bullshit slurs
Podosky, P.C. & Cousens, C. (2026). Bullshit slurs. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05835-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Slurs can be used and misused in different ways. We examine two related misapplications. Sometimes a speaker mistakes the person they are slurring as being correctly targeted by a slur. Other times, a speaker is indifferent to whether the slur correctly applies at all. We argue that this latter case constitutes a bullshit slur . Such slurs are distinctively troubling. From the speaker’s perspective, indifference to a slur’s conventional application widens the subordinating force of their utterance, directly threatening their target’s equal standing and warranting greater offence than cases of mistaken identity. From the hearer’s perspective, bullshit slurs foreclose a crucial form of counterspeech: self-protection via linguistic correction. This leaves the target in a particularly precarious position: subordinated directly and unable to engage in self-protective counterspeech.

259. Reconsidering the challenge to traditional metaethics from empirical research on folk moral objectivism
Yang, Q. (2026). Reconsidering the challenge to traditional metaethics from empirical research on folk moral objectivism. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05837-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Recent empirical research on folk moral objectivism appears to bring nothing but bad news for traditional metaethics. Experimental philosophers and psychologists claim that their findings are incompatible with traditional metaethical theories and therefore provide reasons for developing non-traditional metaethical theories. In this paper, I argue that many traditional metaethical theories have the resources to show that they are not inconsistent with the findings of disagreement task studies. If this is correct, then these findings do not, by themselves, settle the choice between traditional and non-traditional metaethical theories. I then sketch how further empirical research might help distinguish between these competing theories. Finally, by distinguishing five different understandings of moral objectivity and explaining how they matter for traditional metaethics, I show that the significance of existing empirical findings depends on the relevant sense of objectivity and suggest what future research on folk moral objectivism should take into account.

260. Understanding metaphysical misunderstanding: (non-)individuals and the metaphysical nature of quantum objects
Arroyo, R. & Arenhart, J.R.B. (2026). Understanding metaphysical misunderstanding: (non-)individuals and the metaphysical nature of quantum objects. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05839-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Deep scientific realism is scientific realism plus the claim that science requires a metaphysical interpretation. Metaphysics is supposed to fill an intelligibility gap: science by itself, it is argued, does not provide a clear image of reality. The metaphysics needed to deliver the goods comes from a toolbox of concepts furnished by free range metaphysics. Such concepts are developed in connection with a world that is familiar to us, and by being applied to science, they carry this familiarity to the most unfamiliar confines of reality. This article examines the prospects of such a strategy. Focusing on the case of quantum objects’ (non-)individuality, it argues that deep realism fails to deliver the promised intelligibility. Construing quantum entities as non-individuals faces an empty toolbox, ‘non-individuality’ being too closely attached to quantum mechanics itself. Dressing quantum entities as individuals distorts the usual familiarity brought by the concept of individuality, since it now has to conform to the unusual demands of quantum mechanics to the point of being unrecognizable. Instead of enhancing our grasp of quantum reality, the imposition of metaphysical categories risks producing not understanding, but metaphysical misunderstanding.

264. Epistemic Discipline in Psychedelic-Assisted Therapy: Memory, Therapy Dynamics, Attachment Patterns, and Sexual Abuse
Simon, G. et al. (2026). Epistemic Discipline in Psychedelic-Assisted Therapy: Memory, Therapy Dynamics, Attachment Patterns, and Sexual Abuse. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09672-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Psychedelic-assisted therapy (PAT) is increasingly used for trauma-related disorders, raising ethical questions beyond client safety. We argue that PAT clients have epistemic needs and clinicians have corresponding obligations that call for epistemic humility and discipline, which are essential for regulating foreseeable epistemic risks and for PAT's ethical structure. Drawing on threads of epistemic vulnerability theory, memory research, and emerging empirical evidence on the cognitive and relational effects of psychedelics, we argue that phenomenological characteristics, including noetic quality, are unreliable indicators of historical veridicality and are associated with epistemic risks. Focusing on individual differences associated with attachment patterns and work with sexual abuse survivors, we show how disrupted epistemic trust, authority sensitivity, and structural disempowerment may magnify those epistemic risks. We then develop epistemic discipline as a fiduciary stance that requires restraint in both directions: neither endorsing experiential material as historically veridical when this cannot be established, nor introducing unwarranted doubt that undermines epistemic agency. Because such epistemic vulnerability and its associated risks are foreseeable, if not entirely predictable, we frame epistemic discipline as a fiduciary obligation of the clinician that spans preparation, dosing, and integration, as well as obligations of training programs, credentialing bodies, and professional communities with responsibility to identify and remedy relevant omissions in training and supervision. Finally, we systematize features of epistemic vulnerability and governance mechanisms across PAT phases.

268. The Epistemic Floor: Institutional Conditions for Genuine Inclusion in Psychiatric Classification
Zeller, M. (2026). The Epistemic Floor: Institutional Conditions for Genuine Inclusion in Psychiatric Classification. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10150
Abstract (original): Abstract The American Psychiatric Association is now designing the process that will produce the next iteration of the Diagnostic and Statistical Manual of Mental Disorders (DSM). Recent work on epistemic injustice in psychiatry converges on a shared diagnosis. The exclusion of psychopathologized perspectives undermines the social objectivity of psychiatric classification, and the literature leaves open how psychiatric science can engage with alternative interpretations of its domain without reducing them to the dominant framework. I propose four institutional conditions that inclusion must satisfy if it is to produce epistemic engagement and avoid tokenism. They are constitutive participation in agenda-setting and evidence evaluation, structural reproduction of participatory mechanisms across revision cycles, procedurally differentiated translation interfaces that preserve the critical content of non-standard perspectives, and bidirectional uptake with epistemic attitude and documented accountability. The conditions operate on the epistemic process that produces diagnostic categories without modifying the operational form those categories take, and are compatible with the functional requirements of the DSM and with the professional identity of psychiatry. They are designed to make inclusion structurally reproducible, where it has so far been contingently achievable, and they supply evaluative criteria for the design decisions the revision now confronts.

269. Protecting (Trans and) Non-Binary People Against Discrimination in and Through EU Law: Time to Overcome Blind Spots

https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10236
Abstract (original): Abstract This Article examines the persistent gaps in the protection of trans and non–binary people against discrimination in European Union law, situating its analysis within a broader socio–legal context marked by rising anti–gender rhetoric and uneven national safeguards. Positioned within a human–rights–oriented and social–justice–driven framework, the Article ultimately calls for renewed legal and political commitment to ensuring that EU equality law effectively safeguards the rights of trans and non–binary individuals.

270. How to Avoid ‘Moralism’ about Obligation
Sekine, K. (2026). How to Avoid ‘Moralism’ about Obligation. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101569
Abstract (original): Abstract Sometimes it seems that no matter how faultlessly we conduct ourselves, we cannot but fail in our obligations, making us apt to feel morally responsible. But according to the popular view I call ‘moralism’ about obligation, we cannot breach our obligations faultlessly, leading moralists to pathologize our feelings, or worse still, to find fault in our conduct. I argue that we should reject moralism, but that we should not do so in the ‘hard’ way, which denies the plausible claim that blameworthiness entails fault. Yet we cannot ‘soften’ our anti-moralism unless we can explain how ordinary agent-regret can rise to a ‘moral’ quality sufficient to do justice to the phenomenology. I offer the outline of a proposal according to which a failure to meet our obligations gives rise to agent-regret with the following ‘moral’ quality: it is extraordinarily hard to let go of or justifiably repress it.

315. The Doctrine of State Necessity
Peftinas, A. (2026). The Doctrine of State Necessity. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70064
Abstract (original): This article examines the doctrine of state necessity as a distinct but relatively underexplored doctrine of constitutional law. Drawing on case law from a range of jurisdictions, it considers instances in which courts have accepted otherwise unconstitutional measures as lawful in order to preserve the operation of state institutions or protect significant parts of the legal order. The article reconstructs the doctrine as a limited, temporary and conditional remedy for situations of constitutional paralysis or systemic legal invalidity. It argues that state necessity should be distinguished both from other cases of emergency law and from a general ‘doctrine of necessity’. The article questions the central theoretical grounds that are offered in the case law and literature in support of the doctrine, such as those based on special executive powers, on analogies with the necessity defence, or on a broader supra‐constitutional ‘law of necessity’. It argues that the doctrine should be understood as a doctrine of constitutional law shaped within the tradition of equity. On that account, the doctrine functions as a limited corrective in exceptional cases of constitutional deadlock in order to preserve the legal order without displacing the constitution itself.

316. Wittgenstein: The Senses of Use By Sandra Laugier, Trans. Daniela Ginsburg. University of Chicago Press, 2025. 240pp. ISBN: 9780226824574

317. In Defence of Open Voting
Seglow, J. (2026). In Defence of Open Voting. J Soc Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70071
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper argues against the secret ballot on the basis of a view of democracy where voters are mutually accountable to one another for their electoral choices. In contrast to Mill's and more recent arguments for opening up the ballot which have a broadly consequentialist nature, this paper offers an principle argument. First it suggests that the egalitarian defence of democracy could be supplemented by augmenting what the egalitarian relationship between citizens consists in. It argues for a ‘substantive egalitarian’ view of democracy where citizens cast their vote on the basis of the values and ideals they support, delegating the role of legislating on their bases to representatives. Each citizen is both an agent, qua co‐author of the ideals underlying state action, and a subject, qua her status as an individual affected by legislation grounded in those ideals. On that basis I argue that citizens' egalitarian political relationship is one of mutual horizontally accountability. The ideal of accountability implies that voters have a legal obligation to publicise their identities and a civic obligation to respond to one another's requests for the reasons for their votes. Only through presenting themselves to each other in this way can citizens as co‐authors properly assume responsibility for their role in providing the normative bases of law. The paper concludes by addressing objections to open voting, specifically that it is unfeasible because it would encourage corruption, and depress turnout and lead to civic shame.
