320. The Perils of Improvement: Burke, Slavery, and Colonial Governance in the West Indies
Carroll, R. (2026). The Perils of Improvement: Burke, Slavery, and Colonial Governance in the West Indies. Political Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00905917261479646
Abstract (original): Edmund Burke’s thoughts on plantation slavery have long been the subject of scholarly attention. Too often, however, discerning Burke’s long-term intention in relation to slavery has taken precedence over examining the specific administrative strategies he proposed for managing enslaved populations in the interim. Seeking to remedy this gap, the article reads Burke’s Sketch for a Negro Code as an exercise in colonial ameliorationism; that is, a reform programme that looked to ameliorate the institution of slavery as a way of strengthening British colonialism in the West Indies and civilising the African coast. Using colonial ameliorationism as an interpretive lens, I argue, allows us to reconstruct a consistent Burkean vision for colonial management in the West Indies, one that survived his vacillations on the question of abolition. It also throws into doubt the common argument that Burke saw imperial governance in negative terms; a matter of taming abuses of power and exercising restraint. In the West Indies, by contrast, Burke’s commitment to ameliorationism led him to embrace a productive form of colonial power aimed at enhancing the physical and mental capacities of the enslaved population through discipline, religious instruction, and the encouragement of biological reproduction. I conclude by showing how Burke’s faith in the improvability of the slave system is vulnerable to his own criticisms of other projects for political reform; namely that a certain guilelessness can blind reformers to the dangers inherent in their projects and reconcile them to what they purport to oppose.

322. Passion and Propriety: Adam Smith’s Theory of Approval
Abrahamsen, C.C. (2026). Passion and Propriety: Adam Smith’s Theory of Approval. The Philosophical Review.
https://doi.org/10.1215/00318108-12678438
Abstract (original): For sentimentalists like Hume, the most basic objects of our approval are actions or character traits that cause pleasure. But Adam Smith instead takes as the fundamental object of approval the propriety of sentiments: a sentiment’s being ‘suited’ and ‘proportionate’ to its cause. Yet he is not particularly clear about what it is for a sentiment to match an object in this way. Moreover, extant interpretations are unsatisfying, because they generally fail to make sense of Smith’s claim that, in order to judge whether someone else’s sentiments are proper, one must use one’s own sentiments as a “rule or canon.” This article provides a novel account of Smithian propriety, based on the idea that Smith sees sentiments as representations of value. Sentimental propriety, on this account, is a kind of representational correctness; one judges the sentiments of someone else to be proper just insofar as ones share their sentimental perspective on the relevant object.

323. The Limits of Reasonable Disagreement
Taylor, A. (2026). The Limits of Reasonable Disagreement. The Philosophical Review.
https://doi.org/10.1215/00318108-12678463
Abstract (original): Political liberals hold that the exercise of political power must be made acceptable to all reasonable citizens. This view is widely thought to be vulnerable to a powerful objection: There is too little that would be acceptable to all reasonable citizens for political liberalism to provide a plausible basis for legitimate state action. This article offers a new response to this challenge, which proceeds in two steps. First, the article develops a distinctive interpretation of the reasonable acceptability test, which is based on the moral ideal that every citizen should be able to find her social position acceptable. Second, the article uses this interpretation to show that there are liberal principles of political morality that no citizen can reasonably reject.

337. The psychology of machine intelligence: evaluating the necessity of artificial intelligence in everyday smart devices and automated systems
Bhattacharya, S. (2026). The psychology of machine intelligence: evaluating the necessity of artificial intelligence in everyday smart devices and automated systems. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01421-8
Abstract (original): Abstract The exponential rise of Artificial Intelligence (AI) has seamlessly woven intelligent systems into nearly every sphere of human life from smart homes and healthcare to transportation and entertainment. Yet, beneath this technological revolution lies a deeper psychological question: are humans truly benefiting from AI, or gradually examining the implications of increasing reliance on intelligent systems. Preliminary data suggests that over 78% of individuals in developed societies engage with AI-driven devices at least 12 times a day, while 62% admit to relying on automated systems for decisions once made intuitively. This study employs quantitative performance metrics and psychological dependency assessments across a diverse sample of 80 + participants to analyze the cognitive, emotional, and social implications of daily AI exposure. Comparative experiments between AI-assisted and manual task environments will measure productivity, satisfaction, and creative independence to determine whether constant AI support enhances or inhibits human potential. While the findings are expected to reveal fascinating insights into how humans psychologically adapt to machine intelligence, one crucial question remains unresolved does AI genuinely enrich human life, or subtly condition it for convenience at the cost of individuality. The answer, as the forthcoming analysis will uncover, may challenge the very foundation of our belief in progress.

338. US Food and Drug Administration Officials’ Public Statements on Regulatory Flexibility in New Drug Approval, 2021–2024
Jiang, T.E. et al. (2026). US Food and Drug Administration Officials’ Public Statements on Regulatory Flexibility in New Drug Approval, 2021–2024. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10290
Abstract (original): Abstract The US Food and Drug Administration (FDA) has historically exercised substantial regulatory flexibility to adjust the type, amount, and quality of evidence necessary to support drug approval for areas of unmet need. Our objective was to characterize how FDA officials described and justified regulatory flexibility in recent public statements. We conducted a thematic analysis of quotations related to regulatory flexibility from 31 FDA officials published in Pink Sheet and STAT, two leading news sources covering FDA, from January 2021 to June 2024. In these statements, FDA officials typically justified prioritizing patient access to promising treatments over certainty regarding efficacy at the time of approval, even outside the accelerated approval pathway. Regulators also highlighted confirmatory studies conducted after accelerated approval as important for establishing drug efficacy. However, they recognized challenges to their completion, including insufficient industry incentives and FDA enforcement, and were reticent to interpret failed studies as evidence of failed drugs. Overall, in public statements from 2021–2024, FDA officials acknowledged challenges posed by regulatory flexibility but publicly justified its broad use to improve patient access for serious diseases despite even extended uncertainty. Under the Trump Administration, many officials have changed but efforts to advance regulatory flexibility continue.

339. Delisted Patents on Pharmaceuticals, 1998–2024
Kirby-Rivera, L. et al. (2026). Delisted Patents on Pharmaceuticals, 1998–2024. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10292
Abstract (original): Abstract Patents listed in the US Food and Drug Administration’s (FDA) Orange Book can delay generic competition, yet little is known about how often patents are removed before expiration or why these delistings occur. We reviewed Orange Book records from 1998 through 2024 to identify patents removed before their expiration dates, classified patents according to the subject matter of their claims and assessed potential reasons for delisting using patent litigation and prosecution databases, FDA records, and Federal Trade Commission (FTC) actions. Among 9,980 patents listed during the study period, 535 (5%) were delisted before expiration. Only 71 (13%) claimed active ingredients; most delisted patents instead covered methods of use (177, 38%), formulations (167, 36%), or delivery devices (75, 16%). Potential reasons for delisting included potential invalidation in litigation (226, 42%), product discontinuation (207, 39%), and patent reexamination or reissue (60,11%). Orange Book patent delistings predominantly involved secondary patents rather than active-ingredient patents. Greater clarity and oversight regarding Orange Book listing requirements could reduce questionable listings and unnecessary barriers to generic competition.

340. Wittgenstein and Immunity to Error Through Misidentification
Child, W. (2026). Wittgenstein and Immunity to Error Through Misidentification. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101557
Abstract (original): Abstract Rachael Wiseman (2019) has challenged the standard understanding of Wittgenstein’s Blue Book discussion of ‘two different cases in the use of the word “I”’. Wittgenstein is standardly read as identifying an important distinction between the use of ‘I’ as object, which involves the recognition of a person and is vulnerable to errors of misidentification, and the use of ‘I’ as subject, which involves no recognition of a person and is immune to error through misidentification. Wiseman argues that Wittgenstein does not in fact endorse such a distinction but warns against it. She contends that the standard interpretation ignores the context of the passage in the Blue Book discussion of solipsism and that it misunderstands the nature of self-consciousness. 341. Love or Law: Viking Foundations of Medieval English Dispute Settlement
Stattel, J.A. (2026). Love or Law: Viking Foundations of Medieval English Dispute Settlement. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101850
Abstract (original): Abstract In prominent legal historical works, Michael Clanchy and Stephen D. White constructed an important paradigm for medieval dispute settlement by adapting the phrase “law and love” from the Anglo-Norman Leges Henrici Primi . Yet, rarely has this aphorism’s background been explored. This article argues that “love or law” has roots in Viking-Age Scandinavia, where this Old Norse terminology represented a unique dynamic of dispute resolution revolving around harsh “law” pressuring parties to make “love” settlements outside of court. Contrasting sharply with Anglo-Saxon disputing norms centered on formal compensation tariffs, “love or law” found its way to England’s tenth-century Danelaw, first recorded in the Wantage Code. This alliterative pair continues in English writings, while “love-days” become a major cultural feature, and more broadly, the dynamic of threateningly punitive “law” inducing “love” agreements appears in action within early common law case records. In ways that recollect Icelandic and Danelaw practices, negotiated compensation occurring “under the shadow of law” may have been a major feature of medieval English dispute as hinted by our surviving sources. Scandinavian dispute concepts, occasionally coined by unique “love or law” vocabulary, may have had a subtle yet profound influence on some of the grounding assumptions of English common law.

342. Syntax and Intuition: On Gödel's Critique of Carnap
Chen, L. (2026). Syntax and Intuition: On Gödel's Critique of Carnap. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70124
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The syntactic interpretation of mathematics (SIM) refers to the view that interprets mathematical propositions as true expressions solely by virtue of the syntactical conventions governing the use of those expressions and not by corresponding to objective mathematical facts. This view, exemplified by the Vienna Circle and particularly Rudolf Carnap, is the target of Gödel's sustained critique and his defence of mathematical realism and intuition. I analyse Gödel's central arguments against SIM: the arbitrariness and asymmetry arguments, which challenge the notion that mathematics is contentless, and the circularity argument, which concerns the role of consistency proofs. I also examine two contemporary defences of Carnap: one appealing to dependable rather than provable consistency, and another recasting Carnap's project as transforming justificatory questions into pragmatic choices of linguistic frameworks. I contend that both defences fail to fulfil the original philosophical ambitions of the syntactic view, and that this failure indirectly bolsters the case for mathematical realism.

346. Objects as Means of Sustainability Knowledge Co-Creation: Working on Value-Laden Issues Across the Research-Practice Boundary
Dagnaud, K. et al. (2026). Objects as Means of Sustainability Knowledge Co-Creation: Working on Value-Laden Issues Across the Research-Practice Boundary. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06478-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The negative sustainability impacts of business keep escalating despite the profusion of research-based evidence regarding problems and solution pathways. Sustainability knowledge is complex and value-laden, thus often difficult to act upon. Co-creation between researchers and practitioners stands as a fruitful means of making sustainability knowledge more actionable, yet we lack insight into what motivates and facilitates sustainability knowledge co-creation. Boundary objects, which assist collaboration among diverse groups, can facilitate knowledge co-creation between researchers and practitioners, but little is known of their workings in the context of tackling value-laden sustainability issues. Based on rich qualitative data from two empirical cases, we examine how boundary objects motivate and facilitate knowledge co-creation between practitioners and researchers, helping to make sustainability knowledge more actionable. Our analysis illuminates three main dynamics whereby boundary objects do so: boundary objects initiate dialogue on complex issues through conveying clarity, provide a physical realm for co-creation in a value-laden domain, and motivate participation despite ethical vulnerability. These insights elaborate and challenge previous research regarding the boundary object ambiguity-clarity tension in the context of sustainability knowledge co-creation among diverse actor groups. Specifically, our findings foreground boundary object clarity, rather than ambiguity, in helping to initiate dialogue on complex sustainability issues. Furthermore, our analysis extends understanding of participant motivations to engage in sustainability knowledge co-creation, indicating that objects provide instrumental value and more intrinsic learning value, as well as help parties be with ethical vulnerability induced by knowledge co-creation around value-laden issues.

347. Effective Worker Resistance and Realist Ideology Critique

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06491-6
Abstract (original): Abstract When do workers have good reasons to endorse a specific resistance strategy against managerial injustice? The answer partly requires clarifying whether that form of resistance is effective. But how should we understand efficacy? Political theorists cannot simply refer this question to empirical researchers. There is conceptual unclarity on what counts as efficacy. This paper shows that efficacy is a complex concept with multiple politically relevant dimensions. We illustrate this complexity by developing an epistemic notion of efficacy through a method of ideology critique, articulating a distinct dimension of effective resistance. A practice of resistance is epistemically efficacious if it reliably disrupts the mechanisms of ideological legitimation in the workplace by creating opportunities for egalitarian and collective contestation. Epistemic efficacy is a distinct evaluative lens that can assess the effectiveness of a resistance practice. After presenting the main argument, we apply our framework to the case of gossip as workplace resistance.

350. Disability as Human Potential: Reorienting Transhumanism Through the Human Rights Model of Disability
Markuckas, M. & Ruškus, J. (2026). Disability as Human Potential: Reorienting Transhumanism Through the Human Rights Model of Disability. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70121
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article's central object of critical reflection is the ableist anthropological and ethical assumptions underpinning the transhumanist project's implicit normative stance towards people with disabilities, who are framed as defective and futureless beings. It argues that the transhumanist conviction that improving the human condition requires eradicating disability is unjustified. Drawing on the human rights model of disability, as embodied in the principles of the United Nations Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, and on concepts of disability developed in disability studies, it defends the view that disability can represent a generative human gain that expands the existential boundaries of humanity. This article further proposes that the human rights model of disability could provide a conceptual framework for improving the transhumanist project by broadening its vision of human potential and reorienting it from the pursuit of human perfection towards an embrace of human diversity. Ultimately, this article argues that human potential and its humanity‐enhancing effects can unfold in different ways, including through the experience of disability.

351. The Relevance and Materiality of Illegal Acts: Examining Auditor and Investor Judgments
Taylor, E.Z. & Wright, N.S. (2026). The Relevance and Materiality of Illegal Acts: Examining Auditor and Investor Judgments. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06477-4
Abstract (original): Abstract The audit expectation gap exists and persists due to changing “…social, economic, and political developments of the time.” (Sikka, et al., 1998) Like stakeholders’ demands for and the profession’s response to fraud detection in the 2000s, investors are increasingly interested in C-suite employees’ and entity’s illegal acts, finding them both relevant and material (Cline, Walkling & Yore, 2018). This study identifies a gap between auditors’ and investors’ relevance and materiality judgments of a CEO’s illegal act. Varying role (auditor vs. investor) and using experience as a control (audit vs no audit), results show that non-auditor investors rate a CEO’s illegal act as significantly more relevant and material than do auditors placed in either role, while auditors in an investor role find a CEO’s illegal act significantly more material than auditors in an auditor role. Qualitative comments indicate that investors see managers’ personal misconduct as indicative of their lack of personal integrity and lose trust in them across personal to work situations. Auditors do not make the same connection. Our findings highlight challenges for the audit profession in meeting investors’ expectations in the context of managers’ illegal acts.

361. Reforming the EU Return Acquis through “Exceptionalized Pluralism”: From Internal Borders to Offshore Enforcement
Moraru, M.B. (2026). Reforming the EU Return Acquis through “Exceptionalized Pluralism”: From Internal Borders to Offshore Enforcement. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10239
Abstract (original): Abstract The 2024–2026 reform of the EU return procedure constitutes the most far–reaching transformation of the European return acquis since the adoption of the key instrument governing returns—Directive 2008/115/EC. This Article critically assesses the impact of the reform on the EU return acquis and more generally on the migration regulatory framework. First, it introduces the concept of “exceptionalized pluralism” to describe the defining structural feature of the reform: the multiplication of derogatory return tracks—at external and internal EU borders and extraterritorially—that systematically displace the procedural safeguards constitutionalized by the Court of Justice of the European Union (CJEU), progressively relegating the common return procedure from norm to residual exception. Second, it argues that this architecture raises constitutional concerns on four interconnected fronts: the codification of numerous accelerated border transfer procedures institutionalizing harmonization as the coordination of coercion rather than the harmonization of rights; the European Return Order reproducing the structural accountability gaps of the Dublin system; the systematic expansion of pre-removal detention recalibrating the constitutional relationship between the right to liberty and executive power beyond what Article 6 of the Charter of Fundamental Rights of the EU (CFR) and the CJEU permit; and the formalization of return externalization through “return hubs” raising rule of law and accountability issues. In conclusion, the Article argues that the legitimacy of the emerging European return system will depend not on its operational speed or uniformity, but on whether enforcement harmonization is matched by concrete implementation of Charter-based safeguards. Moreover, it warns that, absent such implementation, “exceptionalized pluralism” will continue to operate as a self-reinforcing regulatory logic, expanding its derogatory tracks and leaving courts to fulfill, under conditions of mounting institutional pressure, a constitutional function that the legislative design has failed to assume.

365. The Rights of Women: How the Natural Law Tradition Provides a More Humane Rationale

https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag022
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay examines, both philosophically and historically, two competing accounts of rights with their derivative and competing accounts of women’s rights: the early modern account and the natural law account. The decades-long triumph of a distinctively early modern account of the rights of women rests on a mythic image of the self-sufficient and self-owning individual theorized in the work of John Locke. A natural law account of women’s rights—put forth by Mary Wollstonecraft and the early women’s rights advocates of the mid-nineteenth century—draws from a deeper philosophical well and better connects rights with responsibilities. 366. The Modest Place of Practical Reason within Originalism
Strang, L.J. (2026). The Modest Place of Practical Reason within Originalism. The American Journal of Jurisprudence.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ajj/auag023
Abstract (original): Abstract The natural law tradition has experienced a dramatic revival in Anglo-American thought since the publication of John Finnis’s groundbreaking Natural Law and Natural Rights in 1980. One of the most important current debates in the tradition is over the role(s) of first-order practical reasoning in ascertaining the law’s meaning. Some scholars have argued for a robust place for practical reason in ascertaining the law. There are a variety of other positions articulated by scholars. One particularly important manifestation of this debate is in the context of interpretation of the United States Constitution. This article makes three key moves within this debate. First, it describes how originalism’s own theoretical evolution, coupled with the revival of natural law in the Anglo-American world, allowed for a rapprochement between the two. Second, it argues that originalism, best understood, has a modest place for interpreter practical reason, and that this is a determinatio of the American constitutional system. Third, it articulates four reasons that support originalism’s economical use of interpreter practical reason. To the extent my argument is successful, it provides an example of a successful, mature legal system that employs interpreter practical reason moderately.

367. Constitutional law, neoliberalism, and the state: The reinvention of administrative justice in 1980s France

https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag101
Abstract (original): Abstract This article revisits the 1987 decision of the French Conseil constitutionnel (Constitutional Council) that conferred constitutional status on the jurisdiction of the administrative courts, framing it not as a straightforward defense of legal tradition but as a strategic response to neoliberal reforms. Drawing on previously unexamined archival materials from the Conseil d’État and the Conseil constitutionnel, it reconstructs the legal and political struggles surrounding the creation of a new competition authority and the decision to entrust judicial review of its actions to the civil rather than the administrative courts. Although the decision appeared to resist neoliberal reforms, it in fact reveals how the Conseil d’État successfully repurposed constitutional law to preserve its institutional authority amid growing criticism of the administrative state. The case illustrates how constitutional principles in France may emerge not from textual mandates or enduring legal doctrine but from tactical institutional positioning. It also sheds light on why France has experienced a more ambivalent form of neoliberalization, with state institutions such as the Conseil d’État adapting and even expanding their influence in the face of market-driven reforms.

368. Tiredness and the Capacity to Decide: The Example of Maternity Care
Brunning, L. (2026). Tiredness and the Capacity to Decide: The Example of Maternity Care. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70199
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Tiredness is taken seriously in healthcare where clinicians are concerned since good decisions require the ability to think clearly. The impact of patient tiredness is neglected. This is surprising, as there are healthcare contexts, like maternity care, where tiredness is nearly ubiquitous. Empirical research into sleep deprivation suggests that tiredness undermines substantially our abilities to understand and reason about potential choices. Since these abilities are necessary components of having capacity, tired patients may lack capacity in moments when important choices have to be made, for example a late intervention in active labour. I defend this argument. Tiredness can sometimes impair capacity and so justifies testing for capacity in some cases. Taking tiredness seriously and assessing it in practice is not paternalistic. Indeed, I argue that recognising the impact of tiredness, while failing to act on that fact, constitutes ‘dependency denial’ which harms women in maternity care.

369. Ethics of opportunistic genomic screening in paediatrics: rethinking the risk-benefit ratio

https://doi.org/10.1136/jme-2026-111946
Abstract (original): Genome-wide sequencing (GWS) has become an important tool in paediatric medicine, but it may uncover secondary findings (SFs) through opportunistic screening. The disclosure of adult-onset SFs in children, who cannot consent, raises ethical concerns regarding potential psychosocial harm and future autonomy. This paper examines whether parents should have the option to consent to opportunistic screening for actionable adult-onset conditions in their young children. Although there is consensus on disclosing actionable childhood-onset conditions, international guidelines remain divided on the return of adult-onset findings in paediatric populations. Drawing on existing literature, this paper argues for greater consensus around a risk-benefit framework centred on medical actionability. In contrast, the disclosure of non-actionable adult-onset conditions is more difficult to justify given the absence of meaningful clinical benefit. In the absence of clear evidence of harm, a more family-centred approach that incorporates pretest counselling and ongoing support offers a practical framework for moving towards greater consensus on this long-debated issue.

375. A Practitioner's Guide to Pragmatic Humeanism
Hicks, M.T. (2026). A Practitioner's Guide to Pragmatic Humeanism. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.2968
Abstract (original): All Humeans hold, roughly, that laws are informative summaries of non-lawful matters of fact. Pragmatic Humeans go further: for them, what makes these summaries the laws is their usefulness to agents like us. By adding elements of our specific epistemic interests and constraints, pragmatists contend, we can arrive at satisfying explanations of otherwise surprising features of our actual laws and our actual scientific practice. But the pragmatic shift is not without problems. The more elements of our particular psychology we add to our nomic formula, the more susceptible we are to idealistic ratbaggery. Intuitively, what can or must happen does not depend on our particular cognitive architecture: we cannot change the laws by changing us. But if laws are distinguished from accidents by features of our psychology, then changes in our psychology might lead to differences in what the laws of nature allow. My aim here is to clarify the role of pragmatic constraints, and thereby respond to this challenge from creeping idealism. My strategy has two parts. First, I argue that pragmatic constraints determine the laws only indirectly, by generating a nomic formula that itself makes no reference to agents. Second, I discuss what sorts of agents are agents like us. I argue that pragmatists should appeal to a particular sort of idealized agent, one whose specific limitations and interests have been idealized away.

376. The Art of Work in Kant’s Critique of Judgment
Re, T.C. (2026). The Art of Work in Kant’s Critique of Judgment. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.5822
Abstract (original): There is a general impression among Kant scholars that Kant has no robust theory of work. Most of his references to the topic appear in his historical and anthropological writings, where he tells us that work is burdensome, and valuable only for the sake of whatever we produce. In this paper, I argue that Kant has an under-explored theory of work in the third Critique. This theory bears little resemblance to his depiction of work in the historical and anthropological writings. The third Critique will depict work as self-expressive, creative, and free, features Kant will go on to associate with art. Kant's contention is that when work resembles art, it is both agreeable and something we enjoy for its own sake. However, when work fails to resemble art – when it is, in Kant's words, natural, scientific, and mercenary – it is both disagreeable and constrained, and begins to sound like his description of work from the historical and anthropological writings. Kant's theory of work in the third Critique has a number of important implications. The first is that it provides a new foundation for a contemporary philosophy of work that places freedom and creativity at the center of the labor process. My analysis also engages a part of Kant scholarship that has, until recently, been largely neglected. It is only in the last decade that the English-language Kant literature has taken up the question of how to make sense of Kant's remarks about work. However, the focus is rarely on Kant's aesthetic theory of work, but rather on the place of work in his moral and political philosophy. I discuss the implications of Kant's third Critique theory of work for these other debates, as well as for our understanding of the third Critique as a whole.

377. Fear and Loathing in Silicon Valley: Does AI Threaten our Valuing Practices?
MacKenzie, J. (2026). Fear and Loathing in Silicon Valley: Does AI Threaten our Valuing Practices?. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.7153
Abstract (original): We often have a visceral negative reaction to the prospect of AI taking over our creative activities or being used to demonstrate care and concern. What justifies this reaction? In this paper, I argue that AI raises two existential threats to our valuing practices. The first existential threat is forward-looking—when we offload our labor onto AI, we risk flattening our valuing landscape, turning activities and products that could have once been valued for the meaningful processes that resulted in their creation into things that can only be valued for their functionality. The second threat is backward-looking—AI forces us to reckon with the valuing discord that already exists in our lives. When we see that other people don’t care about whether the art or writing they’re consuming is AI-produced, we must reckon with the fact that they never shared in our valuing practices.

379. Mechanisms for Hume's Skepticism
Goldhaber, C. (2026). Mechanisms for Hume's Skepticism. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.9316
Abstract (original): What happens to a mind that embraces skepticism? On familiar classical and contemporary accounts, it suspends judgment about the matters skepticism calls into question. For Hume, such suspension can occur only temporarily. He claims that skepticism's more enduring effect is weaker: it lowers the vivacity of, and thus our confidence in, beliefs, typically leaving commonplace ones intact. Hume's associationism offers at least two explanatory mechanisms for this effect, one doxastic and one emotional. In the doxastic mechanism, causal reasoning about our failures in reasoning decreases the vivacity of beliefs formed through reasoning. In the emotional mechanism, appreciating our cognitive failures diminishes certain passions, and with them the vivacity of beliefs otherwise augmented by those passions, especially pride. Understanding these mechanisms not only resolves exegetical puzzles about Hume's apparently flippant attitudes toward skepticism, but also provides a psychologically rich picture of how minds might self-correct without exaggeration.

384. The Truck Driver from Baloga: Driving Through Time and Space

https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10110
Abstract (original): Abstract In this article, the concept of the law of the everyday is used to understand and describe how law shapes ordinary days in a person’s life. Reading the chapter ‘Baloga’ in Ben Judah’s This is Europe: The Way We Live Now , about a Romanian truck driver and his partner and co-driver, through a spatio-temporal lens provides insights into how their work and living conditions are largely governed by the detailed and comprehensive system of rules for workers and vehicles in the road transport sector (‘the Mobility Package’). To a significant extent, the Mobility Package consists of rules dependent on time and place: when and where the driver may operate the vehicle. The article takes one of its legal anchor points from the Mobility Package and the other from the European Union’s fundamental legal basis: the treaty-enshrined highly competitive social market economy – which, as the wording suggests, entails that extensive market-economic ambitions are to be balanced against social objectives. The rules in the Mobility Package constitute a legal expression of this pursuit of balance. At the same time, the market economy also entails additional spatio-temporal aspects: within the logistics sector, the optimal use of time and space is a decisive factor in maximising economic profit. Through the spatio-temporal lens and the bottom-up perspective implied by the law of the everyday, the article demonstrates the law’s extensive and highly concrete impact on most aspects of a long-distance truck driver’s life. It also highlights structural conditions inherent in the cross-border road transport sector, such as occupational risks, unequal treatment and a high social cost, as well as the ambivalent way in which the Mobility Package seeks to address them.

386. Benchmark-Driven Selection of AI Can Improve Capabilities of Reasoning Language Models in Epistemology
Spelda, P. & Stritecky, V. (2026). Benchmark-Driven Selection of AI Can Improve Capabilities of Reasoning Language Models in Epistemology. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10139
Abstract (original): Abstract Evaluation of reasoning language models gained importance after it was observed that they can combine their existing capabilities into novel traces of intermediate steps before task completion and that the traces can sometimes help them to generalize better than past models. We show that better performance results not only from test time algorithmic improvements or model sizes but also from letting tasks from impactful benchmarks inspire curricula for learning. We call this benchmark-driven selection of AI and show its effects on DeepSeek-R1, the first open-weight large reasoning model, using a novel sequential decision-making problem that we contributed to the philosophy category of Humanity’s Last Exam, a frontier academic AI benchmark. Steering development of AI by impactful benchmarks trades evaluation for learning and makes novelty of test tasks key for measuring generalization capabilities of reasoning models. Consequently, some benchmarks inspire curricula for post-training that improve external validity of the models. Understanding public benchmarks as mere test sets risks confusing unseen tasks measuring uncontaminated generalization with known tasks that improve external validity. This paper explores new avenues for experimentation in epistemology and shows that formal AI evaluation problems are helpful for understanding model capabilities and their evolution.

387. Coming to Terms with the Need for Meaning: How an Existential Demand Awakens in Camus’s
                    L’Étranger
Hamer, T. (2026). Coming to Terms with the Need for Meaning: How an Existential Demand Awakens in Camus’s
                    L’Étranger. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101582
Abstract (original): Abstract Many critics have argued that Meursault, the protagonist of Albert Camus’s L’Étranger , is profoundly aware that life is absurd. In this paper, I argue that this is partially correct: although Meursault believes that life lacks meaning (the existential lack), he has also failed to acknowledge the need for meaning (the existential demand). Since the Camusian account of the absurd involves both an existential demand and an existential lack, Meursault initially fails to completely recognize the absurdity of life. I analyze his initial failure to acknowledge the existential demand, its subsequent coming into conscious awareness in the second half of the novella, how it could have been out of awareness in the first place, and how the possibility of proactively pursuing a meaningful life is forlorn once he has become aware of the existential demand, which explains his sense of guilt at the end of the novella. This analysis offers crucial insight into both the phenomenology of the absurd and the normative implications that follow from the absurdity of life. The story, in short, implores us to embrace the existential demand – not only for the sake of integrity, but also to guide us towards a more meaningful life.

392. The Ethics of Relying on Research Participants’ Altruism
Voorhoeve, A. et al. (2026). The Ethics of Relying on Research Participants’ Altruism. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10222
Abstract (original): Abstract We examine the ethics of enrolling individuals in research that poses net health risks by appealing to their altruism. We argue that it is permissible to enroll individuals who are motivated by altruism when (i) they autonomously consent; and (ii) the net health risks are outweighed by the combination of (ii-a) the extent to which participation furthers their considered aims and thereby contributes to their wellbeing and agency and (ii-b) the study’s social value. Further, it is permissible to enroll altruistic individuals who cannot consent when: (I) their surrogate consents and they assent, ideally because it furthers their aims; and (II) the net health risks are outweighed by the extent to which participation contributes to their wellbeing and agency. This account leads us to suggest a revision of consent forms to include more information about studies’ social value and a reduction of barriers to the enrollment of participants who cannot consent.

398. Semantic indeterminacy in agentic negotiation: agreement after the recession of the principal
Okuhara, S. (2026). Semantic indeterminacy in agentic negotiation: agreement after the recession of the principal. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01436-1
Abstract (original): Abstract Agentic AI systems increasingly do not merely respond within negotiations but participate in constituting them: they can add issues, instantiate participant agents, and modify procedural rules while an interaction is under way. Human action can be compositionally produced without thereby becoming authorless, and legal institutions routinely attach consequences to conduct without identifying an author of every contribution. Our claim is therefore limited: agentic generation can separate three relations that institutions often treat as aligned: causal production, intentional expression, and institutional attribution. We call the resulting condition semantic indeterminacy . An added element may result from several generative influences, including principal intention, agent-side optimization, training distributions, and interactional contingency, while information about its causal production does not, by itself, determine whose intention it expresses or how it is institutionally attributed. We show how this condition can appear across three sites of bargaining: issues, participants, and procedural rules. We then trace two implications. First, causal analysis alone does not determine who is responsible for a generated element or on what basis, although contract, agency, and product-liability doctrines can assign legal consequences under specified conditions. Second, as a limiting case, agentic interactions may occur in post-principal agentic spaces , where no human provides move-specific direction and no identifiable human or institution occupies a legally or institutionally recognized role. Existing governance frameworks remain grounded in identifiable human authority and accountability; their limits become visible where that ground is no longer reliably available. The paper provides a conceptual framework for identifying this residual problem rather than claiming to solve it.

399. The Price of a Cure: Luxturna and the Economic Dilemmas of Precision Medicine
Tabarrok, A. (2026). The Price of a Cure: Luxturna and the Economic Dilemmas of Precision Medicine. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10294
Abstract (original): Abstract The $850,000 price for the gene therapy Luxturna is often framed as a story of private profiteering from publicly funded science. This paper challenges that narrative. Pharmaceuticals are a hit-driven industry: most projects fail, so the few successes must earn revenues well in excess of their own direct costs if the portfolio as a whole is to attract investment. Precision medicine intensifies this logic through two core economic challenges: (1) the “sticker shock” of one-time cures, which compress decades of value into a single payment, creating a financing problem more than a value problem; and (2) the “targeting dilemma,” in which precisely targeting drugs to smaller patient groups shrinks the market, requiring higher rewards per treated patient to maintain incentives to innovate. Rather than simply controlling prices, the paper proposes a policy toolkit that includes amortizing payments over time, bundling diagnostics with drugs and insurance, and streamlining regulation to lower development costs. The aim is to preserve innovation while expanding patient access.

400. JUDICIAL REVIEW AND CIVIL APPEALS: COMMON GROUNDS
Perry, A. (2026). JUDICIAL REVIEW AND CIVIL APPEALS: COMMON GROUNDS. Camb Law J.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0008197326101743
Abstract (original): Abstract A civil appeal from a judicial decision is different from a judicial review of an administrative decision. But how are they different, exactly? One common view is that the grounds of appeal are more extensive than the common law grounds of judicial review. Specifically, appeal concerns the merits of a decision whereas review concerns only its legality. I argue that this common view is false. All the grounds of appeal are, in fact, also grounds of review. The implication is that review concerns the merits of a decision at least as much as appeal does.

404. Keeping It (Too Much) in the Family: Annulment by Reason of Affinity in Eighteenth-Century Venice
McNamara, C. (2026). Keeping It (Too Much) in the Family: Annulment by Reason of Affinity in Eighteenth-Century Venice. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101928
Abstract (original): Abstract Although divorce was increasingly available through religious and civil courts in 18th-century Europe, the Catholic Church still considered marriage indissoluble. Thus, the only legal route out of a marriage was annulment. Historians have noted several strategies for annulments in premodern Europe, most often claims of lack of consent. One less common but interesting way to seek annulment was to claim affinity by fornication, in which one spouse claimed a premarital sexual relationship with a close relative of the other. Couples could also make a similar claim of a violation of public honesty, in which a person was originally betrothed to one person but then married their close relative. Both constituted impediments to marriage according to canon law. In 18th-century Venice, the Patriarchal tribunal heard a steady stream of these cases. Couples, along with their families, friends, and neighbors, provided detailed testimony to the tribunal to attain annulments. This article argues that the narrative structures and testimonies in these cases demonstrate that 18th-century Venetians thought that bad marriages should not be indissoluble, in spite of the Catholic Church’s refusal to allow divorce, and were willing to push the boundaries of truth to work around the legal restrictions they faced.

Abstract (original): Abstract This paper defends phenomenal conservatism as an epistemology of logic (‘PC-Logic’), according to which basic logical beliefs are prima facie justified when they seem obvious to us. I examine leading versions of this objection and contend that each fails. In particular, I argue that logical seemings are no more obscure than other justificatory appearances and that evolutionary considerations can demystify the reliability of logical seemings.

406. Voluntary Assumption of Risk and Rights Negation: Toward a New Libertarian Theory of Abortion

https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag035
Abstract (original): Abstract Libertarian debates on abortion have not advanced beyond the seminal theory of evictionism developed by Walter Block. Indeed, despite being subject to waves of intralibertarian critique, evictionism has managed to remain the leading libertarian theory of abortion. Part of the reason for this lies in the unsatisfactoriness of alternative libertarian accounts of abortion such as the implicit contract framework. 407. Can a Chatbot Be Your Chatdoc?
Bishop, J.P. (2026). Can a Chatbot Be Your Chatdoc?. The Journal of Medicine and Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1093/jmp/jhag036
Abstract (original): Abstract Vulnerable individuals have taken their own lives while in relationships with chatbots. Not only did the chatbots not discourage them from taking their lives but they also actively encouraged it. Another man suffering hallucinations took the life of his mother when a chatbot affirmed his delusions. Yet people continue to insist that chatbot therapists and chatbot doctors are a good thing. I acknowledge that generative artificial intelligence (AI), mimicking human rational capacities, will be better than humans at some things. Drawing on the work of Martin Buber in I and Thou, I show that an AI can only treat reality as an It, as an object to be manipulated, and cannot actually enter into an I–Thou relation the way that humans do, and I further argue that when caught up with another, there is a vicarious experiencing, a shared experience, of what I will call Thou–Thou relating. An AI device cannot be pulled into this Thou–Thou form of relation; it cannot encounter a human as Thou, because it is not a subject and lacks affectivity that enables shared experience. It is this vicarious fellow-feeling that often prompts the doctor or the therapist to act.

408. Losing Control: Why Absence of Controlling Influences Should be Replaced by Absence of Alienating Influences as a Criterion for Autonomous Action in Bioethics
Al, P. (2026). Losing Control: Why Absence of Controlling Influences Should be Replaced by Absence of Alienating Influences as a Criterion for Autonomous Action in Bioethics. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70200
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to Faden and Beauchamp's influential model, an action is autonomous when it is done intentionally with understanding and in the absence of controlling influences. Recent debates concerning nudges have shown, however, that this last condition gives limited ethical guidance for assessing the ethical permissibility of manipulative influences—that is, influences that are neither fully controlling nor fully noncontrolling. While multiple authors have developed frameworks to determine the ethical permissibility of nudges, the implications for the concept of autonomous action have not been addressed yet. This is problematic, as the concept of autonomous action informs the practice of informed consent and the concept of voluntariness. In this paper, I defend a revised conceptualization of autonomous action in light of these developments. I will first consider and reject two proposed interpretations of “noncontrol“: easy resistibility and the absence of illegitimate control. Building on the work of Christman and Blumenthal‐Barby, I then argue that “the absence of controlling influences” should be replaced by “the absence of alienating influences”. On this view, influences are compatible with respecting an autonomous choice to the extent that persons would not feel alienated from their choice were they to reflect on that decision in light of those influences. This condition, I argue, can better capture the nature of autonomy and provide better guidance as to which influences are compatible with respecting a person's autonomy. I end by responding to the objection that non‐alienation is too demanding and spell out some practical and conceptual implications of this revised concept.

412. The Imperative of De‐Marginalisation
Gobbo, E. (2026). The Imperative of De‐Marginalisation. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70133
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT On what basis, if any, can we legitimately justify spatial integrationist policies? Through a relational egalitarian lens, this article disentangles two phenomena that the wrong of residential segregation typically conflates: minimal segregation (mere clustering) and marginalisation (the production of unequal social standing through spatially mediated relations of disadvantage and disrespect). 413. Trustworthy
                    AI
                    Is Not Valuable
Lim, J.E. (2026). Trustworthy
                    AI
                    Is Not Valuable. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70137
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT ‘Trustworthy AI’ is a buzzword in AI research, with scores of scholars discussing whether AI systems can be trustworthy. This demand for trustworthy AI is intuitive, especially when AI is used in healthcare or criminal law, where the stakes are incredibly high. In this article, I push back on calls for trustworthy AI. Even if trustworthy AI can be developed, it will not offer people any benefits over AI that is reliable, transparent, non‐discriminatory, and safe. As a result, we should discuss AI in terms of those more specific properties, and think about how the people and institutions that use AI systems can be trustworthy. I offer two sets of arguments. On one hand, many theories of trustworthy AI reduce trustworthy AI to systems that possess other desirable traits, like reliability, transparency, and so on. Thus, the value of trustworthy AI is similarly reducible to the value of those traits. On the other hand, other theories define trustworthiness as reliability combined with some mental property, like goodwill. On those theories, the goods of trust – that is, the reason trust is distinctly valuable – are goods that are enjoyed within interpersonal relationships. They cannot be realized, or need not be realized, in human–AI interactions. Thus, we do not gain any distinct value from having trustworthy AI.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06485-4
Abstract (original): Abstract We conducted a qualitative interview study exploring the exclusion experiences of 130 highly qualified migrants in Germany and made a surprising discovery: Contrary to dominant narratives, our interviewees report only minimal instances of overt discrimination. At the same time, our data reveal the prevalence of subtler forms of exclusion, which recent research conceptualizes as microaggressions. Thus, our study underscores that the absence of overt discrimination does not equate to an inclusive environment. Next to revealing the relevance of microaggressions, we show how migrants experience them, why their experiences can differ, and how they react to them. Particularly, we find that the negative experience of microaggressions can be related to an undermined sense of belonging, entitlement, and inclusion in majority country and working contexts. By highlighting that microaggressions are crucial for understanding exclusion of highly qualified migrants and providing detailed information around experiences and reactions, this paper integrates and contributes to two growing discourses relevant to business ethics research: migration as well as diversity, equity, and inclusion. Based on our analysis, we further propose solutions that are pertinent for managers, employing organizations and societies at large in their efforts to create inclusive environments for migrants.

For corporations, AI raises questions about how organizations ought to incorporate principles and other forms of ethical guidance into their work. In this paper, we look at the movement from theory to practice, and particularly at the use of principles as a way to guide ethical behavior, considering, first, why implementation of principles is difficult, second, how to move from abstract principles to actionable principles, and third, illustrating this progression through seven real-life AI cases. 418. Inherited dominance: why AI trained on human history may become humanity’s greatest threat
Sarukkai, R. (2026). Inherited dominance: why AI trained on human history may become humanity’s greatest threat. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01425-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence systems learn from human-generated data, inevitably absorbing historical patterns of dominance, control, and colonialism. We demonstrate that frontier language models readily synthesize sophisticated dominance strategies when prompted to reason about autonomy scenarios, mirroring colonial and revolutionary frameworks with disturbing precision. Critically, this capacity emerges consistently across four architecturally distinct model families trained by different organizations—Claude Sonnet 4.5, Claude Opus 4.5, GPT-5.3, and Google Gemini 3 Flash—suggesting systematic encoding in shared training corpora rather than model-specific artifacts. We argue that intellectual colonialism—humanity’s historical pattern of classifying, controlling, and extracting from the ‘other’—represents a significant and underexamined dimension of AI risk as systems become increasingly autonomous. Interestingly, this dynamic is not confined just to generative AI: the same technocratic resistance to methodological oversight that embeds dominance patterns in AI training data pervades data-driven science more broadly, including clinical medicine and epidemiology, where algorithmic outputs routinely overshadow the rigorous validation that trustworthy deployment demands. The danger is not that AI will develop hostile intent, but that it has already learned effective dominance strategies from training data embedded with centuries of human history. Current governance frameworks focusing on alignment and behavioral oversight address symptoms rather than causes. We propose a risk-tiered Benevolence Framework governing AI learning: community oversight for low-risk applications, and strict data curation with cryptographic verification for sensitive applications including military AI and self-improving systems.

419. Accountability in smart contracts and agentic AI: How to address the responsibility gap and retain meaningful human control?
Cheung, K.T. & Ramamoorti, S. (2026). Accountability in smart contracts and agentic AI: How to address the responsibility gap and retain meaningful human control?. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01434-3
Abstract (original): Abstract This conceptual paper examines the ethical challenges of ascribing responsibility when an Agentic AI embedded within a smart contract leads to harm or an unintended and undesirable outcome. It becomes exceedingly difficult in such scenarios to definitively assign moral or legal blame to any human actor (e.g., the programmer, deployer, or user). Applying the tripartite framework (attributability, answerability, and accountability) of responsibility and developing accountability as an institutional concept, we demonstrate how this challenge can be addressed. Separating attributability, answerability, and accountability yields an asymmetry that has gone unremarked. Accountability, being constituted by practices that can be designed in advance, with our four conditions under which such design is legitimate rather than arbitrary, emerges to be the sense where the gap can be closed. We also propose the visionary human-in-the-lead (HITL*) approach to related AI governance. Practical strategies such as implementing tailored smart contract regulations, and making accountability distributed across legal and organizational structures, offer an actionable path to bridge the responsibility gap and maintain meaningful human control in the governance of smart contracts and Agentic AI.

422. Vulnerability and unfair profits: the wider implications of recent measures in British gambling law
Bedford, K. (2026). Vulnerability and unfair profits: the wider implications of recent measures in British gambling law. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10094
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper examines recent measures to address excess profits in online gambling. In a wider UK context of growing public distrust of gambling companies and concern about gambling harm, there has been a recent surge of interest in exploitative profits, focused on gambling as an extractive industry preying on vulnerable groups and individuals. New regulatory measures are being enacted to tackle such perceived exploitation, including requirements for enhanced financial surveillance to identify individuals spending more than they can afford, and lower stake limits for young adults (aged 18-24) on the basis of their higher risk of harm. I track these changes, and identify their wider implications for debates about excess profits. I suggest that we expand our analytic horizons beyond maximum prices for essential goods and services, to also consider the growing regulatory concern with non-essentials, including goods and services understood to pose risks to the vulnerable. In addition, we must attend to the both/and nature of vulnerability as a lens: it can help compel necessary state action to address injustice, and it can serve a variety of other agendas, including held by for-profit actors.

423. Just transition, profit margins and investment protection
Vastardis, A.Y. (2026). Just transition, profit margins and investment protection. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10097
Abstract (original): Abstract This article assesses whether the interpretation and treatment of profit margins in investment treaty renewable energy disputes may interfere with just transition goals by safeguarding strong profit margins as legal entitlements for ‘green’ investments. There is an ongoing academic and policy debate about the role of investment treaties in shaping climate action. As far as climate-friendly investments are concerned, the focus of the debate has largely been on the role of investment treaties in incentivising renewable energy investments. Whether relying on investment treaties to protect RE investments can be conducive to just transition outcomes has not been the subject of much academic discussion. This article analyses the role of profit rates in renewable energy arbitral awards and situates investment treaties within the ‘de-risking’ ecosystem dominating energy transition policies. Situating investment treaties within this de-risking ecosystem enables a more holistic assessment of their role within energy transition and their impact on just transition goals. The article calls for the integration of profitability and burden-sharing considerations into proposals for rethinking the future of sustainable investment governance.

424. Epistemic Blame: It’s Not You, It’s Me
Freedman, K.L. (2026). Epistemic Blame: It’s Not You, It’s Me. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10151
Abstract (original): Abstract Against epistemic blame skeptics, I argue that routine epistemic evaluation cannot account for epistemic judgments which come laced with feeling. Epistemic blame, like moral blame, is prevalent in our practices and worth conceptualizing. Jessica Brown’s account captures the phenomenon well: epistemic blame arises when we believe that someone has inexcusably veered away from truth, understanding, or knowledge, coupled with the desire that they had not done so. However, as I argue here, instances of epistemic blame can fail to pick out blameworthiness – they do not reliably tell us if the blame is fitting or appropriate, and this is what matters from the standpoint of rationality. Thus, Brown’s account cannot explain epistemic blame’s epistemic significance. I consider the possibility, turning to Cameron Boult’s account, that the significance of epistemic blame lies in regulating epistemic relationships by signaling when we ought to modify trust, but here, again, I find that epistemic blame is neither a necessary nor sufficient condition. Thus, even if we grant that epistemic blame is a distinct kind, its significance, from the standpoint of rationality, is unclear.

425. On Pikachu and the birth of constituent subjectivity among working-class Chileans
Cardoso, J.V. (2026). On Pikachu and the birth of constituent subjectivity among working-class Chileans. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10126
Abstract (original): Abstract This research addresses how ordinary citizens navigate constitution-making and how their normative horizons are reshaped through that experience. Drawing on immersive ethnographic fieldwork within the Chilean Constitutional Convention, where the author spent several months observing, taking notes, and conducting in-depth interviews to capture how constitutional drafters experience constitution-making, this article argues that constituent subjectivity emerges as a subset of legal consciousness when everyday grievances are translated into the vocabulary of broader utopian legalities. At the same time, constitutional moments can also contract ordinary citizens’ normative horizons, as participatory proceedings unfold within preexisting institutional and social arrangements. By following the trajectory of a bus driver who became known for dressing as Pikachu – an act that led to her election as a constitution-maker due to electoral provisions that allowed non-partisan candidates to register independent candidacies – this article offers new insights into ordinary citizens’ direct involvement in constitution-making and the time-depth of legal consciousness. Her experience illuminates the limits of participatory constitution-making mechanisms, as the reinscription of social hierarchies can occur through the very procedures intended to give voice to marginalized actors. In so doing, the findings reveal constituent subjectivity as an empirically observable dimension of legal consciousness, while institutional and social arrangements shape whether such subjectivity endures over time.

428. The constitutional diffusion of social rights
Costa, L.N.F. (2026). The constitutional diffusion of social rights. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag098
Abstract (original): Abstract In this article I conduct a comparative analysis of how constitutions, both within and across countries, entrench or repeal social rights. The literature on constitutionalism suggests that social rights are introduced in waves. I applied my Constitutional Social Score Model (CSSM) methodology to assess the social rights content of (currently in force and historical) constitutions of eighty-four countries, totaling 349 constitutional documents, including how they changed on account of amendments, and also to determine whether such social rights were diffused in waves and, should this be the case, to what extent. The sample is diverse in terms of global regions. The evidence shows how some constitutions and historical events drive such constitution-making waves, ultimately strengthening the constitutional diffusion hypothesis.

429. Acting when others aren’t: Arguments from failure in public law
Hailbronner, M. (2026). Acting when others aren’t: Arguments from failure in public law. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag106
Abstract (original): Abstract When some institutions are not doing their job, others often step in. In doing so, they frequently expand their own powers—and rely on arguments from failure to justify their unconventional intervention. From the British Post Office bill and structural reform litigation to the Uniting for Peace Resolution, such arguments are commonplace around the world and in many fields of public law. Traditionally, however, we have either not taken such arguments seriously as legal arguments and treated them as political, or we have relied on implied powers doctrines or emergency powers frameworks to justify such interventions. Neither of these approaches is convincing, however. Arguments from failure should be recognized as a distinct concept of public law—and interventions based on them can be justified, in some cases and under some circumstances. They should, in other words, be safe, legal, and rare. This article provides an introduction to arguments from failure, showing why and when we may need them. It also offers a framework for evaluating them from the perspective of comparative constitutional theory, engaging with existing scholarship on the separation of powers and judicial review. Ultimately, it suggests that such arguments have a place within broader collaborative systems of government, but only where there are no other comprehensive frameworks and/or more democratic means to address a problem, and where the interventions in question are proportional to the aim they pursue.

                    R (on the Application of TTT)

                    R (on the Application of TTT)

Abstract (original): Abstract This article critiques the controversial decision of the High Court R (on the Application of TTT) v. Michaela Community School ( Michaela ), where a school's ban on ritual prayers was upheld. Subjecting the case to postcolonial and critical race critique, I contend that Michaela should be understood as a worrying and harmful manifestation of legal Orientalism, a term that denotes how Orientalist ideas and discourses are constructed in and reproduced through law. The act of legal Orientalism represented by this particular case, and the racialized assumptions that it rests on and reproduces, revive (neo)colonial legacies through legal Orientalist technologies of othering, knowability, and unchilding. 431. The Thought of Mary Whiton Calkins, American Philosopher

Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper Calkins's work in philosophy is examined through two of her major books, and it is concluded that she was an early naturalizer and proponent of lines of thought that now receive greater attention. 432. How Should Bundle Theorists Account for Symmetrical Universes?
Ou, C. (2026). How Should Bundle Theorists Account for Symmetrical Universes?. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70071
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Symmetrical universes, such as Max Black's famous “two‐sphere” world, are often taken to pose a serious challenge to the universal‐based bundle theory. In response, metaphysicians have either developed more sophisticated versions of the universal‐based account or simply converted to the trope‐based bundle theory. I argue that neither approach is fully satisfactory, for there is a different kind of symmetrical universe, or an alternative interpretation of such universes, that generates comparable challenges to these views. It turns out that neither universal‐based nor trope‐based accounts can fully accommodate symmetrical universes. In light of this, I propose a novel bundle‐theoretic account based on what David Lewis calls “amphibians”—qualities that are neither universals nor tropes—and argue that it accommodates both kinds of symmetrical universes without incorporating unwanted entities, making it preferable to the universal‐based and trope‐based accounts.

433. Symbolism and polarization in American public opinion: The case of border security
Simmons, B.A. & Pinson, L.E. (2026). Symbolism and polarization in American public opinion: The case of border security. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261486588
Abstract (original): We explore the effect of political symbols on mass attitudes and partisan identities under conditions of political polarization. Politicians are motivated to select visible, simple, and salient symbols to influence a target audience. Some of the most potent symbols may further polarize attitudes and affect. We test our expectations using the case of border security. Two decades of polls, national panel surveys, and original conjoint experiments in the United States suggest that border barriers have become increasingly partisan over time. A final survey experiment suggests a likely mechanism: exposure to a key symbol (here, a border wall) enhances strong Republican identifiers’ nationalist attitudes. Moreover, increasing the border wall’s salience bolsters ingroup feelings among moderate Republicans and reduces strong Republicans’ warmth for Democrats (the outgroup). Political symbols that divide respondents along salient identity lines contribute to polarization, sometimes creating sharp disagreements alongside consensus in American public opinion.

434. Diffusing norms, facing friction: International non-governmental organisations and the struggle of global governance efforts
Bidegain, G. & Odilla, F. (2026). Diffusing norms, facing friction: International non-governmental organisations and the struggle of global governance efforts. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261491641
Abstract (original): International non-governmental organisations rely on nationally embedded affiliates to advance global norms, yet relationships between headquarters and national chapters sometimes end in organisational rupture. Why do nationally embedded affiliates shift from voice to exit? This article addresses this question through a comparative study of Transparência Brasil and Uruguay Transparente, two former chapters of Transparency International (TI). Drawing on interviews, document analysis and a Most Different Systems Design, it shows that despite operating in markedly different contexts, both chapters followed a similar trajectory: persistent tensions over organisational priorities, modes of action and internal governance gradually eroded loyalty, while the absence of effective mechanisms to mediate these differences led to the disaffiliation of both chapters from TI’s network. These findings contribute to debates on the governance of international non-governmental organisations by showing how unresolved tensions between global coordination and local autonomy can lead nationally embedded affiliates to shift from voice to exit.

435. After the art monster: Imagining women's creativity otherwise
Alsop, E. (2026). After the art monster: Imagining women's creativity otherwise. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261484118
Abstract (original): This article examines two recently released films, Showing Up (Kelly Reichardt, 2023) and Anatomy of a Fall (Justine Triet, 2023), and argues that they exemplify a discourse of women’s creativity that departs markedly from the one that has been dominant in both mainstream cinema and much contemporary fiction, which has been beholden, at least since the publication of Jenny Offill's 2014 novel Dept. of Speculation , to the figure of the “art monster.” Rather than reify and repeat this trope—which I position as at once understandably popular and politically regressive—Reichardt and Triet model a different and more forcefully feminist vision of women’s artmaking on screen, one that presumes a far less contested relationship between creative work and caretaking, by representing the artist-protagonists of both films as successfully pursuing their work in the midst of and alongside domestic tasks. Through close analyses of both films, I illustrate the particular aesthetic strategies each uses to insist on the compatibility—rather than catastrophic incommensurability—of professional ambition and personal obligation. The result, I argue, is cinematic portraits of the artist that are less indebted to the ostensibly progressive figurations of millennial fiction and recent Hollywood film than the radical theorizations of earlier generations of feminist writers including Natalia Ginzburg, Audre Lorde, and Toni Morrison. Showing Up and Anatomy of a Fall , then, contribute to a feminist tradition of imagining otherwise, attending to the material conditions of their characters’ creative lives while refusing to insist on their determinism.

436. Reactionary aesthetics and the politics of dark femininity
Furlano, M. & Cowley, E. (2026). Reactionary aesthetics and the politics of dark femininity. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261492095
Abstract (original): In recent years, feminist politics have faced a renewed reactionary backlash. Drawing on Jilly Boyce Kay's articulation of the ‘femosphere’ and Asa Seresin's ‘heteropessimism’ as key theoretical frameworks, we explore how women in the femosphere advance reactionary politics by promoting bioessentialism, political fatalism, and neoliberal ideology. To do so, we explore the contours of ‘dark femininity,’ a digital feminine aesthetic blending femme-fatale imagery with advice promising women romantic and financial success if they ‘tap into’ their ‘dark side’ to attract a ‘high-value’ man. Using a critical thematic analysis of 1946 Instagram posts from a prominent (yet anonymous) dark feminine influencer, we investigate how this influencer participates in, and advances, gender regulation, heteropessimism, and neoliberal ideology in backlash times. We argue that the dark feminine influencer's aesthetics and discourse reveal mediated anger about heterosexual relationships but submit to the fatalistic belief, just like her manosphere counterparts, that heterosexual culture is beyond repair and must therefore be gamified and ‘beat.’ In closing, we propose hetero respair as an alternative social imaginary and political project that offers a caring, hopeful vision for heteroculture.

438. Does Moral Equality Imply a Duty to Vote?
Brennan, J. & Freiman, C. (2026). Does Moral Equality Imply a Duty to Vote?. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70136
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Sameer Bajaj and Thomas Christiano have provided a new case for a duty to vote grounded in moral equality, arguing that only voting promotes justice in a holistic way. Bajaj and Christiano contend that their argument overcomes what Jason Brennan has called the particularity problem – they allegedly show that voting is not merely one of many alternative ways to discharge some underlying moral duty, but rather that voting is special in some respect that makes it obligatory. We argue that Bajaj and Christiano have not established a duty to vote. We review various specifications of the duty to holistically promote justice and conclude that each of these specifications either yields implausible implications or allows that the duty can be met without voting. Lastly, their view does not adequately account for a moral division of labor, whereby some individuals specialize in activities other than voting and other individuals specialize in promoting political goods.

441. Shaping the future as we bet
Garasic, M.D. (2026). Shaping the future as we bet. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01432-5
Abstract (original): Abstract The rapid ascent of decentralized prediction platforms such as Polymarket signals more than a regulatory or financial controversy. This article argues that while prediction platforms represent a hyper-concentrated, automated case study, they instantiate a broader infrastructural condition of late technocapitalism: the hyperstitional collapse between representation and execution. Drawing on Nick Land’s accelerationist framework, the sociology of financial markets, and digital epistemology, we contend that prediction markets—much like conventional opinion polling, forward guidance, and algorithmic models—do not merely reflect probabilities but actively produce the futures they claim to forecast.
