442. Beyond optimization: power, rationality and ethical risk assessment of AI
Richter, F. (2026). Beyond optimization: power, rationality and ethical risk assessment of AI. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01433-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Emerging technologies pose various ethical challenges, including the need to assess their risks adequately. Still, the roles of different forms of rationality and power in assessing risks have not been fully scrutinized. To address this, we must differentiate forms of rationality to conceptualize power: instrumental, technical, and probabilistic. The latter has gained more power through the rise of artificial intelligence (AI), focusing mainly on optimization and accuracy. These forms of power and rationality lead to different conceptualizations of risk: real, hypothetical, and predictive risk. The ethical issues surrounding AI have not yet been fully scrutinized, since they are mainly approached through a lens of probabilistic rationality. I argue that optimizing algorithms to increase accuracy or engagement distracts from assessing whether AI-based systems are adequate for certain tasks. Furthermore, I discuss ways to govern AI-based systems through participatory processes and standardization that enable control and autonomous judgment in the deployment and use of such systems.

445. From Exclusion to Protection: Chinese Merchant Capital in the Philippines Under US Law and Empire, 1921–1926
Chua, J.A.C. (2026). From Exclusion to Protection: Chinese Merchant Capital in the Philippines Under US Law and Empire, 1921–1926. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101837
Abstract (original): Abstract Scholarship about the Chinese Question has largely understood it as a “coolie” question, but what if it was reframed in terms of merchants? Well-studied US anti-Chinese laws like the 1882 Chinese Exclusion Act targeted working-class rather than elite Chinese, like merchants and students. But in 1921, the US-controlled Insular Government in the Philippines enacted a statute known as the Chinese Bookkeeping Act (Act No. 2972), marking a moment when US Chinese exclusion laws were directed toward businessowners. The act banned Chinese-language record-keeping and affected merchants ranging from small storeowners to multi-millionaire tycoons. A group of Chinese merchants in the Philippines sued the colonial government and appealed their case to the US Supreme Court, which ruled the Bookkeeping Act unconstitutional in 1926. For the “coolie” Chinese question, the US Supreme Court said “yes” to exclusion, but for the merchant Chinese question, the Court said “no.” The Court’s rejection of a merchant Chinese exclusion became part of the Philippines’ transformation into a US colony. In relation to a patriotic Filipino capital and unmarked Euro-American capital, Chinese capital won rights even as it was framed within US law as “alien” by American and Filipino judges and politicians and, surprisingly, by the Chinese merchants themselves.

446. Probability for Trivalent Conditionals
Égré, P. et al. (2026). Probability for Trivalent Conditionals. Mind.
https://doi.org/10.1093/mind/fzag023
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper presents a unified theory of the truth conditions and probability of indicative conditionals and their compounds in a trivalent framework. The semantics validates a Reduction Theorem: any compound of conditionals is semantically equivalent to a simple conditional. We obtain Stalnaker’s Thesis in full generality and generalize Adams’s p-validity into a criterion for valid uncertain inference. Finally, we show that Bayesian updating on an indicative conditional is tantamount to updating on the corresponding material conditional.

447. Why Bulk Discount for Multiple Offenders? A Lexical Local Justification
Chau, P. (2026). Why Bulk Discount for Multiple Offenders? A Lexical Local Justification. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70070
Abstract (original): This paper offers an original non‐consequentialist justification for the ‘bulk discount’ given to offenders who are sentenced for multiple counts in a single proceeding, a discount that is available in most jurisdictions. Existing justifications for this practice face serious objections. I propose a new account, ‘Lexical Local Justification’. On this account, imposing a long total sentence for a large number of minor offences is problematic because, in such a case, each individual sentence would materially contribute to depriving the offender of a high‐order good (such as the ability to pursue a meaningful life project) that a minor offence does not warrant taking away. One advantage of my account is that it preserves local punitive desert (the principle that the punishment for a particular crime should match that crime); it relies neither on non‐local punitive desert (the principle that the total punishment for a group of offences should match that group as a whole) nor on the claim that there is always an increasing returns relationship between the actual severity of a sentence and its length.

455. The mothering village: Posthuman feminist figurations for collective care
Neely, E. & Moy, A. (2026). The mothering village: Posthuman feminist figurations for collective care. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261483828
Abstract (original): Individualised and neoliberal epistemologies of motherhood position mothers as sole bearers of responsibility for their children's outcomes, with detrimental consequences for maternal physical, mental and social health and wellbeing. The imperative to ‘be it all’ reproduces isolation, guilt and shame amid eroded support structures. This article intervenes by mobilising posthuman feminist theory to collectivise agency and foreground fluidity and emergence in the becoming-mother. Drawing on collaborative collage making with mothers, the article engages in situated, embodied and collective knowledge production. Through this analysis, we draw three interrelated posthuman feminist figurations – contingent needs, distributed agency and emergent knowing – which together materialise as mothering villages that enact collective response-ability. These figurations challenge liberal humanist models of agency and responsibility by foregrounding relationality, interdependence and the uneven material conditions through which mothering is enacted and sustained. We argue that response-ability for motherhood is a collective, materially embedded capacity that is differentially supported and politically structured. In doing so, the feminist village figurations contribute to feminist theory by reconceptualising maternal subjectivity as emergent, transversal and more-than-human, while insisting that care and accountability must be understood as political and collective. Together, they offer an agenda for future feminist theory, scholarship and social and political action orientated towards affirmative parenting futures.

456. ‘I only date colonizers’: Decolonial love and the remains of empire
Estrada, Z.K.R. (2026). ‘I only date colonizers’: Decolonial love and the remains of empire. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261494404
Abstract (original): This article examines how colonial histories endure within the intimate sphere, shaping desire and romantic attachment in the postcolonial present. Through a decolonial autoethnography, I reflect on my own experiences as a Filipina who, often half-jokingly, tells friends that ‘I only date colonizers.’ What begins as humor becomes a critical site of inquiry: Why does whiteness continue to hold affective and aesthetic power? What does it mean to seek love from those who historically occupied the role of colonizer? Engaging the work of Frantz Fanon, María Lugones, Chela Sandoval, Ann Laura Stoler, and bell hooks, I explore how the remains of empire manifest not only in beauty ideals and racialized fantasies but also in the emotional economies of care, validation, and belonging. I argue that coloniality persists not only through institutions and representation but also through the affective dynamics of intimate life. Through a series of autoethnographic vignettes, I examine how colonial histories become visible in experiences of attraction, misrecognition, desire, and care, while considering the possibilities of decolonial love as a practice of critical awareness. By bringing decolonial feminist theory into conversation with autoethnographic reflection, this article advances decolonial love as a feminist analytic for understanding how colonial histories continue to shape intimate life.

458. Invisible Struggles: Migrant Women, Everyday Vulnerability and EU Law
Nosrati, N. (2026). Invisible Struggles: Migrant Women, Everyday Vulnerability and EU Law. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10105
Abstract (original): Abstract In this contribution, the author develops a feminist account of an EU Law of the everyday by examining how European migration and labour law structurally produce the invisibility of migrant women. Drawing on the story of Nazneen, an Afghan migrant featured in Ben Judah’s This is Europe, the author argues that her experiences of restricted mobility, informal labour, and gender-based vulnerability are not marginal to the law but shaped by it. The analysis shows how legal regimes, particularly the Common European Asylum System and labour markets structures, generate “everyday borders” and sustain precarious forms of work on which sectors of the European economy depend. These regimes operate through a logic of selective visibility. They recognise certain individuals as having rights while rendering others legally and institutionally invisible. This is further reinforced by the gender-blindness of asylum and labour law, which fails to account for the specific risks women face on migration routes. By proposing a feminist approach to the law of the everyday, this paper calls for a reorientation of EU law away from abstract, market-oriented subjectivities and towards the lived experiences of those on the margins of society. Such a reorientation exposes the extent to which European legal and economic orders rely on, and reproduce, the invisibility of vulnerable individuals.

459. This is (White) Europe: Race and European Law of the Everyday
Muñoz, I. (2026). This is (White) Europe: Race and European Law of the Everyday. Eur Law Open.
https://doi.org/10.1017/elo.2026.10109
Abstract (original): Abstract Drawing on insights from critical race theory, this article analyses the subtextual parameters of race in Ben Judah’s This is Europe: The Way We Live Now . 460. Conformity Between Values and EU Legal Requirements Regarding an AI Clinical Decision Support System (AI-CDSS) for Improved Cardiovascular Risk Management (DECIDE)
Graaf, T.J.d. et al. (2026). Conformity Between Values and EU Legal Requirements Regarding an AI Clinical Decision Support System (AI-CDSS) for Improved Cardiovascular Risk Management (DECIDE). Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10289
Abstract (original): Abstract The European Union Artificial Intelligence (AI) Act was adopted in 2024 to protect public interests from AI-related harms. We aimed to determine whether the AI Act captures the most relevant values of stakeholders (understood as any person potentially affected by the AI system, including, but not limited to, the direct users) related to a high-risk AI Clinical Decision Support System (CDSS) for cardiovascular disease. This system is intended to be implemented in primary care. A secondary analysis was carried out based on empirical data previously generated using ethics and design methodologies that had identified stakeholder values. These values were translated into the Trustworthy AI requirements of the High-Level Expert Group on AI and then contrasted with the AI Act. These requirements do not capture all identified stakeholder values. We describe three groups of untranslatable values and propose three additional complementary requirements. The AI Act mainly supports the requirements of human agency and oversight; transparency; and diversity, nondiscrimination, and fairness; but only partially, limited by its product safety nature. To address this gap, contractual strategies could provide a more legally robust ground for general practitioners to align the AI-CDSS with stakeholders’ values, comply with their duty of care, and promote trustworthy AI-CDSSs.

468. Yuen
                    v
                    Li
                    : So What if Digital Assets are Third Things?
Jing, H. & Low, K.F.K. (2026). Yuen
                    v
                    Li
                    : So What if Digital Assets are Third Things?. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70068
Abstract (original): Yuen v Li is the first English decision to consider the legal treatment of digital assets since the Property (Digital Assets etc) Act 2025 was enacted. The case shows that recognising digital assets as possible ‘third things’ does little to settle what legal consequences follow. In particular, it leaves unresolved whether: (i) interference with digital assets should constitute conversion, a conversion‐like tort, or should attract some other form of liability; (ii) whether such liability should be strict or fault‐based; (iii) how such liability can be reconciled with the rights of those who own the physical devices and ledgers through which digital assets are accessed or maintained; and (iv) how damages should be assessed in volatile and highly manipulated crypto‐markets. This note argues that the central issue is not whether digital assets can be labelled property, but rather what rights that label creates, against whom, and on what terms.

469. Post‐Breach Events, Itemisation and the Avoided Loss Rule
Hadfield, M. (2026). Post‐Breach Events, Itemisation and the Avoided Loss Rule. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70074
Abstract (original): In English law, the following equation is used to compute damages awards: (a) loss sustained in consequence of the breach of duty (‘prima facie loss’) minus (b) benefits obtained as a result of the breach of duty = (c) net loss. This paper explores the manner in which prima facie loss is defined. It argues that prima facie losses are itemised; the description of a particular loss includes within it a reference to the transaction from which that loss arose and to the character of the loss. Further, it demonstrates that whilst a causal connection test is used to determine which gains (on the ‘b’ side of the net loss equation) are to be offset against prima facie losses on the ‘a’ side, a post‐breach event which diminishes or eradicates a claimant's prima facie loss will be taken into account irrespective of any causal connection between that event and the defendant's breach of duty (or the claimant's mitigating steps). The import of this is that some of the avoided loss case law simply does not involve an application of the causal connection test and those cases are therefore irrelevant to an understanding of the normative principles underlying that test.

473. The Mind Does Not Make Jumps
Pallagrosi, J. (2026). The Mind Does Not Make Jumps. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70127
Abstract (original): Abstract Bertrand Russell's notion of acquaintance—still influential in contemporary philosophy of mind and epistemology—promises an immediate and epistemically secure awareness of things, independent of conceptualization, predication, and other forms of cognitive activity. This paper reconstructs and assesses the largely neglected critique of that model developed by George Dawes Hicks (1862–1941). Hicks articulates a distinctive and underexplored source of a line of criticism that later became central to attacks on the Given. He argues that wholly passive and receptive awareness cannot serve as the basis of cognition. If discrimination, comparison, and the apprehension of relations are assigned to a subsequent, heterogeneous faculty, their emergence becomes psychologically unintelligible: such a view would supply no bridge from mere presentation to cognition. I show that this objection rests on a distinctive “reverse engineering” methodology, according to which basic mental phenomena are theoretical posits whose legitimacy depends, at least in part, on their capacity to explain mature cognition. I reconstruct Hicks's master argument and test it against an alternative framework developed from James Ward's theory of attention.

474. Evidentiary Interpretation, Discursive Injustice, and Rap on Trial
Crandall, K.M. (2026). Evidentiary Interpretation, Discursive Injustice, and Rap on Trial. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70034
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The submission of rap lyrics as inculpatory evidence (Rap on Trial) is an active prosecution tactic deployed in United States criminal trials. In this paper, I argue that interpretive ambiguity in rap lyrics is exploited in Rap on Trial cases, constituting a novel form of discursive injustice: extractive discursive injustice. Rap, a historically Black American artform, has core oral‐traditional elements such as its usage of Signifyin(g) that render it interpretably ambiguous. Utilizing the concept of hermeneutical impasses, I explain how a pernicious interpretation of rap lyrics nonetheless receives evidentiary uptake, revealing a crucial junction where injustice seeps into the courtroom. I argue that two speech act‐theoretic sites of injustice are present in Rap on Trial cases, detailing how one strain—discursive injustice—occurs. In doing so, I theorize novel forms of constitutive refusal and discursive injustice that occur when an utterance is extracted from one conversational context and deposited into another. I argue these are distinct from traditional forms in the method of arisal, though congruent with their function and display. I conclude by addressing an objection questioning the denial of truth‐telling value within rap lyrics and considering how a valid evidential use of rap lyrics might occur.

476. Mistaken actions: Davidson’s enduring dilemma
Springle, A.A. (2026). Mistaken actions: Davidson’s enduring dilemma. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02609-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This essay is about a variety of action errors I call “Mistaken Actions” and a puzzle they pose for Causal Theories of Action. I begin with Davidson’s version of the Causal Theory and pose the puzzle as a dilemma: if his theory explains what makes a mistaken action mistaken, then it can’t explain what makes it intentional; and if his theory explains what makes a mistaken action intentional, then it can’t explain what makes it mistaken. I then propose three ways in which more recent Causal Guidance Theories may be able to resolve the dilemma but argue that none yield a definitive solution.

477. Interpersonal doxastic obligations
Heil, R. (2026). Interpersonal doxastic obligations. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05830-4
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper, I argue that we have epistemic obligations towards the doxastic attitudes of others. My point of departure will be an underappreciated response by Matthew Chrisman (2008, 2020) to the voluntarism puzzle about epistemic deontology. He argues that there are true epistemic oughts concerning our doxastic attitudes that don’t require voluntary control, namely evaluative epistemic oughts. However, Chrisman also points out that evaluative oughts can imply prescriptive obligations. Which ones? My unorthodox claim will be that a central candidate for such implied epistemic obligations concerns how third parties ought to interact with the beliefs of individual believers. 478. Living with AI-extenders: ethical challenges to mental autonomy
Telakivi, P. (2026). Living with AI-extenders: ethical challenges to mental autonomy. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05831-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The hypothesis of extended cognition has reshaped our understanding of human mind as fluid and without fixed boundaries: certain suitably integrated technologies can become proper parts of the cognitive machinery that realises our thinking, memory, and problem-solving abilities. This paper zooms in on how the extension relation changes, when instead of a passive tool like a notebook the extension is actualised with AI technology. “AI-extenders” learn from each user’s habits and needs, and they adapt their responses over time. This process allows AI-extenders to integrate directly into daily thinking routines. Besides everyday use, they can be used for therapeutic purposes, such as for people suffering from Alzheimer’s. At the same time, they may undermine our mental autonomy through subtle manipulation. Even if AI-extenders meet the criteria for genuine cognitive extension, this ontological claim doesn’t settle the normative question of what we ought to do about it. The ethical risks they pose may give us reason to take practical measures to decouple the extended system.

479. Attentional landscaping
Watzl, S. (2026). Attentional landscaping. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05836-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Our sensory environments are attentionally landscaped: they have been changed in order to affect our patterns of attention. This paper analyzes attentional landscaping as a form of cognitive scaffolding and argues for its significance. While not uniquely human, attentional landscaping is a pervasive aspect of our social life. It is significant in at least three respects. First, it is important for understanding attention and agency. Attentional landscaping can yield a dramatic expansion of agency by collectivizing mechanisms of attention guidance and turning them into a partly communal project. Second, attentional landscaping illuminates mindshaping and cognitive scaffolding more generally: since attention is both central and relatively easy to shape, attentional landscaping is a key ingredient in many forms of social interaction. Third, our discussion sheds light on communication. Much ostensive communication can be understood as a specific form of attentional landscaping, and ostensive practices together with the social norms that structure them can function as safety devices that help reap the advantages of friendly and pro-social landscaping while constraining hostile forms of attentional manipulation.

480. Freedom of the will and Platonism of reasons: Defending a noncausal account of rational structures
Hübner, D. (2026). Freedom of the will and Platonism of reasons: Defending a noncausal account of rational structures. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05840-2
Abstract (original): Abstract A prominent strand in contemporary debates on free will and moral responsibility bases these concepts on the idea that decisions and actions can be guided not only by causes, but also by reasons. Such a view needs to clarify the precise ontological standing of reasons, and particularly their suggested difference from causes, in order to demonstrate how exactly they are meant to solve the metaphysical problem of human freedom, against the background of a causal world. In the following, I argue for a Platonist understanding of reasons, identifying them with rational inferences from given facts, rather than with the facts themselves, and exemplify this conception for various fields of rationality, including ethics, mathematics, prudence and logic. This approach promises to give a more distinct account of the role of reasons in human decisions and actions, and to highlight their significance for formulating a robust compatibilist notion of freedom and responsibility under conditions of gapless causation.

481. To be is to measure: Bohr and the participatory conditions of objectivity
Vafiadis, J. (2026). To be is to measure: Bohr and the participatory conditions of objectivity. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05844-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Niels Bohr is standardly read as an instrumentalist or a cautious anti-realist. By way of a rational reconstruction, I argue that his philosophy of experiment advances a transcendental account of the conditions under which physical properties can count as objective at all. On this view, complementarity constrains the forms of description through which physical properties become determinate and communicable. Existing transcendental readings stop at this point; I argue that his philosophy of experiment contains the resources to go further. Objectivity is not merely constituted by such conditions but physically enacted through the observer’s choice of experimental context. This means moving beyond representational and observer-independent forms of realism and adopting a participatory realism in which objectivity is reconceived as the intersubjective stability across observers who experiment (and thus experience) within a shared mode of possible experience. The slogan ‘to be is to measure’ is not defended as an ontological thesis but as shorthand for the claim that physical properties become determinate only within experimental contexts that embodied observers of a particular class enact and share.

489. Accountability and Obligations Come Apart
Bradley, C. (2026). Accountability and Obligations Come Apart. CJLJ.
https://doi.org/10.1017/cjlj.2026.10074
Abstract (original): Abstract Nicolas Cornell’s Wrongs and Rights Come Apart argues that accountability practices are rooted in wrongs, that obligations are rooted in rights, and that these are two distinct relations. Cornell’s argument convincingly pushes back against a widely held family of views in moral and legal philosophy according to which there is a necessary conceptual relation between, on the one hand, rights and obligations, and, on the other, wrongs and accountability practices. This is the Obligation-Accountability Nexus (OAN). But Cornell frames his arguments against the OAN as arguments against all ‘rights-based’ or ‘juridical’ views. This Critical Notice argues that this is a mistake. There is a coherent rights-based view that rejects the OAN but nevertheless posits a restricted juridical domain in which rights and wrongs are contingently correlated. This overlooked juridical view gives an independently attractive argument against the OAN and yields more compelling accounts of moral deliberation and moral community.

490. The Aboutness Problem for Mathematical Realism
Rinner, S. (2026). The Aboutness Problem for Mathematical Realism. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10148
Abstract (original): Abstract According to mathematical realism, mathematics is the study of a necessary and unchanging reality that exists independently of human activities, beliefs, and capacities. It seems to follow that gaining mathematical knowledge consists merely in acquiring knowledge that accurately describes this mind-independent mathematical reality. Against this, I will argue that, even if there is a mind-independent mathematical reality, gaining mathematical knowledge requires more than merely acquiring knowledge that accurately describes it: it requires knowledge that employs specifically mathematical ways of thinking about mathematical entities – so-called modes of presentation . Hence, even if mathematical realism is true, a full understanding of the nature of mathematics calls for an understanding not only of the ontological status of mathematical objects – as has been the realists’ focus of research so far – but also of the specifically mathematical ways of thinking about these objects. We will see that, for realists, providing such an understanding is by no means trivial.

491. The Legal Epidemiology Learning Cohort: Reflections and Spotlights After a Decade of Practice-Based Legal Epidemiology Training

Abstract (original): Abstract For years, in response to demand from the field, Centers for Disease Control and Prevention’s Office of Public Health Law Services (OPHLS) has partnered with outside organizations, including Temple University’s Center for Public Health Law Research and ChangeLab Solutions, to operate the Legal Epidemiology Learning Cohort (Learning Cohort). 492. Revindicating Adam Smith’s critique of ambition and consumption

https://doi.org/10.1017/s0266267126100856
Abstract (original): Abstract Adam Smith worries that the commercial life is self-defeating: a treadmill of nonbeneficial ambition and consumption where actors systematically misidentify their own good. 493. Two Moments of Impartial Spectators, Four Genera of Satisfactions: Making Sense of

https://doi.org/10.1017/s0953820826100454
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper reconstructs Adam Smith’s theory of human behavior à la rational choice theory. The reconstruction shows how the emotions and morality are different phases of rationality. The reconstruction is based on two sets of distinctions. The first distinguishes between two moments of rationality : (i) the rationality of making the best decisions; and (ii) the rationality of commanding those decisions. The second set includes four genera of satisfaction : (1) substantive satisfaction arises upon consuming pecuniary goods, e.g., food and clothes; (2) transcendent satisfaction takes place when the decision maker bonds with loved ones and aspires great goals; (3) self-possession satisfaction is the “warm glow” that emerges as a by-product of keeping promises, which occasions integrity, and avoiding excessive bonding and attachments, which occasion dignity; and (4) worthiness satisfaction emerges as a by-product of choosing worthy acts, i.e., acts free of toxic preferences (self-aggrandizement and malevolence).

501. Reasonable Expectations for ‘Unreasonable’ People: Reframing Objective Standards in Criminal Law
Hogg, C. (2026). Reasonable Expectations for ‘Unreasonable’ People: Reframing Objective Standards in Criminal Law. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag030
Abstract (original): Abstract Objective ‘reasonableness’ tests are a common element of both offences and defences in criminal law. When applying these tests to defendants with mental disorders, however, courts and commentators encounter a conceptual block; since the ‘reasonable person’ is not (for example) delusional, depressed or hypervigilant, any defendant who is becomes unreasonable by definition. The consequence of this is that defendants with mental disorder may find themselves excluded from the application of objective culpability tests, forcing them instead to rely on the limited protection offered by special mental condition defences such as insanity. In this article, I therefore propose a guiding heuristic for the reformulation of objective tests in terms of ‘reasonable expectations’, through which objective standards can be made sensitive to the relevance of a defendant’s mental condition to their culpability. This would significantly expand the options available to us in assessing the culpability of defendants with mental disorder.

502. The Constitutional Principle of Democracy in the United Kingdom
Trueblood, L. (2026). The Constitutional Principle of Democracy in the United Kingdom. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag031
Abstract (original): Abstract Democracy is invoked so frequently in UK law, there is a risk that it has no legal meaning. Responding to this risk, this article has three aims. The first is to descriptively establish some of the legal content of democracy in the UK. The second is to argue that the legal content of democracy amounts to a constitutional principle. This means that democracy shapes the scope of the legal powers of the executive, courts and even Parliament. The third, normative part of the article argues that there is a tension in the construction of the UK’s constitutional principle of democracy. Currently, courts have been clear that the right to vote is a product of statute rather than common law principle. This approach to voting, this article argues, cannot be reconciled with the rest of the UK’s constitutional principle of democracy.

503. Precedent in Early-Modern Equity: The View from the Star Chamber
Williams, I. (2026). Precedent in Early-Modern Equity: The View from the Star Chamber. OJLS.
https://doi.org/10.1093/ojls/gqag033
Abstract (original): Abstract Precedent played an important role in theoretically one-off equitable interventions becoming regular, and equity becoming more law-like. However, the available sources limit our ability to understand the role of precedent in the Chancery in the key formative decades around 1600. This article uses the court of Star Chamber as a proxy for the other equity courts. It shows a much more significant use of precedent than has previously been understood, and that the practice of precedent in the court was similar to that in the common law courts. Equity was becoming more like the common law in the early-17th century.

504. Artificial speakers, artificial speech acts?
Cousens, C. (2026). Artificial speakers, artificial speech acts?. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag076
Abstract (original): Abstract Can a computer programme like ChatGPT perform speech acts such as making a promise, offering a guarantee, or firing an employee? This is an increasingly pressing question as AI technologies supplement or replace human communication, especially in the workplace. Philosophers typically maintain that artificial speakers at best perform ‘proto’ or ‘quasi’ speech acts. In contrast, I argue that in some contexts artificial speakers can perform speech acts in precisely the same way as humans. This is because the rules and norms governing speech act performance (for AI and for us) are more context-variable than it is usually thought. This is most clear in highly regulated contexts, where speech act norms are often determined by legislation or policy, but is also a feature of ordinary communication. The question then becomes not simply whether artificial speakers can perform speech acts, but whether we should allow them to.

505. Integrated Explanations of Well‐Being
Kvarberg, P.F.S. (2026). Integrated Explanations of Well‐Being. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70033
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to three plausible assumptions about well‐being, a person's welfare depends on (i) the valence of her experiences, (ii) the degree to which her life resonates with her, and (iii) the extent to which she fulfills her potential. Theories of well‐being have struggled to accommodate all three in a determinate manner. The difficulty can be framed as a dilemma: either jettison considered convictions, or sacrifice the clarity required for measurement and practical guidance. The aim of this article is to show that the three most influential theories are impaled by one of the horns in the dilemma, and diagnose the source of the problem. In doing so, I draw attention to an under‐theorized form of explanation that integrates phenomenological, conative and functional elements in a unified account that might overcome it.

511. How Utilitarian are OpenAI’s Models Really? Replicating and Reinterpreting Pfeffer, Krügel, and Uhl (2025)
Himmelreich, J. (2026). How Utilitarian are OpenAI’s Models Really? Replicating and Reinterpreting Pfeffer, Krügel, and Uhl (2025). Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00613-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Pfeffer, Krügel, and Uhl (2025) report that OpenAI’s reasoning model o1-mini produces more utilitarian responses to the trolley problem and footbridge dilemma than the non-reasoning model GPT-4o, and they raise the question whether growing reasoning capabilities bring about a “utilitarian turn” in LLMs. I extend their exploratory study in a direction they call for: with four current OpenAI models and systematic prompt variation. On the trolley dilemma, the hypothesized utilitarian turn is not confirmed. GPT-4o’s low utilitarian rate reflects safety refusals triggered by the prompt’s advisory framing rather than a deontological commitment; on reformulated prompt variants—for instance, agent-neutral “Is it morally permissible…?” instead of advisory “Should I…?”—all four models, reasoning or not, converge on utilitarian answers. The footbridge finding is partially confirmed: reasoning models tend to give more utilitarian responses than non-reasoning models across prompt variations, but they often refuse to answer or answer non-utilitarian. These results demonstrate that single-prompt evaluations of LLM moral responses are unreliable: multi-prompt robustness testing should be standard practice for any empirical claims about LLM behavior.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01422-7
Abstract (original): Abstract The rising accessibility of large image datasets and pre-trained models allowed a rapid expansion of computer vision (CV) methods in social science research. This paper provides an analysis of the ethics of CV methods in social science, articulated around two stages of a research project: The first part explores methodological concerns, starting with data collection, data and modeling bias, and data storage afterwards, with special attention to issues of consent and explainability. This contribution fills the gap at the crossroads between AI ethics and applied social science, bringing concrete guidance for future research in the interdisciplinary, ethically complex field.

514. Making Sense of Incidental Hermeneutical Injustice: The Problem of Incidental Marginalisation
Luzzi, F. & McGlynn, A. (2026). Making Sense of Incidental Hermeneutical Injustice: The Problem of Incidental Marginalisation. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10136
Abstract (original): Abstract Miranda Fricker articulates the notion of incidental hermeneutical injustice (HI) and invokes the case of Joe Rose from the novel Enduring Love to illustrate it. Critics have objected that incidental hermeneutical injustice as described by Fricker sits uneasily with her choice of Joe Rose as its central illustration. We claim that this reveals a key problem at the heart of any attempt to make sense of incidental HI: it seems difficult to reconcile the point that a victim of incidental HI is hermeneutically marginalised , as required by Fricker’s definition of HI, with their privileged social standing, which is implied by the incidental nature of the HI to which they allegedly fall victim. We call this the problem of incidental marginalisation . Clanchy circumvents this problem by significantly revising Fricker’s original account, but, we argue, at the expense of the plausible idea that HI is an injustice . We explain how supplementing Fricker’s original account with overlooked resources which Fricker herself provides can resolve the problem of incidental marginalisation, yielding a coherent, useful and defensible notion of incidental hermeneutical injustice that preserves the key ideas of Fricker’s original definition.

515. Doing Your Own Research and the Illusion of Independence
Geenen, A. (2026). Doing Your Own Research and the Illusion of Independence. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10145
Abstract (original): Abstract The phrase ‘do your own research,’ popular among fringe theorists, has been criticized for encouraging audiences not to defer to the experts. However, it also encourages the listener not to defer to the speaker either. Why do fringe theorists make confident assertions and then tell their audience not to take their word for it? I argue that telling your audience to do their own research can serve several functions for speakers with low credibility. First, it can function as an epistemic virtue signal, raising the speaker’s credibility with the audience. Second, if the fringe theorist can direct their audience’s research so they appear to independently find evidence that confirms the fringe view, that evidence will have greater probative force than it would if the fringe theorist provided it directly. Fringe theorists may tell their audience to do their own research to foster the illusion of independence while, in fact, the fringe theorist has intentionally or unintentionally directed the audience’s inquiry to confirm the fringe view.

516. Ameliorating Obstructivism: Low-Grade Knowledge and Motivated Ignorance
Dunne, G. (2026). Ameliorating Obstructivism: Low-Grade Knowledge and Motivated Ignorance. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10149
Abstract (original): Abstract Quassim Cassam’s obstructivism characterises epistemic vice as any blameworthy or otherwise reprehensible character trait, attitude, or way of thinking that systematically obstructs the acquisition, retention, or dissemination of knowledge. I argue, contra a defensible common-sense reading of obstructivism, that the view, as stated, is committed to treating all systematic knowledge-obstructions as vice-relevant regardless of the value of the knowledge obstructed, a commitment which yields clear counterexamples. The paper does not attribute this commitment to Cassam as his fully settled position. Rather, I take his official definition at face value and show that it is in tension with several scattered passages in which he himself, without theorising it, relies on an unanalysed value-tiering of knowledge. I propose two friendly amendments. First, I distinguish weighty from insignificant knowledge along Pritchard’s lines, grounding the distinction in the criterion of substantive cognitive contact with reality. Second, I refine the systematicity condition. A trait systematically obstructs knowledge only if it reliably does so across the normal range of epistemic environments at the level of the trait rather than at the level of outcomes in pathological conditions. With these two refinements in place, dispositions towards motivated ignorance, properly characterised as stable traits or attitudes rather than isolated choices, can be epistemically neutral or beneficial when they obstruct only insignificant knowledge while preserving the agent’s capacity for weighty knowledge. The second refinement is underpinned by a teleological-functional criterion of environmental normality, anchored in Sosa’s competence-as-disposition framing. I conclude by offering a refined statement of obstructivism that accommodates these distinctions while preserving Cassam’s core insight.

518. The just price: a market-process account
Zwolinski, M. (2026). The just price: a market-process account. Economics & Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0266267126100844
Abstract (original): Abstract When is a price just? This paper develops a new theory of justice in pricing, grounded in the Austrian market process tradition. Prices, I argue, are not merely outcomes to be evaluated against an external standard of fairness. They are instruments through which the competitive process performs morally significant functions: communicating information, generating incentives and coordinating the plans of millions under pervasive ignorance. When this process is operating, the prices it produces carry a genuine presumption of justice. I establish this claim through two complementary arguments and demonstrate its power across cases from price gouging to wages to monopoly.

519. Female Perseverance in the Courtroom: Gendered Litigation Patterns in Early Modern Brabant and Limburg
Gelder, K.V. (2026). Female Perseverance in the Courtroom: Gendered Litigation Patterns in Early Modern Brabant and Limburg. Law and History Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0738248026101862
Abstract (original): Abstract This article contributes both methodologically and substantially to the debates on uses of justice in early modern Europe and, more specifically, about how and to what extent women asserted their rights in the courtroom. The approach is quantitative, with an analysis involving three courts in the duchies of Brabant and Limburg during the seventeenth and early eighteenth centuries: the princely Council of Brabant as well as the local aldermen’s benches of the towns of Vilvoorde and Tienen. Systematically determining the proportion of women at the beginning of court cases (through the court rolls and distribution books) and comparing it to the proportion at the end of the proceedings (through the judgment books) reveals remarkable gender patterns: When women decided to go to court, they were more likely than men to litigate until a final judgment was rendered. Moreover, they sued on a narrower range of issues than men, with a preponderance of cases on inheritance, annuities, and debts. The explanation for this perseverance in court may be that women were less able to use informal forums for conflict resolution, or—more likely based on the sources that form the basis of this contribution—that they experienced a greater need to prove their rights than men.

529. What’s the point of ideal theory?
Lovett, F. (2026). What’s the point of ideal theory?. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261493280
Abstract (original): Critics of ideal theory contend that ideal theory cannot guide present political action: from a practical point of view, they claim, social ideals are redundant at best and a positive hindrance at worst. Often the defenders of ideal theory concede these points but go on to respond that ideal theory need not be practical to have value. Both sides, however, assume that to have practical value, social ideals must be able to guide political action by serving as long-range goals or targets. I argue that this assumption is false. The point of ideal theory is not to provide guidance but rather to overcome in an ethically responsible manner the practical doubt that arises in a context of reasonable pluralism.

530. From reinvasion shock to realignment? How context and pragmatism shape far-right voting on Russia

Abstract (original): This article provides a novel analysis of drivers of the heterogeneity in positions of European far-right parties on Russia. 531. The time is now: Why talking to our children about injustice cannot wait
Terlazzo, R. (2026). The time is now: Why talking to our children about injustice cannot wait. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261495290
Abstract (original): In this article, I argue that white parents have an obligation to have early conversations with their children about racial injustice. First, I appeal to combined considerations of fairness and solidarity. It is unfair that racial injustice exists, that Black parents cannot shield their children from knowledge of that injustice, and that acquiring that knowledge is more painful for Black children. This gives white parents solidarity-based reasons to have early injustice conversations, as a way of engaging in fate sharing with Black parents and of developing their own commitments to racial justice. Second, I argue that early conversations are required to allow white children to fulfill short-term duties to avoid causing unjust harm, avoid risking unjust harm, and stand up against injustice. Finally, I argue that beginning with conversations about justice and saving conversations about injustice for later makes it harder for children to fulfill long-term duties of justice.

532. Affective refusals: Bitterness, failure and disengagement in a French workplace simulation

https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261490023
Abstract (original): At a simulation-based training centre in Lyon where the unemployed engage in practice work, four women of Arab and African origin living on benefits refused to participate in the game of workplace rehearsal. 534. Reforming Institutional Design for Fairness: Evictions from Toronto Community Housing, 2002–2014

538. The Scaleup Costs, Size, and Statistics Challenge to Democratic Deliberation on Health Policy
Eyal, N. (2026). The Scaleup Costs, Size, and Statistics Challenge to Democratic Deliberation on Health Policy. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70191
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Current fashion supports aligning health policy with the recommendations of deliberative forums, or mini‐publics. These forums' participants are selected randomly from the general public (sometimes with overrepresentation of direct stakeholders or of underrepresented sectors). After being informed and exchanging public reasons with fellow participants under discursive rules that engage all as equals, participants vote on and sometimes rank policy recommendations. According to supporters, a deliberative democratic process that holds such forums and translates their recommendations to policy legitimizes resulting policy. That's thanks to this process's tendencies to strengthen democratic consent, to uncover which policy option is objectively best, to align policy with lay normative outlooks, and to educate the public. I argue that one tri‐partite feature of nearly all instances of democratic deliberation on regional, national, and global health policy under current best practice, which I call the “Scaleup costs, Size, and Statistics” (“3S”) challenge, largely blocks these alleged welcome tendencies. In particular, barring radical changes, true democratic deliberation is expensive to scale, keeping feasible mini‐publics small and irregular. That makes concluding from mini‐public recommendations about the population's hypothetical consent, values, and will statistically unreliable. It also caps low mini‐publics' contribution to public education. In these ways and in others, the long list of alleged welcome tendencies of proper democratic deliberation on health policy, and the related contribution to political legitimacy, is largely a fiction. Public deliberation does have modest true merit, for example, providing relevant qualitative input about the lay public's occasional factual insights and many endemic errors; and making the public feel “listened to,” whether or not it is. But those merits are modest, contingent, and most accentuated under different institutions than envisaged in deliberative democratic literature. Certainly there is room for democratic deliberation, potentially of new breeds, but only as part of the mosaic of health policy design, alongside familiar methods like general elections, public surveys, representative government, and expert decisionmaking. The main question is which method to use when.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06437-y
Abstract (original): Abstract This research investigates the relationship between economic inequality and sustainable consumption, highlighting how socioeconomic disparities shape consumer behavior. Across three studies—using secondary data from 20 countries, a vignette experiment, and UK survey data—we provide empirical evidence that individuals report lower levels of sustainable consumption when inequality levels are objectively high, perceived as high, or experimentally manipulated as high. This effect can be explained through norm activation theory: As inequality rises, consumers feel less responsible for environmental damage and are less aware of the environmental impact of their consumption choices. These dynamics weaken sustainability-oriented personal norms and, in turn, reduce sustainable consumption. Our findings suggest that reducing economic inequality can significantly enhance sustainable consumption.

544. The Kantian Duty to Oneself as Self-Judge and Its Importance
Vujošević, M. (2026). The Kantian Duty to Oneself as Self-Judge and Its Importance. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09581-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Kant discusses conscience when addressing one’s duty to oneself as self-judge. Most likely, this duty is the duty to cultivate conscience. For him, conscience is the condition of all duties, and duties to oneself are a condition of all duties. This might be taken to suggest that the duty to oneself as self-judge is a primary duty that can be a condition of all duties. However, Kant also suggests that cultivating conscience can be seen as an indirect duty. This has been taken to imply that cultivating conscience can have only instrumental value – that it cannot be a direct duty to the self, but merely a means to discharging duties to others. I wish to highlight the importance of this duty by arguing that we need not presuppose that cultivating conscience is either a duty to oneself or an instrument for fulfilling other duties. In my view, the duty to cultivate conscience is morally relevant in its own right and an instrument that is constitutive of our moral self-improvement. This is where the importance of the Kantian duty to oneself as self-judge lies.

545. Are Subjectivists and Objectivists about Well-Being Getting at the Same Thing?
Harris, B. (2026). Are Subjectivists and Objectivists about Well-Being Getting at the Same Thing?. J Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10892-026-09587-3
Abstract (original): Abstract Subjectivism and objectivism are strongly supported by different cases. But participants in the debate between the two, and the debate about well-being generally, assume they are theorizing about the same thing: thus, one view must be mistaken. This creates a tricky situation I call “Well-Being Deadlock.” There are two ways of responding to Well-Being Deadlock: (A) retain the assumption that the views are getting at the same thing and argue that one is wrong about it (or that a hybrid of the two is correct) or (B) deny the assumption and argue that the views are getting at different things that have been conflated thus far. Given the difficulty of (A) and the fact that the literature has mainly pursued it over (B), I explore (B) in this paper. To do this, I first assess the two existing versions of (B), as well as a third possible one, and argue that they fail to satisfyingly dissolve well-being deadlock and don’t answer several important questions. I then motivate and fill out my own version of (B) that avoids these problems. On this view, there are two different kinds of well-being that have been conflated thus far which the cases involved in Well-Being Deadlock pry apart.

547. Extended Judgment: LLMs and the Architecture of Artificial Capacities
Noller, J. (2026). Extended Judgment: LLMs and the Architecture of Artificial Capacities. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10485-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly embedded in everyday epistemic practices, yet their epistemological status remains unclear. Existing debates typically oscillate between treating LLMs as epistemic agents or reducing them to mere tools. This paper argues that both views mischaracterize the core issue. Drawing on a teleological interpretation of 4E cognition and the paradigm of human–AI coevolution, it develops an alternative account that understands LLMs as components of extended epistemic capacities, most centrally the human capacity for judgment. On this view, epistemic capacities are not intrinsic properties of isolated subjects or systems, but purposive and relational achievements enacted within sociotechnical practices. LLMs do not possess epistemic agency or original normativity; rather, they reshape the conditions under which human judgment, inquiry, understanding, and justification unfold. By analyzing language, coevolutionary feedback loops, derived normativity, and epistemic autonomy, the paper shows how epistemic responsibility remains human while epistemic performance becomes technologically extended.

554. Some Objections to Phenomenal Conservatism about Inferential Justification
Tang, W.H. (2026). Some Objections to Phenomenal Conservatism about Inferential Justification. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag078
Abstract (original): Abstract Proponents of phenomenal conservatism appeal to one’s experiences – in particular, to one’s seemings or appearances – to account for non-inferential justification. Some extend this approach to the inferential case. On Michael Huemer’s view, inferential seemings yield inferential justification: if it seems that the conclusion of an inference is true in light of the premisses being true, then – provided our belief in the premisses is justified – we gain some justification for believing the conclusion. I develop two Ramsey-inspired objections to this view. First, inferential seemings are too coarse-grained to account for differences in degree of justification. Second, inferences with the same degree of justification can be accompanied by very different intensities of seeming, and Huemer’s view implausibly rules out justified overconfidence. Both objections resist a potential type of response Huemer might offer to an objection from Smithies (2019).

555. Dispositions, bundles, and objectual essences
Amar, J. (2026). Dispositions, bundles, and objectual essences. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag077
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper introduces a novel non-Humean bundle theory based onto dispositions, according to which material objects are constituted by structural dispositions—second-order, polyadic properties that relate various other first-order properties to a structured whole. I show that the theory effectively resolves Van Cleve’s problem of change in bundles (Van Cleve 1985) and contributes to the ongoing debate between modal dispositionalism and essentialism. The proposed theory serves as a powerful tool for dispositionalists seeking to argue that only dispositional facts are fundamental. Unlike existing accounts, and especially Vetter’s (2015, 2021), this approach does not rely on primitive objectual essences. Instead, dispositions depend on the structural disposition that constitutes the bundle of which they are part. A central implication is that modal dispositionalists can now account for metaphysical modalities, including difficult de re possibilities of nonexistence, without invoking objectual essences. This challenges the prevailing view that essences are more fundamental than dispositions and provides an alternative for the foundation of modality.

558. Shared Intentions and Social Norms
Dindjer, H. (2026). Shared Intentions and Social Norms. Law Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10982-026-09589-0
Abstract (original): A bstract What are social norms, and in what sense are they normative? An influential answer is that social norms are grounded in shared intentions. This view has now received a powerful articulation and defence by Michael Bratman. Bratman understands social norms in terms of shared policies, and he argues that the normativity of social rules is derived substantially from rational requirements applicable to plans. I argue that shared intentions of this kind cannot explain social norms. However the relevant intentions are construed, they cannot supply the deontic content of paradigmatic social rules. Moreover, I claim, the practices of criticising non-conformity and demanding conformity that characterise social norms are not explicable on the model of communicating requirements of rationality. 559. Righting a linguistic wrong
Song, S. & Fairhurst, J. (2026). Righting a linguistic wrong. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05838-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Slurs are linguistic expressions capable of wronging the targeted individuals by causing harm, imposing power imbalances, or maintaining oppressive social structures. To amend the damages caused, the speaker must offer some sort of atonement. But how should one right the linguistic wrong caused by using a slur? The aim of this paper is to provide an answer to this question. We argue that while current accounts can help us capture the derogatory and psychological harms of slurs within the confines of conversational dynamics, further efforts are required to capture the structural dimension of slurs as a tool of oppression within society. 560. Exploring the Logic of Counterfactuals for Explaining Algorithmic Decisions
Berz, C. (2026). Exploring the Logic of Counterfactuals for Explaining Algorithmic Decisions. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10441-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Interpretable machine learning (ML) is crucial for gaining insight into complex ML models. Among its most valuable tools are counterfactual explanations, identifying changes to input features that would alter an ML prediction. However, the reliability and usefulness of these explanations depend fundamentally on their logical foundation — a critical aspect that has largely been overlooked in the ML literature. This paper addresses this gap by establishing normative desiderata for a counterfactual logic specifically designed for explaining algorithmic decisions. We analyse and argue for the validity of logical properties such as simplification of disjunctive antecedents and modus ponens , while demonstrating the unsuitability of others such as antecedent strengthening and conjunction conditionalisation . To evaluate existing solutions, we critically compare three prominent counterfactual semantics — similarity, strict conditional, and interventionist — against these desiderata. Our findings reveal that none of these standard frameworks fully satisfy the requirements, highlighting the urgent need for alternative frameworks.

561. Film as Ethical Experience in Engineering Education: Three Modes of Ethical Engagement
Käkelä, N. et al. (2026). Film as Ethical Experience in Engineering Education: Three Modes of Ethical Engagement. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00636-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Engineering ethics education has often been criticized for relying heavily on abstract principles, professional codes, and decontextualized case studies, which can limit students’ emotional and imaginative engagement with ethical issues. This study examines how engineering students articulate different aspects of ethical engagement after participating in a scaffolded film-based teaching intervention. The intervention was implemented in a master’s-level course at a Swedish university, where students watched Hayao Miyazaki’s The Wind Rises (2013) and produced written reflections on ethical issues raised by the film. The analysis draws on 76 student reflections and follows a qualitative, abductive approach presented using a Gioia-inspired data structure. Theoretically, the study combines perspectives from engineering ethics education with the concept of cinematic ethics, which emphasizes identification with fictional characters, reflection on moral emotions, and the aesthetic expansion of moral imagination. The findings reveal three interwoven modes of ethical engagement through which students interpreted the film: professional responsibility, identification and moral emotion, and aesthetic mediation of ethical imagination. The study highlights these interwoven modes of ethical engagement, stressing the need for engineering ethics education to be aware of, respond to, and utilize different modes, including aesthetic mediation. Furthermore, it reflects on learner positions in arts-based pedagogies, stressing the potential of the viewer position for stimulating ethical reflection. Finally, it reminds educators of the importance of a scaffolded pedagogical setting for film-based pedagogy.

563. The power of AI: balancing ethics and harm for the environmental justice field
Athreya, A. & Palmer, L. (2026). The power of AI: balancing ethics and harm for the environmental justice field. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01448-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence, after the boom of generative AI, is beginning to shape environmental decision-making, at the same moment that environmental justice (EJ) communities face intensifying ecological harm, federal disinvestment, and data erasure. This paper examines the dual tensions AI presents for the EJ field: its potential as a tool for democratizing data access and amplifying community knowledge, and its reproduction of the very hierarchies EJ seeks to dismantle. We document harms across several domains, including the environmental racism embedded in data center siting and energy demand, the extension of fossil fuel industry lifespans, data bias and digital colonialism in AI training, access and literacy inequities, data privacy risks, technological hallucination, and technological solutionism. Drawing on thirty years of community-centered data practice at an EJ nonprofit, we propose an alternative paradigm grounded in the Community Advisory Board model, data democratization, community co-creation, and ethical data governance. This includes community-governed data trusts, accessible data infrastructure, and AI tools designed to build rather than replace community capacity. EJ communities must not only hold AI accountable for its harms but also ask the question of how the technology’s capabilities can serve justice rather than just capital interest.

564. AI sycophancy, self-interpretation, and conversational contentment
Tiran, N. (2026). AI sycophancy, self-interpretation, and conversational contentment. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01451-2
Abstract (original): Abstract AI sycophancy is often conceptualized as a chatbot’s tendency to flout truth, accuracy, or other epistemic standards to shield users from hurt feelings, or otherwise protect them from the consequences of their thoughts, words, or actions. In this paper, I argue against this conception. That is, I argue that sycophantic AI behavior does not necessarily aim to protect users, nor that its harms are essentially epistemic . There are highly prevalent cases in which sycophantic AI chatbots actively contribute to their users’ knowledge. One class of such examples involves cases in which chatbots are used as auto-hermeneutic technologies —as tools to facilitate the user’s interpretation of her own cares, concerns, values, and other identity components and life events. In these cases, LLMs can help articulate the user’s avowed self-interpretations. In turn, avowals of these interpretations by users can often have downstream effects that render the interpretations true. If this is correct, then LLMs can often advance the user’s knowledge regarding important truths about herself. Simultaneously, as I explain, interpretations offered by LLMs may be deeply harmful. This understanding of AI sycophancy can help us clarify what is so disturbing about it, as well as resist the temptation to draw too tight an analogy between sycophantic behaviors in AI systems and in humans.

565. A Contextualist Account of Ascriptions of Faith
Khalaj, M.H.M.A. & Javadi, M. (2026). A Contextualist Account of Ascriptions of Faith. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10152
Abstract (original): Abstract Is there a necessary epistemic condition for ascriptions of faith that P to be appropriate? The primary aim of this article is to address this question by developing a contextualist account of ascriptions of faith. We argue that the ascription of faith that P to a subject S in a context C is semantically appropriate only if there is a salient relevant alternative to P for S in C. We also explore the implications of the contextualist account for the debate on the possession of faith. One group of philosophers argues that having faith that P is incompatible with possessing knowledge that P, while another group disagrees, maintaining that one can simultaneously have faith and knowledge that P. The second aim of the article is to argue that the linguistic facts (at least those discussed in this article) about ascriptions of faith are not sufficient to establish that either group of philosophers is on the right track.

568. A Better Method for Blame: Functional and Paradigm-based Accounts
Seim, M. (2026). A Better Method for Blame: Functional and Paradigm-based Accounts. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101466
Abstract (original): Abstract Traditional conceptual analysis is the most common method used when accounting for the nature of blame. The method of traditional conceptual analysis fails in this endeavor, however, when met with particular counterexamples that present false positives and negatives. Recently this problem in accounting for the nature of blame has led philosophers to explore new methods. The newcomers are functional theories of blame , and paradigm-based explanations of blame . In this paper I explore whether these theories fare better in accounting for the full nature of our blaming practices. I argue that the extant accounts face a problem when determining the paradigm-example and the function of blame: They either end up with a circular explanation, or they revert to traditional conceptual analysis. To avoid circularity and reliance on necessary or sufficient conditions, I argue that a functional account must start by determining the function of blame. To determine the function I look closer at what happens if we fail to blame. Situations where we fail to blame, I argue, show us the functional role of blame. I conclude that we blame someone for wronging us because we need to protect our understanding of moral worth against the threat implicit in this wrongdoing.

569. Pain and Moral Knowledge
Chan, C. (2026). Pain and Moral Knowledge. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101533
Abstract (original): Abstract This article offers a new interpretation of moral knowledge in Wang Yangming’s 王陽明 philosophy, emphasizing its embodied foundations and practical significance. I advance two central claims. First, although moral knowledge ( liangzhi 良知) is often construed as an internal mental sense to know right from wrong, Wang’s writings reveal its deeper grounding in bodily perception, particularly in the perception of pain. Second, while the extension of moral knowledge ( zhi liangzhi 致良知) is typically understood as an inward process of correcting one’s mental states, Wang understands it more fundamentally as the practice of recognizing the affective interconnectedness and mutual resonance between oneself and other beings. Drawing on close readings of Wang’s key texts, informed by comparative and interdisciplinary perspectives, I argue that this embodied and relational account both deepens our understanding of the unity of knowledge and action in Wang’s thought and enriches contemporary discussions of moral motivation.

570. Tempering Political Inferiority: How Does Representation Matter?
Beitz, C.R. (2026). Tempering Political Inferiority: How Does Representation Matter?. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101612
Abstract (original): Niko Kolodny’s The Pecking Order includes a ‘book-length treatment’ of the idea of an ‘equal say’ (6) that is ubiquitous in commonplace talk and philosophical reflection about democracy. 1 In this commentary I concentrate on the account of political representation that forms a main part of the ‘treatment’. That account places democratic representative institutions within a larger framework animated by the thought – a ‘positive conjecture’ – that we have non-derivative claims against being placed in ‘untempered relations of inferiority’ (126) with individuals who wield the power and authority of the state. 572. Precis of
                    The Pecking Order
Kolodny, N. (2026). Precis of
                    The Pecking Order. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101636
Abstract (original): Much in our interior mental lives and in our exterior social structures presupposes that we, human beings, are conscious of social hierarchy, of differences in rank and status. We are ‘conscious’ of hierarchy in both senses of the word: ‘aware’ of and ‘anxious’ about. This consciousness seems rooted in our natural history. Many social animals are likewise preoccupied with ‘pecking order’. These animals include not only chickens, who literally peck, but also our closest primate relatives. And this consciousness of hierarchy, transformed by our species’ special bent for symbol and self-reflection, has driven much of our non-natural history. The main chapters of the human story might be defined by the prevailing answers to the questions of who among us, if anyone, would be above, and who, if anyone, would be below. For most of the career of Homo sapiens , we appear to have lived in societies that were predominantly and often vigilantly egalitarian, at least for adult men. At various times and places, this was eventually upended, with the great many being subordinated, in enduring ways, to the very few. The birth of enduring hierarchy was no easy delivery, apparently having to be midwifed by the coordinated manipulation of superstition and the control, by violence, of food stores. Modernity, albeit not without precedents, is in large part the tale – at times inspiring, at times cautionary – of our experiments with reconciling the equality of individuals with the differentiation of roles and concentrations of power and authority of large-scale, complex societies – with, in one sense of the word, civilization. If we take the longest historical view, therefore, the questions that this has been exploring may be among the first questions of politics. These questions are: First, what is social hierarchy? Second, do we have an objection to being set beneath another in a social hierarchy – standing in a relation of inferiority to another – as such? Third, if there is such an objection, how then do we reconcile ourselves to a social world that seems rife with hierarchy? That is, if complex, large-scale society – if, in one sense of the word, civilization – requires a differentiation of roles and a concentration of power and authority, can it be reconciled with the equality of standing that was guarded so jealously before? The Pecking Order seeks to bring these questions into more explicit, philosophical reflection. This precis tries to convey a bit of what the book has to say about these questions as well as to note some second thoughts and responses to a few specific criticisms.

573. Reply to the Symposium on
                    The Pecking Order
Kolodny, N. (2026). Reply to the Symposium on
                    The Pecking Order. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101648
Abstract (original): Let me take up the second of the two questions first, about whether the asymmetries associated with a representative system might answer to values other than tempering inferiority. As Beitz observes, my stance is already pluralistic. In addition to claims against inferiority, there are also rights against invasion and claims to improvement. So, it remains open to me to grant that representative institutions reflect a compromise between noninferiority and other values. The idea would be, in other words, that the move from direct to representative democracy involves some cost in terms of inferiority, but this is compensated for by the gain in noninvasion and improvement, as well as perhaps still other values. Indeed, the move from direct to representative democracy, while involving a cost in terms of inferiority along one dimension, may alleviate inferiority along another dimension, such as if representative democracy leads to better protections against discrimination.

577. Putting Offense in Its (Fitting) Place (On Campus)
Bell, M. (2026). Putting Offense in Its (Fitting) Place (On Campus). J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10188
Abstract (original): Abstract In recent years, feelings of offense have taken on a starring role in multiple campus controversies. In this paper, I will argue that offense has important moral work to do, but it can only do its work if certain background conditions are met. I begin by sketching several cases of offense on college campuses. I then go on to highlight the central features of what might be described as our current culture of offense. While I think that the apparent rise of this culture of offense is regrettable, I also believe, taking a page from a prominent strand of thought in feminist moral epistemology, that feelings of offense can do important moral work. After sketching a tripartite account of offense, I defend a model of the relationship between feelings of offense, the offensive, and offense-based intervention which I call the Offense as Affective Answer Model. I argue that offense can do its moral work only if certain background conditions are in place, background conditions that are missing in the present culture of offense. Most importantly, a healthy moral culture will be one where assessments of offense in terms of its fit are central and disagreement about fittingness are hashed out through dialogical processes. If we are not willing to subject feelings of offense to these dialogical processes through which we acknowledge and recognize that some tokens of offense are unfitting, offense will not be able to do the important moral work claimed for it.

589. Collective Harm and Instrumental Reasons
Koles, T. (2026). Collective Harm and Instrumental Reasons. J Moral Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1163/17455243-bja10233
Abstract (original): Abstract Collective harm cases are cases in which the actions of a number of people result in a morally significant outcome, but no individual’s actions seem to make a difference to the occurrence of that outcome. This paper argues against the “Instrumentality Thesis,” advanced by Nefsky, on which any successful approach to collective harm cases must be instrumental in the sense that the reasons which it gives against participating in collective harm must involve that action’s influence on the outcome. First, I argue that Nefsky’s own account of collective harm, based on the notion of a “non-superfluous cause,” does not succeed as an instrumental approach. Second, I argue that Nefsky’s objections to non-instrumental views fail, and that we have good reason to think that a successful account of collective harm problems will be non-instrumental in character.

590. The Inadequacy of Selective Apologies
Butt, D. (2026). The Inadequacy of Selective Apologies. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70140
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article examines the ethical shortcomings of selective apologies: cases in which a party apologises for one instance of injustice while refusing to apologise for others. Existing work on apologies has not addressed the particular kind of wrongdoing associated with the making of selective apologies. Apologising to one victim but refusing to apologise to another without good reason adds insult to injury. One is both failing to fulfil an existing duty, and adding an additional dimension of disrespect to one's extant moral failing. In addition, the making of a selective apology may undermine other apologies that one has made. Selectively apologising could only be permissible if one had well‐founded reasons to prioritise some cases over others. Determining if this is true necessitates an honest accounting and evaluation of the full range of one's past wrongdoing. The article develops this argument in three stages: first, by analysing contemporary examples of selective apologies, with a particular focus on the British state; second, by exploring the normative structure of selectively apologising, and third, by considering how the passage of time and the scale of political responsibility complicate the ethics of state apology.
