596. The Case for Human Dignity in Neurotechnology Governance: A Latin American Perspective Beyond Freedom of Thought
Borbón, D. (2026). The Case for Human Dignity in Neurotechnology Governance: A Latin American Perspective Beyond Freedom of Thought. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09681-1
Abstract (original): Abstract The academic debate on the governance of neurotechnology has progressively shifted from proposals to create new lists of so-called neurorights toward the reinterpretation of existing human rights, with freedom of thought attracting particular attention, among a broader set of candidate rights. This article examines whether freedom of thought can currently perform that anchoring role in Latin American jurisdictions. Drawing on a comparative analysis of constitutional texts, jurisprudence, and the Inter-American human rights system, the article argues that freedom of thought does not yet possess a clear, strong, and autonomous normative content in Latin American domestic law and in its regional human rights system. As a complementary and, in the region, more operative anchor, it argues that human dignity, which in several Latin American constitutional orders functions as an absolute autonomous fundamental right of direct application with extensive jurisprudential development, constitutes a more operationally effective legal basis for absolute prohibitions on the use of people as instruments or objects, and can protect them from coercive neurotechnological interventions. The article proposes that human dignity supplies the absolute floor against instrumentalization and coercion, while freedom of thought, as its doctrinal development advances, can complement that floor with the protection of the forum internum . It further contends that any rights-based strategy is structurally insufficient on its own, because rights depend on judicial interpretation that is inherently contextual and contingent, and therefore supports a multilevel governance approach combining the interpretation of existing rights, soft-law principles, and potential specific hard-law provisions.

604. Why Social Egg Freezing for Single Women Should Not Be Prohibited: A Confucian Perspective
Deng, R. & Fan, R. (2026). Why Social Egg Freezing for Single Women Should Not Be Prohibited: A Confucian Perspective. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70202
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Beginning with a recent high‐profile case in mainland China, this paper argues that the current prohibition on social egg freezing for single women cannot be justified from a Confucian ethical perspective. We first explain why Confucian ethics provides a normatively appropriate and socially salient framework for evaluating reproductive policy in the Chinese context. We then articulate a theoretical model that we term Confucian familist virtue ethics . A central contribution of the paper is to distinguish between Confucian ethical ideals and its minimal standards. While the ideals—such as timely marriage and childbearing within an intact family—are properly the objects of moral aspiration and voluntary cultivation, only violations of the minimal standards warrant legal prohibition. On this basis, we develop three lines of argument to show that although social egg freezing by single women may fall short of Confucian aspirational ideals, it does not violate the ethical minimum. Accordingly, it does not justify coercive state intervention. Finally, we examine the predictive concern that permitting social egg freezing would undermine marital norms or substantially increase single motherhood. We argue that such negative effects are empirically uncertain and in principle manageable through regulatory design. They therefore fail to provide sufficient grounds for a categorical prohibition.

605. From Imminence to Institutional Incidence: Rethinking Fetal Status Beyond Metaphysical Classification
Sakr, J. (2026). From Imminence to Institutional Incidence: Rethinking Fetal Status Beyond Metaphysical Classification. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70203
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This response examines Joel Cox's proposal to reconceptualise fetal moral status as “imminent personhood.” While welcoming Cox's effort to move beyond static potential/actual personhood binaries, it argues that the concept of imminence remains metaphysically suggestive but institutionally under‐determined. Legal systems cannot operate on continuously variable metaphysical properties; they require discrete and administrable allocations of rights, duties, and powers. To address this gap, the paper proposes an institutional incidence framework, in which legal status is understood as the selective assignment of juridical incidents according to regulatory objectives without treating metaphysical classification as dispositive. On this view, the central question is not simply whether the fetus is a person (actual, potential, or imminent), but which incidents of legal status should attach at particular stages and for particular regulatory purposes. This reframes the legal regulation of abortion as a problem of institutional design rather than ontological determination. The paper argues that such an approach better explains existing legal practice, avoids threshold arbitrariness, and enables stable legal regulation amid persistent moral disagreement. While Cox's imminence proposal captures an important phenomenology of fetal development, an institutional incidence framework provides a more durable foundation for legal and policy architecture in pluralistic societies.

609. Expert perspectives on framing risk in engineering biology: anticipating ethical discourse about climate change mitigation
Fulvi, D. et al. (2026). Expert perspectives on framing risk in engineering biology: anticipating ethical discourse about climate change mitigation. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05848-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Given the rapid acceleration of the climate crisis, the IPCC has deemed Carbon Dioxide Removal (CDR) technologies as unavoidable to sufficiently mitigate the crisis. In this context, engineering biology (EngBio) is increasingly gaining traction in CDR research. While the deployment of EngBio is perceived by some as inherently high-risk for society, the economy, and the environment, the risks posed by insufficiently abated climate change are likely to be irreparable. Against this background, a pilot study was conducted, aimed at exploring experts’ perspectives on risk in decision-making regarding the use of advanced EngBio applications to develop CDR technologies. Our results show that EngBio experts are moderately or highly open to discussing, accepting, or taking the risks of using EngBio-based CDR. Our findings also indicate expert trust in the potential of rapid advancements in EngBio-based CDR technologies. While these findings do not entail an unconditional and uncritical acceptance of all risks arising from using EngBio for CDR purposes, they underscore a notion of risk as integral to environmental responsibility, namely as necessary to avoid the higher risk of insufficiently abated climate change. In our view, this notion of risk highlights the epistemic context in which ethical debates on CDR will unfold as the climate crisis intensifies, shifting the focus from traditional risk-risk analysis to how EngBio‑based CDR can contribute to environmental responsibility and to effective and ethical decarbonisation.

610. Cognitive enfeeblement by design
Sharadin, N.P. (2026). Cognitive enfeeblement by design. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05851-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Thinking hard is hard. Cognitive misers like us are therefore inclined to seek extreme cognitive assistance : assistance that is ubiquitously available, highly substitutive, and relatively domain-general. Increasingly, we are being offered assistance like this as the default mode of cognition – it is being embedded in the infrastructure of our digital lives. But whatever its merits for adults, providing extreme cognitive assistance to children is pro tanto morally wrong. This is because the cognitive capacities that make a person self-governing are skills: they are built through deliberate, regular exercise and lost without it. Extreme cognitive assistance removes both the opportunities for that exercise and the incentive to undertake it. Maybe this won’t matter: in a future awash with extreme cognitive assistance, maybe persons won’t need or want to think for themselves. But people should decide for themselves how much of their cognitive self-governance to hand over; and doing that requires having the capacity for cognitive self-governance in the first place.

611. Professional ethical guidance for AI use (PEG-AI) in healthcare: a rapid scoping review
Smith, H. & Ives, J. (2026). Professional ethical guidance for AI use (PEG-AI) in healthcare: a rapid scoping review. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01443-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Healthcare professionals (HCPs) and the patients they treat would benefit from professional ethical guidance for the use of AI (PEG-AI) in healthcare. A proactive approach, asserting unified and standardised guidance covering all HCPs in the United Kingdom, would assert clear and authoritative standards for HCP practice to abide by. This rapid scoping review seeks to aid this goal by answering the question: What ethical principles, rules, or claims that can guide the use of AI in professional healthcare practice can be identified within the literature?. The literature search resulted in the inclusion of 124 items and generated 9 overarching themes comprising 33 distinct normative claims about the ethical use of AI in healthcare. This review captures current published thinking about principles to govern AI use in clinical settings across a wide range of peer-reviewed literature, grey literature, and formal guidance. This provides a baseline on which to build unified guidance across the healthcare professions.

613. Metaepistemology and the Value Problem
Dandelet, S. (2026). Metaepistemology and the Value Problem. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10137
Abstract (original): Abstract What makes knowledge more valuable than mere true belief? This is the so-called value problem . Some epistemologists think that providing a solution to this problem is a key desideratum for theories of knowledge. One simple argument for this position starts with the claim that knowledge is intuitively more valuable than true belief. A more subtle and ambitious argument is rooted in the idea that theorists about knowledge must explain why knowledge is distinctively valuable, on pain of embarrassment; for without such an explanation, the thinking seems to go, epistemologists must confront the awkward possibility that they are spending time and energy on an unworthy subject. In what follows, I flesh out each of the arguments sketched above in a number of ways, and I argue that none of these versions succeed. The moral is that the value problem does not have the metaepistemological significance that is sometimes attributed to it.

614. Innocence by Accreditation: Mythic Branding and Audit Feminism in Athena Swan
Arandjelović, O. (2026). Innocence by Accreditation: Mythic Branding and Audit Feminism in Athena Swan. Hypatia.
https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10111
Abstract (original): Abstract The UK’s Athena Swan Charter positions itself as a policy instrument, an award-based scheme for improving gender equality through self-assessment, evidence gathering, and action planning. I argue that this description overlooks the fact that Athena Swan is also a semiotic framework that governs gender through naming, iconography, and the public performance of institutional virtue. I defend three claims. First, the Charter’s mythic-inflected branding, acronym, and iconography frame the ideal of intelligible, respectable gender-equality practice that is in tension with emancipatory aims of loosening coercive gender norms and challenging credibility hierarchies. Second, insofar as Athena Swan operates by conferring public credentials, I elucidate how its semiotics function not merely expressively but also as instruments of legitimation that shape incentives, trust, institutional identity, and the handling of dissent. Third, I contend that the Charter’s credential economy is supported by audit feminism which abstracts equality into auditable artefacts (documents, metrics, and narratives) assessed as a basis for external recognition. The result is an audit regime that intensifies performativity, encouraging institutions to substitute appearances for substantive change and to commodify individuals as interchangeable units in a managed representational economy.

616. The Impact of Semantics in Medicines Regulation
Costa, E. & Kesselheim, A.S. (2026). The Impact of Semantics in Medicines Regulation. Journal of Law, Medicine & Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1017/jme.2026.10293
Abstract (original): Abstract Words can have inherently multiple meanings; therefore, semantics can directly impact policies and behaviours. In regulatory practice, semantics refers to the specific, actionable meaning behind terminology, and attention to semantics can help ensure that legal frameworks are interpreted accurately. Refining semantics is not a purely theoretical exercise, but a subtle yet powerful element that has the potential to make the regulatory system more efficient in setting priorities and more reliable in meeting societal expectations and needs. When their intended purpose does not align with their true meaning, labels widely used in pharmaceutical regulation such as “breakthrough,” “orphans,” or “unmet medical need” risks undermining the clarity and precision essential to a sound regulatory framework. In addition, differences in definitions, scopes, eligibility criteria, and incentives can obstruct the efficient convergence of efforts in drug development and access. As pharmaceutical investments and clinical research increasingly transcend borders, the adoption of consistent terminology and regulatory definitions becomes crucial. This alignment can enhance cooperation among investors, governments, regulators, researchers, health care practitioners, and patients.

https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag078
Abstract (original): Abstract According to Paul Boghossian’s ‘Inference Problem’, rule-following generates a regress because it involves inference, which itself involves rule-following. 625. A Document Beyond the Request: A Phenomenological Case Study of
                    ChatGPT
Ramello, S. (2026). A Document Beyond the Request: A Phenomenological Case Study of
                    ChatGPT. Theoria.
https://doi.org/10.1111/theo.70127
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article examines a document that the ChatGPT interface made available during an exchange about capacity for saved memories. The response presented the reorganisation of writing rules for a narrative project as already completed, although my request concerned capacity alone, and no recorded prompt requested a file or specified the proposed hierarchy. Earlier, I had copied every visible saved memory entry verbatim into another document, allowing comparison in both directions to identify corresponding formulations and changes in their placement or prescriptive force. The phenomenological account follows my remembered reading and remains separate from those findings. In the linked document, rules appeared within a hierarchy not specified in my request and reading those formulations in their new positions changed how I understood decisions. ChatGPT presented the organisation as already complete, whereas a human collaborator could have presented the same arrangement together with reasons.

626. Stringent policies were associated with greater Covid-19 vaccine uptake including among those who did not trust the vaccine
Savani, M.M. et al. (2026). Stringent policies were associated with greater Covid-19 vaccine uptake including among those who did not trust the vaccine. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261484277
Abstract (original): Are stringent vaccine promotion policies associated with higher vaccine uptake? We argue policy stringency, which changes the costs and benefits of immunisation choices, can affect individual vaccination decisions. In a survey of G-7 countries, 22% of respondents ( n = 42,417) said they did not trust the Covid-19 vaccine, but half of them reported having at least one vaccination. We investigate factors associated with take-up. Our data indicate a high-stringency policy environment (with vaccine mandates and travel restrictions) is associated with increased uptake of the initial vaccination protocol (7 percentage points) compared to a low-stringency policy environment. Among those who did not trust the Covid-19 vaccine, a high-stringency policy environment is associated with increased uptake of the initial vaccine (16 percentage points) and the booster vaccine (20 percentage points). Our findings suggest high-stringency policies can promote vaccine uptake but raise questions about how to strike the right balance between persuasive and coercive policies.

627. Too big to fail: On a systemic injustice of modern finance
Ibsen, M.F. (2026). Too big to fail: On a systemic injustice of modern finance. Eur J Polit Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14748851261499651
Abstract (original): This article argues that the existence of financial institutions considered ‘too big to fail’ (TBTF) represents a systemic injustice requiring fundamental reform of the existing financial system. Drawing on and extending recent work on structural domination in republican political theory, the article develops the concept of ‘systemic institutional domination’ to capture the distinctive form of unfreedom that TBTF engenders. Systemic institutional domination is a reflexive form of domination, where the democratic state is subjected to uncontrolled power vested in identifiable financial institutions but grounded in the norms, rules and regulations of the financial system that the state itself enacts and upholds. Because the dominated public institution is at once the constitutive author of the system and the only agent capable of reforming it, TBTF confronts republican theory with a case of second-order domination: domination of the institution charged with securing nondomination. The article identifies four dimensions of systemic domination: effective compulsion to bail out failing megabanks; de facto legal impunity; the systematic subversion of tax enforcement; and background consolidatory pressure on public finances. The article concludes by discussing the democratic paradox involved in abolishing TBTF: namely, that the dominated institution – the democratic state – is the only institution that could reform the dominating system.

629. Logic as Normative Modeling
Benedetto, M.D. & Osta-Vèlez, M. (2026). Logic as Normative Modeling. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01162-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Many philosophers understand logic as a theory of truth preservation that is value-neutral and becomes prescriptive only when combined with external, non-logical rules. In this article, we develop an alternative view informed by recent work in the philosophy of scientific modeling. We argue that certain logical systems, in certain scientific and philosophical practices, are used as normative scientific models: they represent their targets by combining descriptive idealizations with assumptions adopted—and defended—on normative grounds. We propose a practice-based account of normative models in science, according to which the normative character of a model depends on its use in the relevant research practice, and apply it to the case of classical first-order logic, in its uses as a model of correct reasoning. Finally, we show how our proposal opens up a new perspective on several contemporary debates in the philosophy of logic.

633. Towards a non-referential theory of meaning: intra-theoretical synonymy
Engler, M. (2026). Towards a non-referential theory of meaning: intra-theoretical synonymy. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05847-9
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper develops an axiomatic framework for a non-referential theory of meaning by formally characterizing intra-theoretical synonymy through inferential and syntactic criteria rather than through reference or truth conditions. Building on formalist and inferentialist traditions, a series of axioms are proposed that satisfy basic semantic principles such as theory-dependence, semantic holism, and (restricted) compositionality. The resulting systems ( TS 0 and its extensions) satisfy these principles to progressively greater degrees, yielding a sufficiently fine-grained notion of synonymy. The framework thus shows that equality of meaning can be characterized in non-referential terms, while illuminating the limits of non-referential meaning.

634. Fragile lives and moral duties: on the ethics of modal security
Garcia, A.G. & Gunnemyr, M. (2026). Fragile lives and moral duties: on the ethics of modal security. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05849-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Some people are safe from harm yet remain in positions where things could easily go badly for them. Their proximity to worlds where something bad happens to them makes their situation ethically precarious. In some worlds, they are excluded, punished, or abandoned, which deserves attention in its own right. We argue that this is not just a matter of risk or harm, but about how people are placed within the space of possible worlds. We explain this through a modal account of difference-making and use it to highlight attitudinal responses fitting to fragility and introduce duties of modal security: moral reasons to protect one another from positions where small changes could lead to significant loss.

635. De-extinction, resurrection and biological Frankensteins: scientific metaphors and their impact on public perception
Asteinza, M. et al. (2026). De-extinction, resurrection and biological Frankensteins: scientific metaphors and their impact on public perception. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05854-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Metaphors in science communication do more than simplify complex ideas; they impose certain epistemic frameworks with the potential to steer public attitudes and behavior. In this paper, we investigate the effects of metaphorical framing on public understanding and evaluation of de-extinction, a field where dominant metaphors (“resurrection,” “bringing back to life”) have been criticized for mischaracterizing the technology and fostering undue optimism. We presented a nationally representative sample of the Spanish population ( N = 1,522) with popular science descriptions of de-extinction that varied only in metaphorical framing and assessed its downstream effects on misinformation, perceived scientific rigor, attitudes, funding preferences, and sharing intentions. Promotional metaphors increased misinformation relative to the neutral condition, while critical metaphors reduced it. These effects occurred without altering readers’ perception of the text’s scientific rigor—a dissociation we characterize as credibility without comprehension . Critical metaphors also reduced support for de-extinction, whereas promotional metaphors did not boost support above baseline levels in the neutral condition. Funding allocations and sharing intentions, though related to misinformation, were unaffected by metaphorical framing. We propose that metaphors function as epistemic conditioning devices : they influence downstream judgments not through direct persuasion, but by structuring the understanding upon which those judgments are formed. These findings bear on philosophical debates about the role of metaphors in science, the epistemic preconditions of democratic engagement with emerging technologies, and the limits of lexical precision as a remedy for public misinformation.

636. Two explanations of experts by experience in psychiatry: epistemic capabilities or epistemic diversity
Sugimoto, M. (2026). Two explanations of experts by experience in psychiatry: epistemic capabilities or epistemic diversity. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05862-w
Abstract (original): Abstract In the field of mental health, the role of Experts by Experience (ExE) has been gaining increasing attention. However, theoretical research on this topic remains limited, leaving it unclear how ExE can contribute to improving psychiatric research and services. In this paper, I propose two explanations for the contributions of ExE, drawing on perspectives from feminist philosophy of science. The first explanation focuses on the epistemic capabilities of ExE, grounded in standpoint theory. This approach highlights the unique individual potential of ExE as a factor that enhances psychiatric research and services. The second explanation underscores the epistemic diversity between ExE and professionals in research and/or service contexts, focusing on the value of epistemic diversity within teams and communities. The main difference is that epistemic capabilities focus on the individual, while epistemic diversity focuses on the collective. I argue that these two explanatory models should be chosen based on research design, but in the context of psychiatric PPI, considering ExE participation not only in terms of standpoint but also in terms of epistemic diversity may suggest a more inclusive approach to PPI.

645. Who are the agents?
Steward, H. (2026). Who are the agents?. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05863-9
Abstract (original): Abstract The question who (or which things) are the agents has attracted a very wide range of different answers. For some, only humans can satisfy the necessary conditions for agency; others extend the concept to a wide range of animals or even plants; and some believe there are also agents outside the biological realm. Do these different answers constitute substantively different views about the same question? Or are the disagreements really terminological ones, at root, owed to different ‘definitions’ of agency? In this paper, I try to provide examples both of disputes which do, and disputes which do not seem amenable to this sort of terminological deflation. I then try to diagnose the difference, by offering a view about how we must think about the concept of agency if it is to play a role in genuinely substantive disagreement, combining Susan Carey’s idea that agency is a ‘core concept’ with an externalist account of how the concept must work, if it is to figure in thoroughly substantive disputes about who the agents are.

646. Asking Questions Aloud: A Case for Interrogative Transmission
Willard-Kyle, C. (2026). Asking Questions Aloud: A Case for Interrogative Transmission. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10144
Abstract (original): Abstract I argue that questions can be transmitted through asking just as beliefs are transmitted through testimony. Just as we rely on testifiers to assert only when their epistemic position is good enough, so (I argue) we rely on queriers to ask questions only if they are in a position to know that the question is sound, which guarantees that the asked question has an answer. Along the way, I argue against the view that we can only ask questions of people who (we know) know the answer to our questions. A recurring theme of the paper is that we learn about the norms for asking not just in those cases in which we straightforwardly comply with or violate them, but also by the ways we creatively deviate from the norms to accomplish our conversational goals.

647. Everything Everywhere All at Once – From Migration as Sequence to Migration as Simultaneity
Farahat, A. (2026). Everything Everywhere All at Once – From Migration as Sequence to Migration as Simultaneity. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10248
Abstract (original): Abstract This Article investigates how law conceptualizes the temporality of migration, exposing a mismatch between the legal framework and migrants’ lived experiences. It shows that the dominant mode, which views migration as a sequential process, fails to capture the realities of migration. The Article argues that both epistemic and human rights arguments call for remedying this mismatch. In response, it introduces migration as simultaneity, which better reflects migrants’ experiences and the interplay between space, place, and time in migration and belonging. The argument proceeds in three stages. First, it examines migration as a sequence, the prevailing concept embedded in international, European, and domestic migration and citizenship laws. This sequential framework operates through linearity and temporariness, which function as primary mechanisms for governing inclusion and exclusion. Second, drawing on migration studies and anthropology, it critiques this model, showing its inability to account for how migrants perceive and experience migration and belonging. Finally, it argues that strong power asymmetries shaping the regulation of migration demand a temporal conception that better aligns with migrants’ lived experiences. To this end, it advances migration as simultaneity and suggests that this concept can inform legal frameworks regulating migration and belonging.

648. The Organizational Turn in Corporate Sanctioning Regimes: Rebalancing Profit–Seeking and Stakeholder Protection
Korkka-Knuts, H. (2026). The Organizational Turn in Corporate Sanctioning Regimes: Rebalancing Profit–Seeking and Stakeholder Protection. German Law Journal.
https://doi.org/10.1017/glj.2026.10249
Abstract (original): Abstract Modern shareholder primacy is commonly justified by reference to an equilibrium famously articulated by Milton Friedman: business may pursue profits provided it operates “within the rules of the game.” The legitimacy of shareholder–oriented governance thus depends on effective legal mechanisms capable of constraining harmful profit-seeking. This Article argues that corporate sanctioning regimes play a constitutive role in sustaining that equilibrium and in structuring the relationship between shareholder-oriented corporate purpose and stakeholder protection. Drawing on examples from EU competition enforcement, benefit–based attribution models of corporate criminal liability in the United States and Finland, and the organizational failure-to-prevent regime exemplified by the UK Bribery Act, the Article shows how sanctioning regimes institutionalize competing conceptions of the corporation as an economic instrument or as an organizational actor. Economically calibrated liability models reflect a conception of the corporation primarily as an economic instrument, framing compliance in instrumental terms and narrowing the boundaries of corporate accountability. By contrast, organizationally informed liability models embed responsibility in internal governance structures and preventive capacities. The Article conceptualizes this development as an emerging organizational turn in corporate sanctioning in which liability increasingly serves to shape internal corporate governance, encourage the prevention of harm, and protect stakeholders. It thereby conditions the legitimacy of corporate profit-seeking and redefines the institutional role of corporate sanctions in shaping corporate responsibility.

649. Relational legal consciousness in the age of informational capitalism: how Americans make sense of data privacy
Kaul, V. & Stryker, R. (2026). Relational legal consciousness in the age of informational capitalism: how Americans make sense of data privacy. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10128
Abstract (original): Abstract Socio-legal scholarship increasingly conceptualizes legal consciousness as relational, yet research has focused primarily on how individuals’ understandings of law are shaped through interpersonal interactions and social relationships. Less attention has been paid to how broader cultural beliefs circulating within political-economic systems shape whether – and how – individuals invoke or refrain from invoking law when interpreting everyday experiences. We extend relational legal consciousness scholarship by examining how macro-level cultural beliefs structure meaning-making about digital data privacy in the United States. We analyze data from a survey experiment in which 600 respondents evaluated vignettes describing secondary uses of personal data by government and private corporations for different motives. Quantitative analyses of 2,555 vignette evaluations show that perceived acceptability varies systematically by both actor and motive. Interpretive analysis of open-ended responses reveals that participants draw on multiple cultural beliefs – including everyday expectations of legality, market logics, institutional trust, and narratives of technological inevitability – when evaluating these practices. By showing how cultural beliefs related and unrelated to law and legality work together to shape everyday interpretations of privacy encroachments, we extend relational legal consciousness scholarship beyond interpersonal relationships to encompass the broader cultural environment in which legal meaning-making occurs. We further demonstrate that this relational dynamic has political-economic consequences: a diverse range of cultural beliefs – not only narratives of commodification and technological inevitability – can normalize, conditionally legitimate, or challenge the data practices associated with informational capitalism.

650. Delegated Gatekeeping: A Study of the Alternative Investment Market (“AIM”)
Chan, J. (2026). Delegated Gatekeeping: A Study of the Alternative Investment Market (“AIM”). Camb Law J.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0008197326101755
Abstract (original): Abstract This article develops the concept of delegated gatekeeping to explain regulatory systems where a primary gatekeeper (the “principal”) delegates revocable roles of support withholder or provider to other gatekeepers (“agents”). Delegation requires that agents assume non-traditional gatekeeping roles such as reputational stewardship (safeguarding the principal’s reputational capital). To illustrate delegated gatekeeping concretely, this article conducts a detailed case study of the London Stock Exchange’s Alternative Investment Market (“AIM”). It argues that reputational incentives can partially alleviate agent gatekeepers’ conflicts of interest, and that these incentives have strengthened on AIM relative to historic levels.

651. Biperspectival Regret: The Appropriate Emotion for Faultless Group Harm
Gönüllü, A. (2026). Biperspectival Regret: The Appropriate Emotion for Faultless Group Harm. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s003181912610165x
Abstract (original): Abstract Bernard Williams’s account of regret contains a lacuna with respect to the appropriate emotion for contributors to feel in response to faultless group harms. While he delineates ‘agent-regret’ for agents faultlessly causing harm and ‘spectator-regret’ for observers, neither suffices for individuals who unintentionally or unforeseeably contribute to harms resulting from group actions. This paper investigates the fitting emotion for such cases. I contend that contributors occupy ‘as-if positions’ in that they are quasi-agents in virtue of their own contributions and quasi-spectators owing to others’ roles. This dual standpoint generates a ‘biperspective’ that calls for a distinct form of regret, which is reducible to neither agent-regret nor spectator-regret but synthesises elements of both. I term this distinct form ‘biperspectival regret’. Taking the appropriateness of emotions to depend on their formal objects, I formulate the formal object of biperspectival regret as the ‘comparative disvalue of group harm to which one faultlessly contributed’. Because this formal object corresponds to the defining features of faultless group harms, I argue that biperspectival regret is the warranted emotion for contributors to experience in such scenarios.

652. Disability, relationships and a right against social deprivation
Clough, B. & Herring, J. (2026). Disability, relationships and a right against social deprivation. International Journal of Law in Context.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100688
Abstract (original): Abstract Disabled people continue to experience social exclusion and denial of meaningful belonging despite legal commitments to independent living and autonomy. This paper brings disability studies, legal scholarship and philosophical debates around a right against social deprivation into conversation. We argue that the concepts of social access needs and social contribution needs provide a valuable framework for identifying harms that existing disability rights and human rights approaches often fail to recognise or adequately address. At the same time, rather than simply applying the right against social deprivation to disability, we contend that disability offers a critical lens through which the right itself must be rethought. Drawing on critical disability studies, we foreground interdependence, care, relationality and vulnerability, while exposing ableist assumptions that may shape theorising in this area. Through engagement with disability law and rights frameworks, we explore the potential of this dialogue to reconfigure understandings of rights, responsibility and justice.

656. Adversarialism in the Family Courts in Child Arrangements Cases in England and Wales
Burton, M. & Hunter, R. (2026). Adversarialism in the Family Courts in Child Arrangements Cases in England and Wales. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70076
Abstract (original): Most of the discussion of adversarialism has taken place in the context of criminal justice and it is often assumed that family courts are not adversarial, particularly in child arrangements cases where the stated focus is the future welfare of children. Drawing on data from a recent study of child arrangements proceedings in England and Wales this article will argue that despite modifications to the adversarial process, adversarialism remains a structural barrier to safe processes and outcomes in cases where there are allegations of domestic abuse. 657. Worldview Silencing: Recognizing Reasoned Objections to Brain Death
McCurdy, J.L. et al. (2026). Worldview Silencing: Recognizing Reasoned Objections to Brain Death. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70205
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The determination of death by neurologic criteria (brain death) has increasingly become a topic of controversy. Here we address a question that provokes clinical ethics consultations and scholarly debate: “how should objections to the declaration of neurologic death be handled?” While much of the discourse on this question has focused on the legitimacy of the criteria for brain death determinations or the motivations of individuals' objections to declarations of death, our analysis addresses a deeper issue inherent in the conflicts in clinical settings. We argue that some objections to brain death cannot be explained away as grief, misunderstanding, or different religious beliefs. Rather, tensions between worldviews—fundamental standpoints or lenses through which humans experience and conceptualize the world around them—may drive conflicts around brain death. In what follows, we introduce the concept of worldview silencing as a means of recognizing the injustice that can occur when brain death objections grounded in alternative ontologies of death are unfairly dismissed or disrespected. Ultimately, we contend that the concept of worldview silencing offers moral guidance for just recognition of objections to brain death in practice, education, and policy.

658. On the Identity of Facts: A Reply to Lowe
Imaguire, G. (2026). On the Identity of Facts: A Reply to Lowe. Analytic Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/phib.70025
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT According to a traditional conception, a fact is individuated by its constituents and the way these are structured. Lowe (1998) argues against this, claiming that there is no principled way to ascertain what the constituents of facts are and, therefore, there is no way to coherently individuate them. In this paper, I examine and reject Lowe's argument. It will be shown that there are different conceptions of facts, a fine‐grained and a coarse‐grained conception, and that both can coherently individuate facts. In my diagnosis, Lowe's argument arises from a conflation of both conceptions.

659. The Contraction of the Possible: Algorithmic Infrastructures and the Erosion of Critical Imagination
Ferreira, G.B. (2026). The Contraction of the Possible: Algorithmic Infrastructures and the Erosion of Critical Imagination. Philosophy & Social Criticism.
https://doi.org/10.1177/01914537261496517
Abstract (original): This article argues that the contemporary crisis of the public sphere cannot be adequately understood as a crisis of communication alone. What is at stake is a contraction of the horizon of the possible: a narrowing of the conditions under which alternatives can be imagined. Drawing on the Frankfurt School tradition, it develops a concept of imagination as a critical faculty grounded in Walter Benjamin’s characterisation of it as the “awareness of the de-formations of the future,” and traces its erosion through Marcuse’s analysis of one-dimensional society. The article proposes the model of Infrastructural Conditioning of Imagination (ICI), identifying three interdependent dynamics (filtering, familiarisation and anticipation) through which algorithmic systems pre-structure the field of the thinkable. Each dynamic bears on a distinct dimension of experience: filtering concerns the structure of access, familiarisation the structure of reception, and anticipation the structure of temporality. The article argues that this conditioning operates as a suppression of non-identity in Adorno’s sense, eroding the conditions of Erfahrung under which the preponderance of the object can function as a critical moment, at the level of the conditions through which subjectivity itself is formed.

660. ‘Why are girls lifting that heavy if it makes
                    that
                    happen?’: From abjection to a weak theory of ‘pissing yourself’ in sport
Desjardins, B. & Ketterling, J. (2026). ‘Why are girls lifting that heavy if it makes
                    that
                    happen?’: From abjection to a weak theory of ‘pissing yourself’ in sport. Feminist Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/14647001261491251
Abstract (original): Kristeva’s abjection theory is a popular framework for grappling with leaking bodies. Yet while exploring the significance of women's involuntary urination during sport, abject theory's insistence that pissing oneself must be identity shattering failed to convince us. Indeed, some women find pissing themselves to be identity forming as it marks the threshold beyond which one becomes a ‘serious’ athlete. Drawing on Sedgwick, we frame abjection as strong theory—able to explain some aspects of the experience well but unable to account for other aspects. To supplement our approach, we draw on sexuality studies for inspiration regarding how to approach involuntary urination during sport through weak theory. Rather than trying to proffer a totalizing explanation, weak theory foregrounds near and specific phenomena, allowing for variation and complexity. In this case, weak theory is analytically useful as it allows us to consider women's diverse responses to urinary leakages alongside mainstream cultural responses (disgust at the leaky body) and subcultural sporting norms (prioritization of sport performance). Further, weak theory allows us to attend to the gendered specificities of peeing in sport. Drawing on interviews with women strength athletes, we question what women's responses to their nuanced, specific experience of peeing themselves while resistance training tells us about women's place in sport and their bodily capacities. Ultimately, we argue that far from being abject, bodily leaks can be transformative, creating new subjectivities and new understandings of what women's bodies are capable of: namely, being simultaneously strong, beautiful, and leaky. This article contributes a grounded exploration of the limits of abjection theory by highlighting the necessity of considering cultural context and specific phenomena, arguing that weak theory is a useful lens through which to do so.

661. Sexueller Zugang aus der Ferne Sexual access from a distance
Baron, T. (2026). Sexueller Zugang aus der Ferne Sexual access from a distance. Zeitschrift für Praktische Philosophie.
https://doi.org/10.22613/zfpp/13.1.1
Abstract (original): Der Konsum von Online-Pornos, eine relativ weit verbreitete Praxis, hat im Vergleich zur philosophischen Diskussion über die abstrakte Natur der Pornografie und im Vergleich zur Ethik anderer Konsumentscheidungen vergleichsweise wenig philosophische Aufmerksamkeit erfahren. Im zweiten Teil des Artikels, präsentiere ich ein weiteres Problem des Konsums von Online-Pornografie – unabhängig davon, ob sie gewalttätig ist oder nicht –, da der sexuelle Zugang zu anderen durch den Konsum dieser Medien eine Form sexueller Aktivität darstellt, die den allgemein anerkannten Standards einer sinnvollen (moralisch transformierenden) Einwilligung nicht genügt.

663. Two Ways to Think About (Implicit) Structure
Schiemer, G. (2026). Two Ways to Think About (Implicit) Structure. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01164-2
Abstract (original): Abstract The article compares two ways to think about the implicit structure of systems studied in pure mathematics. The first approach specifies implicit structure in terms of formal languages, relying on a model-theoretic notion of definability. The second approach defines the notion in terms of invariance criteria. The structural features of a given system are often considered to be properties which are invariant under specific transformations of the system. In the paper, I analyze these two approaches to implicit structure in terms of invariance and definability conditions by drawing to the field of finite geometry. I then explore their formal relationship in the framework of relational algebra, based on the concepts of Galois closures and Krasner algebras. Finally, I examine the equivalence of mathematical systems with respect to their implicit structure in terms of the model-theoretic notion of bi-interpretability.

665. Delusions and Compelling Experiences
Nie, C. (2026). Delusions and Compelling Experiences. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01166-0
Abstract (original): Abstract Delusion is one of the most puzzling doxastic phenomena: on the one hand, the subject appears irrational, holding a delusion despite counterevidence; on the other hand, the subject appears otherwise rational, apportioning their non-delusional beliefs to the evidence. Leading theories of delusion, despite their strengths, still face notable limitations. This article argues that they may benefit from a new empiricist approach. Drawing on insights from Hobbes and Locke, it proposes that the subject may have a distinctive kind of compelling experience that is phenomenologically analogous to, and can effectively be more compelling than, the Cartesian ‘clear experience’. How the experience may, in various ways and to varying degrees, compel domain-specific irrational belief in the face of counterevidence will be articulated.

668. Doxastic Deliberation and the Puzzles of Transparency
Vollmer, M. (2026). Doxastic Deliberation and the Puzzles of Transparency. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01169-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Doxastic deliberation about how to doxastically relate to a given proposition p is said to be transparent, in that the central deliberative question of ‘Whether to believe p?’ is equivalent to ‘Whether p?’. I discuss four challenges to this thesis based on non-evidential epistemic reasons, deliberative dilemmas, self-effecting attitudes, and permissivist pragmatism. By employing recent insights from both the theory of epistemic normativity and the literature on practical deliberation, I argue that each of these puzzles can be squared with a properly limited and constrained version of the transparency thesis. Ultimately, I show that the claim that doxastic deliberation is after settling ‘Whether p?’ can only be understood as an approximation of a sub-question of the initial normative deliberative question concerning a very specific set of attitudinal options. Nevertheless, the resulting theory retains the core motivation and upshots of the transparency thesis: one is still able to explain why the deliberative norms of doxastic deliberation appear to be, for the most part, epistemic norms.

669. Expressivism and Evidence for Moral Judgments
Pacheco, R.V. (2026). Expressivism and Evidence for Moral Judgments. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01170-4
Abstract (original): Abstract We often support our moral judgements by appealing to independent considerations. According to some approaches in moral epistemology, the best way to understand this support is as evidential . However, it is unclear how some metaethical positions, like expressivism, can be compatible with such a view. For expressivists, moral judgements are desire-like states, which ostensibly are not the sorts of states which can be evidentially supported. In this paper, I argue that expressivists can indeed make sense of relations of evidential support for moral judgements. Drawing on a common sides-stepping expressivist strategy, I argue that expressivists should first focus not on evidential relations themselves but on our thought and talk of the form ‘ e is evidence for p ’. I then appeal to a form of epistemic expressivism, according to which such claims express complex non-cognitive attitudes or normative perspectives about standards that make it permissible to judge that p given e . This approach allows expressivists to reinterpret questions about what evidential relations between evidence e and moral judgements that p are as first-order normative questions about which standards not ruled out by acceptable normative perspectives make it permissible to judge that p given e . Responses to these first-order questions should be, in principle, compatible with expressivist second-order accounts of moral thought and talk. Finally, to show that this first-order interpretation is plausible, I outline a version of evidential explanationism along those lines.

671. Strange Days Have Found Us
Dohrn, D. (2026). Strange Days Have Found Us. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01181-1
Abstract (original): Abstract Many authors who reject the vacuous truth of counterpossibles still adopt a strangeness of impossibility condition (SIC): possible worlds are always closer than impossible ones. Others doubt that SIC holds across the board. I situate the debate between linguistics and other disciplines. Then I weigh considerations of conservativity, systematicity and intuitiveness against counterexamples to SIC and purported theoretical benefits of abandoning SIC. My result is that neither the supporting nor the opposing evidence so far is compelling. The most plausible hypothesis is that SIC holds for a ‘standard resolution’ of the vagueness of counterpossibles, but that it can be overridden by context and pragmatic considerations even when counterpossibles are assessed ‘out of the blue’.

673. Imagining evil: from violent imaginings to violent extremist video games
Kelsall, J. (2026). Imagining evil: from violent imaginings to violent extremist video games. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09931-z
Abstract (original): Abstract In philosophical work on the ethics of video games, ludic amoralists argue that acts players perform in games are beyond moral scrutiny. This is chiefly supported by the following arguments: the fictional harm argument, the consequentialist rebuttal, and the moral exploration argument. The fictional harm argument supports ludic amoralism in principle: if all game actions are fictional, none can be immoral. The consequentialist rebuttal supports a defeasible default: if violent games do not reliably produce harmful outcomes, amoralism is the default position. The moral exploration argument provides an aesthetic reason for amoralism: play is beyond moral criticism insofar as the player engages with the game in an aesthetic, exploratory way. Drawing on Waltonian theories of fiction, I argue that players often imagine themselves as performing the fictional acts represented in games, ranging from heroic exploits to morally abhorrent deeds. When players do this, they can come to identify with, to varying degrees, their fictional exploits. Strong identification occurs when players interpret their fictional actions as satisfying real desires, such as the desire to harm others. Using examples from players of violent extremist games, as well as mainstream violent games, I show how such identification can arise in practice. Most importantly, I show that in cases of strong identification, all three justifications for ludic amoralism fail.

677. Business Firms and Relational Failures: Situating Relational Goods Beyond Market and Justice Failures

Abstract (original): Abstract Business ethics increasingly explains corporate duties through failure-based theories. 678. A meta-precautionary solution to the dilemma of precaution
Fumagalli, R. (2026). A meta-precautionary solution to the dilemma of precaution. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05856-8
Abstract (original): Abstract This article draws on the cross-disciplinary literature on risk, uncertainty and precaution to address a central dilemma put forward against precautionary principles. According to this dilemma, weak versions of precautionary principles, which hold that policymakers can justifiably adopt precautionary measures even when they lack certainty of impending harm, fail to justify adopting any precautionary measures. Conversely, strong versions of precautionary principles, which hold that policymakers must adopt precautionary measures whenever they face significant risks of harm, yield contradictory policy prescriptions. The article argues that the proffered defences of precautionary principles provide only a partial solution to this dilemma. The article then demonstrates that a meta-precautionary approach, which applies precautionary reasoning to the prescriptions of precautionary principles, can resolve the dilemma and successfully address further prominent objections put forward against precautionary principles.

679. Varieties of self-to-memory dynamics in autobiographical memory: the case of depression

https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05857-7
Abstract (original): Abstract Autobiographical memory and the self are closely connected. Autobiographical memories provide the information needed to form and maintain a stable self-model (memory-to-self). The self, in turn, modulates the construction of autobiographical memories during retrieval (self-to-memory). Past research has mainly focused on the former direction of influence. However, self-to-memory dynamics are being increasingly investigated both in neurotypical and neurodivergent individuals. We contribute to this line of research in two ways. First, we elaborate on the self-memory system framework focusing on self-related processes involved in autobiographical memory retrieval. In particular, we distinguish between long-term and situationally activated aspects of the self, as well as between its representational and agentive aspects. Based on the characteristics of the self in depression, we propose an outline of the self-to-memory dynamics in individuals with depression, drawing on existing empirical evidence. Additionally, we suggest that our framework, adjusted as needed, could be employed to describe self-to-memory dynamics in other mental health conditions.

683. Beyond Skin and Skull: A Dispositional Approach to the Extended Mind
Smart, P. (2026). Beyond Skin and Skull: A Dispositional Approach to the Extended Mind. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10491-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Debates about the extended mind and extended cognition typically focus on the boundaries of cognitive processes and the mechanisms responsible for their realization. This paper develops a different perspective, one that foregrounds the role of dispositional properties such as beliefs, capacities, and abilities. According to the dispositional hypothesis, cases of cognitive extension share a common structure: an entity is credited with a dispositional property whose manifestation is realized by a mechanism that extends beyond the boundaries of that entity. This provides a unified way of understanding otherwise disparate cases of cognitive extension. The dispositional perspective also sheds new light on several familiar issues in the active externalist literature, including cognitive bloat and the criteria used to evaluate putative cases of cognitive extension. More generally, it draws attention to the importance of disposition ascription and the identification of disposition bearers: whether a mechanism qualifies as extended depends not simply on whether it crosses an organismic boundary, but on whether it extends beyond the boundaries of the entity credited with the relevant dispositional property. The resulting framework provides a common basis for understanding both natural and artificial forms of cognitive extension.

684. Capacity-First Philosophy of Psychology
Alexander, C. (2026). Capacity-First Philosophy of Psychology. Topoi.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11245-026-10497-0
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper aims to show that a neo-Aristotelian version of capacity-first philosophy of mind is fruitfully applied to a question which is specific to philosophy of psychology, namely: What counts as an adequate explanation in psychology? Drawing on Kenny, Glock and Schellenberg, the paper argues that the neo-Aristotelian capacity view is built around a set of criteria for the classification of capacities. All capacities are classified according to (1) possession-, (2) exercise-, (3) vehicle-, (4) opportunity-, (5) enablement- and (6) successful employment- criteria. These classification criteria are used to reconstruct the process of de-intellectualizing explanations of psychological phenomena. De-intellectualization targets theoretical assumptions about the circumstances in which the various criteria for classifying capacities are satisfied. The capacity approach offers a tool for regimenting the process of de-intellectualization. It also illustrates the contrast between de-intellectualization and Dennett’s homuncular functionalism. Rather than offering nothing more than conceptual clarity for debates about the metaphysics and epistemology of mind, the neo-Aristotelian capacity view explains why de-intellectualizing explanations is a feature of adequate explanations in psychology.

693. Realism, Idealism, and the Paradox of Subjectivity in Transcendental Phenomenology
Davies‐Barton, T. (2026). Realism, Idealism, and the Paradox of Subjectivity in Transcendental Phenomenology. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70130
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the significance of what Edmund Husserl calls “the paradox of human subjectivity” for the metaphysical realism‐idealism debate in transcendental phenomenology. I distinguish two plausible interpretations of the paradox that are relevant to this debate. I term the first a vicious circle of mutual dependence: the world is characterized as being ontologically dependent on one causally dependent part of the world. I term the second a paradox of bodily self‐constitution: the transcendental subject is defined in such a way that it would be impossible for it to constitute itself as an empirical subject, and yet, in order to constitute the world, it must undergo such a process of self‐objectification. I focus on one prominent version of phenomenology from each side of the realism‐idealism debate, treating them as counterparts insofar as they share three core commitments. I argue that while the main accounts of the subject available to this idealist version generate at least one such paradox, the realist version can explain how one and the same subject is both transcendental and empirical without generating either paradox. My argument therefore provides a substantial prima facie reason to favour this realist version of phenomenology over its idealist counterpart for those who accept their shared commitments and take transcendental phenomenology to have metaphysical implications for the realism‐idealism debate.

695. When disclosure rules are not enough: authorial warrant and judgment in ai-supported scholarship
Mirończuk, M. (2026). When disclosure rules are not enough: authorial warrant and judgment in ai-supported scholarship. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09928-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Editorial policies increasingly require authors to disclose substantive uses of generative AI, but such requirements commonly focus on whether, where, and how a model was used. Even function-specific disclosure may locate a model in the research or writing process without showing how its outputs entered scholarly judgment, how they were evaluated, or whether contributors can reconstruct the relevant reasoning and defend resulting claims without treating those outputs as sufficient justification. Drawing on research ethics, AI disclosure policy, human–model interaction, and researcher formation, this article argues that disclosure documents model use, whereas responsible use requires claim-level authorial warrant: a contributor’s capacity to understand, justify, and defend claims within their contribution. The article distinguishes cognitive delegation, in which model outputs shape substantive choices, interpretations, or justifications, from routine delegation, which does not ordinarily bear on the substance of judgment. Its conceptual contribution is to link the epistemic role of outputs that shape judgment to the function-appropriate evaluation required to justify decisions and retain warrant for resulting claims. It then repurposes disclosure categories as a claim-sensitive ex ante heuristic, mapping six recurrent research functions to risks, forms of oversight, and formative questions for recognising cognitive delegation and selecting function-appropriate evaluation. The article contributes to the ethics of information technology by clarifying when AI-supported scholarship remains under responsible human judgment and when cognitive delegation threatens the epistemic basis of authorship.

696. Protesting Together
Bryan, A. (2026). Protesting Together. Ethical Theory Moral Pract.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10677-026-10586-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Political protests are almost always collective endeavours, in which many people come together to jointly express some claim or demand. The power of those protests lies, in part, in their collectivity. But this collectivity also means that we may end up protesting alongside others who, though they endorse the shared claim the protest is expressing, also make other claims which are deeply unjust or harmful during the protest. This paper explores whether protesting alongside such others makes one complicit in the promotion or expression of those unjust or harmful claims, and whether we therefore have a duty not to participate (or to cease ongoing participation) in such protests. I identify two moral risks involved in protest which may ground such a duty – that we inadvertently amplify those views, and that we are unable to engage in effective counterspeech in response to them – and submit that when these risks are especially high, protestors have a pro tanto duty not to participate. However, I also show that the resources provided by different organisational forms protestors may use can help mitigate these risks.

701. Learning alone: language models, overreliance, and the goals of education
Dung, L. & Balg, D. (2026). Learning alone: language models, overreliance, and the goals of education. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01464-x
Abstract (original): Abstract The development and ubiquitous availability of large language model based systems (LLMs) poses a plurality of potentials and risks for education in schools and universities. In this paper, we provide an analysis and discussion of the overreliance concern as one specific risk: that students might fail to acquire important skills, or be inhibited in the acquisition of these skills, because they overly rely on LLMs. We use the distinction between local and global goals of education to guide our investigation. In our view, the harm of LLM overreliance is specifically that it severs the connection between local educational goals (e.g., successfully solving a specific kind of math problem) and global educational goals (e.g., acquiring general skills for mathematics). Based on this analysis, we sketch three possible responses to the overreliance concern: preserving both local and global goals while making minimal changes to educational practice (a conservative approach), changing global and local educational goals (a revisionary perspective), and preserving global educational goals while changing local goals (an in-between view). Since different types of responses have different benefits and weaknesses, we think that it is likely that an optimal response ultimately combines elements from all three types.

704. Assertion without a Speaker: Testimony, Tracking, and Large Language Models
Saxena, A. (2026). Assertion without a Speaker: Testimony, Tracking, and Large Language Models. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10160
Abstract (original): Abstract Large language models produce assertion-shaped outputs that often bring their users to true belief. I argue for two conclusions of different strengths: that on the best current evidence, these systems do not transmit testimonial knowledge and that hearers are not now positioned to accord their outputs testimonial uptake, which does not wait on the first. From Burge, Williamson, Goldberg, Faulkner, and Lackey, I derive a Tracking Minimum: a source transmits testimonial knowledge that p only if the truth of p , or the evidential support for p , figures in the explanation of its producing that assertion, and does so in virtue of the source’s own production process rather than of a proxy on which that process depends. It is this endogeneity requirement, not any modal profile, that the five accounts share and current systems fail. The argument engages Burge’s extension of his framework to computational sources, the interpretability literature, and the published case for AI testimony, whose dispute with mine turns on an empirically decidable question, for which an experiment is specified. The positive proposal places LLM outputs within an extended epistemology of instruments, with a result that has gone unremarked: a working barometer satisfies the condition these systems fail.

Abstract (original): Abstract Focusing on the UK-Mauritius Chagos Agreement, this article maps the normative effects of advisory opinions of the International Court of Justice (“ICJ”). 709. (Absolute) Generality and (Partial) Grounding

https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag081
Abstract (original): Abstract What constitutes a full ground for a general fact? Others argue that a totality fact must therefore be added to complete the ground. I demonstrate that this debate rests on an ambiguity regarding the very concept of a general fact. Once disambiguated, we see that on one interpretation, the shared initial assumption is false, so both derived views are mistaken. 710. Morgenbesser’s Intuition, Infinitely Many Coins, and Substitution
Wang, J. (2026). Morgenbesser’s Intuition, Infinitely Many Coins, and Substitution. Analysis.
https://doi.org/10.1093/analys/anag083
Abstract (original): Abstract While an indeterministic coin was in the air, you declined to bet that it would land heads. The coin landed heads. Morgenbesser intuited that if you had bet heads, you would have won. Goodsell (2022) argues, through a thought experiment involving infinitely many coins, that Morgenbesser’s intuition is mistaken. Crucially, he assumes the Substitution principle, according to which necessarily equivalent propositions have the same counterfactual consequences. I argue that Goodsell’s argument proves too much, by analysing a closely related thought experiment. My conclusion is two-fold. Goodsell’s original thought experiment fails to put pressure on Morgenbesser’s intuition. Yet my thought experiment, inspired by his, may succeed in proving Substitution false.

711. What Do Mini‐Publics Prove?
Kogelmann, B. (2026). What Do Mini‐Publics Prove?. PPA.
https://doi.org/10.1111/papa.70037
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Lottocracy is a novel political arrangement that replaces elected politicians with ordinary citizens selected at random. It is unlike any political system that currently exists, so it is natural to have anxiety about how it would work in practice. To mollify these concerns, many lottocrats point to the successful performance of mini‐publics as evidence that lottocracy would work well, if tried. This inference resembles a claim about external validity, I argue, and develop a framework for evaluating it by relying on work from the social sciences and philosophy of science about when such claims are warranted. With this framework in hand, I show that mini‐publics teach us little about how lottocracy would work in practice, although the strength of this conclusion depends on which specific version of lottocracy is under consideration. Mini‐publics, however, do provide evidence about the abilities of ordinary citizens more generally, undermining some of the strongest claims about their lack of political competence. The framework developed here applies to a broader class of arguments in political philosophy: arguments that use the performance of existing institutions to predict how similar but untried institutions would perform.

712. Polarising punchlines: The influence of intergroup humour on affective political polarisation
Sleiman, Y. et al. (2026). Polarising punchlines: The influence of intergroup humour on affective political polarisation. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261496123
Abstract (original): How does political humour influence affective polarisation? In today’s media landscape, humour is increasingly used to ridicule political opponents, potentially deepening partisan divides. At the same time, humour may mitigate polarisation by evoking positive emotions and softening reactions to political critique. To date, it remains unclear whether intergroup political humour affects polarisation differently from equivalent non-humorous content. We address this question using a survey experiment in Great Britain ( n = 2011 ) with a 2×3 factorial design varying in message tone (humorous or non-humorous) and target (in-group, out-group or non-political). We find that serious in-group criticism increases affective polarisation, as measured by trait-based evaluations. Notably, this effect is significantly attenuated when the same messages are delivered humorously. Humour, therefore, reduces polarisation in situations that would otherwise provoke defensive reactions among group members. Mediation analyses provide suggestive evidence that this effect operates through increased message discounting and reduced negative emotional responses.

713. Differential Disruption and Human Flourishing: Towards a Capability-Based Approach for Assessing Socially Disruptive Technologies
Prins, G. (2026). Differential Disruption and Human Flourishing: Towards a Capability-Based Approach for Assessing Socially Disruptive Technologies. Science and Engineering Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11948-026-00635-7
Abstract (original): Abstract While the concept of differential disruption has attracted growing attention in the ethics of technology, its normative dimensions remain underexplored. Current literature primarily uses differential disruption as a descriptive concept to highlight how Socially Disruptive Technologies (SDTs) impact diversely situated groups differentially, but rarely interrogates the normative implications of these differential impacts. Building on recent calls to bring to the fore the normative potential of differential disruption, this paper argues that assessments of SDTs must move beyond analyses of epistemic injustice alone, and consider how epistemic injustices are deeply entangled with concerns of wellbeing and human flourishing. The paper shows that epistemic injustices generated by SDTs may constrain people’s access to resources and opportunities necessary for flourishing. It further argues that SDT development and assessment practices often rely on generalised assumptions about wellbeing that risk epistemically excluding marginalised modes of flourishing. Consequently, it argues that ethical assessments of SDTs require a heterogeneous and context-sensitive evaluative framework. To this end, the paper argues for a capability-based approach to the ethical assessment of SDTs. By centring what people are actually able to be and do, as well as attending to people’s diverse conversion factors that shape whether SDTs can be converted into valuable capabilities and functionings, a capability-based framework equips ethicists of technology with the conceptual tools to critically assess when, how, and for whom SDTs may be disruptive, while being sensitive to heterogeneous interpretations and experiences of flourishing.

716. Middleism, but not necessarily
Ferrari, G. (2026). Middleism, but not necessarily. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02606-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Mereological Middleism (MM) is the view that fundamental objects are mereologically intermediate. Together with Mereological Bottomism (MB), the view that fundamental objects are mereologically atomic, and Mereological Topism (MT), the view that fundamental objects are mereologically coatomic, MM constitutes one of the three standard answers to Schaffer’s Question of Fundamental Mereology (QFM). In this paper, I present a novel argument against the claim that MM is necessarily true ( Nec -MM). If one assumes that any answer to the QFM, if true, is necessarily true ( QFM Necessitarianism ), then MM itself is under threat. To support this conclusion, I develop two sets of arguments: the Arguments from Atoms and Quasi-Atoms, and the Arguments from Dependence and Non-Dependence. I further show that the middleist faces a dilemma, since the most promising strategy for resisting the first set of arguments undermines the most promising strategy for resisting the second, and vice versa. After considering and rejecting several responses, I argue that Nec -MM should be abandoned as a naïve formulation of the necessity of middleism and replaced with a more sophisticated formulation that preserves the core commitments of MM. This provides the middleist with a principled reason to reject QFM Necessitarianism , thereby avoiding the argument. Finally, I suggest that analogous reformulations are available to proponents of MB and MT, and that this has important implications for how the QFM itself ought to be understood.

717. Requiring vs. justifying: derived, not basic
Schroeder, M. (2026). Requiring vs. justifying: derived, not basic. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02635-6
Abstract (original): Abstract The dual ranking model holds that when what is permissible or required is determined by the balance of competing reasons, different roles are played by the reasons that require and the reasons that permit. Proponents of this idea often go further, and accept the multidimensional view that this is because there is a deep or fundamental distinction between two kinds of reasons, or between two kinds of weight of reasons, or between two dimensions along which reasons have weight. This paper defends the dual ranking model but offers several arguments against the multidimensional view. The distinction between two roles for reasons in the determination of what is required and what is permissible can be satisfactorily ultimately explained in terms of a single dimension of reasons' weight.

718. On some alleged problems for the distinction between two roles for reasons
Gert, J. (2026). On some alleged problems for the distinction between two roles for reasons. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02639-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Selim Berker argues that the distinction between requiring and justifying weight or strength is not in good standing. In this response I argue that all his worries, when directed at my view, can be answered. It is only certain mistaken or optional interpretive moves that lead him to have doubts in the first place. He interprets me as holding that the criterion for one reason having greater weight in a role than another involves counterfactual conditionals thought of in terms of possible worlds, which would leave me open to the charge than I am committing a version of Shope’s Conditional Fallacy. He also interprets my talk of intelligible objects of human motivation as being so wide as to include absolutely anything we can imagine a person being motivated by, even if it is the result of a “bizarre fixation”. And he interprets me as agreeing with his controversial monistic view of reasons, such that it makes sense to say that all reasons, by their nature, can be taken together to yield an overall verdict as to what one ought to do, simpliciter. In contrast to this, I hold that moral reasons are considerations that make systematic contributions to moral status, legal reasons are considerations that make systematic contributions to legal status, and generic practical reasons – which are the reasons I am concerned with – are considerations that make systematic contribution to rational status.

719. Autonomy: how lesser goods can be justifying reasons
Tucker, C. (2026). Autonomy: how lesser goods can be justifying reasons. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02643-6
Abstract (original): Abstract It seems permissible to eat out instead of doing more good by donating to a charity that effectively prevents suffering. Many ethicists infer that partiality can be permissible, that it can be permissible to choose less good over more. A common thought is that our autonomy somehow explains such permissible partiality. Past attempts to vindicate this natural thought collapse the role of autonomy into the agent’s wellbeing or something that is itself morally assessable, such as attitudes, projects, or dispositions.
