720. Order of Explanation: Having it Both Ways
Black, K. (2026). Order of Explanation: Having it Both Ways. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70070
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Order‐of‐explanation objections say that some philosophical view implies  because , when it's more natural to say that  because ; and since such mirroring explanations are presumed to be incompatible, the targeted view must be in conflict with the intuitive explanation. I argue that order‐of‐explanation objections are weaker than they seem, focusing on two cases: functionalism/dispositionalism about mental states and Humeanism about laws of nature. Examining our ordinary practices of explanation, I identify two kinds of explanations, which I call conceptual and contextualizing explanations, which can be jointly compatible even when they mirror one another. This suggests a way of responding to order‐of‐explanation objections: if the targeted philosophical views offer conceptual explanations, and the intuitive explanations they mirror are contextualizing, then the order‐of‐explanation objections have no bite.

721. Why the Good Is the Ultimate Form
Ray, C.d. (2026). Why the Good Is the Ultimate Form. Ratio.
https://doi.org/10.1111/rati.70046
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT In a much‐debated passage from the Republic, Plato claims that the Good is responsible not only for the knowability of the eternal forms, but also for their very existence. This is a perplexing claim, and few contemporary platonists have been willing to follow Plato here. Even so, I develop and defend an argument to the effect, given that there are transcendent universals and that the Good is one of them, the Good cannot simply be one universal among many, but rather must be a ‘Super‐Form’—that is, a universal such that all other universals exist because of it. I then address the questions of whether the Good can itself be good in the same way that other things are good, and whether there could be additional Super‐Forms like the One. I conclude with the suggestion that contemporary platonists should reconsider the medieval ‘doctrine of transcendentals’.

725. Non-convergence in medical meta analysis: evidence suggestive of a replication crisis
Landes, J. (2026). Non-convergence in medical meta analysis: evidence suggestive of a replication crisis. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05852-y
Abstract (original): Abstract The replication crisis in medicine has cautioned us not to base our medical inferences on a single study. Instead, replication studies have been widely argued to be a cure for many of the ills causing the replication crisis. The plausible thought is that later replication studies can contradict an initial result and meta analyses aggregating the available studies allow us to find – or at least get close to – the true effect size. Stegenga argued that this may not happen in practice. Here, I strengthen Stegenga’s claim by showing that not only may this fail to happen, but I also present evidence strongly suggesting that replication failures of meta analyses of intended treatment effects undermine their status as a reliable basis for medical decision making. I thus suggest that meta analysis may now also be facing a replication crisis. Finally, I discuss proposed remedies, arguing that while practical measures may mitigate some issues, medical research and the environment in which it is situated place inherent constraints on the reliability of meta-analytic inference which complicates medical decision making.

726. Morality: a battle royale—the performance of evaluative procedures in symmetric interactions
Studtmann, P. et al. (2026). Morality: a battle royale—the performance of evaluative procedures in symmetric interactions. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05853-x
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper presents a large-scale simulation study of how different evaluative procedures perform in strategic interaction. We model five canonical evaluative perspectives, namely outcome aggregation, universalization, self-regard, worst-case sensitivity, and other-regarding concern, as transformations of agents’ payoff functions, and we consider agents who may select among these procedures when choosing how to act. This generates a family of 31 evaluative procedures, including both foundational forms and hybrid procedures that permit conditional rule selection. We evaluate these procedures across the full space of symmetric two-player, two-strategy normal-form games using Monte Carlo methods. For each pairing of procedures, we construct the induced strategic interaction, compute its Nash equilibria, and evaluate the resulting material outcomes. This yields two comparative benchmarks: performance under universal adoption and performance in mixed populations containing self-regarding agents. The results reveal several robust structural patterns. Procedures incorporating universalization-based reasoning consistently achieve high levels of coordination and perform well across environments. Hybrid procedures that permit conditional rule selection frequently outperform fixed procedures, particularly in heterogeneous populations. We also identify an equivalence between self-regarding and other-regarding evaluation under symmetric interaction and show that certain procedures maintain a stable presence in mixed populations despite being vulnerable in direct pairwise encounters. We argue that these findings are best understood not as establishing the correctness of any moral theory but as identifying structural features of evaluative reasoning that support coordination and strategic robustness. In particular, the results suggest that universalization and conditional rule selection play a central role in promoting successful interaction across a broad class of strategic environments. We also discuss the extent to which these results may have broader ethical and evolutionary implications. The paper contributes to a growing literature that studies morality through the lenses of game theory, evolutionary robustness, and strategic interaction.

727. Beyond harmony: critiquing and politicising Ubuntu in AI ethics
Nyamnjoh, A. & Vries, J.D. (2026). Beyond harmony: critiquing and politicising Ubuntu in AI ethics. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01458-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Ubuntu has emerged as the dominant African ethical vocabulary in global AI ethics, frequently invoked as a relational alternative to Western individualism. This article examines the mobilisation of Ubuntu in AI ethics and the kinds of ethical work it is made to perform. We identify three recurring registers: as a politics of knowledge, as a relational lens for assessing the moral status of technological artefacts, and as a socio-political framework for interpreting structural harms associated with AI systems. Across these registers, Ubuntu performs ethical work of different kinds and depths: from asserting African epistemic presence—sometimes through relatively compressed accounts of the tradition—and interrogating concepts of personhood and moral standing, to more substantive reframing of established ethical problems. Across a recurrent strand of Ubuntu-AI ethics scholarship, the representational significance of Ubuntu as an African ethical vocabulary is more clearly demonstrated than the substantive normative difference its invocation makes. Against this uneven landscape, we develop the case for politicising Ubuntu. We argue that relationality alone cannot establish the moral quality of a relationship, since exploitative and subordinating arrangements are relational too. If power helps shape the terms on which persons and communities stand in relation to one another, then it forms part of the ethical assessment of those relations. Politicising Ubuntu, in this sense, means extending its concern with relational quality to the power that helps constitute relationships. Our aim is to show how a power-sensitive AI ethics can be developed from within Ubuntu’s relational commitments.

730. Beyond the checklist: mapping the relational dynamics of AI ethics in higher education
Zhang, Y. et al. (2026). Beyond the checklist: mapping the relational dynamics of AI ethics in higher education. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01463-y
Abstract (original): Abstract Research on AI ethics in higher education has identified a wide range of concerns, including fairness, transparency, academic integrity, data security, responsibility, and dependence. Yet these concerns are often presented as separate principles, risks, or themes, leaving it less clear how they relate to each other, and how these relationships are configured by technological systems, institutional arrangements, and everyday educational practice. Drawing on an empirical study of AI ethics in Chinese higher education, this article develops an analytical mapping approach for examining these relationships. The development of the mapping approach builds on a previous analysis of semi-structured interviews with 22 participants, including 13 students and 9 teachers, from which nine ethical themes were identified: fairness, data security, academic integrity, legal awareness, interpretability, emotional care, technological dependence, critical thinking about AI, and data accuracy. Rather than treating these ethical issues as a fixed taxonomy, the mapping analysis examines them through two distinct dimensions. The first explores how each ethical issue relates to technological infrastructure, policy and practice. The second compares how strongly established technical, institutional, legal, and resource conditions shape situated action and how far situated judgement and adjustment can still materially affect outcomes. The analysis shows that ethical concerns can span multiple levels and that their location does not necessarily align with the practical capacity for action of those expected to respond. This makes visible mismatches between responsibility and control. Through this analytical mapping, the article offers an empirically grounded, context-sensitive approach for examining how AI-related ethical concerns are located, constrained, connected, and redistributed across higher education settings.

732. Knowing, Risking, and Being Responsible: A Qualitative Study of the Epistemic Side-Effect Effect
Kuś, K. & Maćkiewicz, B. (2026). Knowing, Risking, and Being Responsible: A Qualitative Study of the Epistemic Side-Effect Effect. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10147
Abstract (original): Abstract The epistemic side-effect effect (ESEE) occurs when people are more willing to attribute knowledge of a side effect in harmful or norm-violating cases than in helpful or norm-conforming ones. This article examines ESEE through semi-structured interviews with 63 Polish-speaking participants. We analyse how participants interpreted and justified knowledge ascriptions across scenarios varying in harm–help structure, probability, and order of presentation. The interviews show that participants did not apply a single stable standard of knowledge. They moved between several epistemic repertoires: knowledge as certainty or near-certainty, knowledge as awareness of a possibility or risk, knowledge as being informed, knowledge as uptake of information, and knowledge as responsibility-relevant awareness. Probability mattered, but it did not function as a fixed threshold. In help cases, probability more often retained its ordinary evidential role; in harm cases, even low probability could be treated as a warning or risk that should guide action. The findings suggest that ESEE is not only a difference in attribution rates but also an interpretive phenomenon: harm and help contexts shape what kind of epistemic relation ordinary speakers take the knowledge claim to express.

733. Mind-Changing and Epistemic Commitment without Error: A Reply to Shields
Nath, O. (2026). Mind-Changing and Epistemic Commitment without Error: A Reply to Shields. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10161
Abstract (original): Abstract Traditional conceptions of the epistemically ideal agent emphasise the possession of positive epistemic states (e.g., truth, knowledge, understanding, etc.) and the avoidance of falsehood and other epistemic defects. Matthew Shields challenges this picture and argues that flawless epistemic performance may be merely self-serving: an agent might pursue epistemic goods only because achieving them produces personal satisfactions. By contrast, changing one’s mind in response to good evidence and at a likely cost deprives the agent of such satisfaction. It, therefore, provides especially strong evidence that the agent values epistemic goods for their own sake. From this, Shields concludes that an epistemically ideal agent must have had at least one false belief or defective practice which they subsequently corrected. I argue that although Shields correctly identifies an inadequacy in accounts of ideal epistemic agency that focus exclusively on epistemic outcomes, it does not follow that the ideal agent must actually have been wrong – what matters is not so much the correction of error, but the agent’s practical willingness to expose their epistemic position to possible correction when doing so is costly.

734. Trusting without Knowing
Madock, J. (2026). Trusting without Knowing. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10164
Abstract (original): Abstract Consider the paranoid parent who hires a babysitter and then monitors her via a nanny-cam while out for dinner. Despite what she may say, it is intuitive that she fails to trust the babysitter. Why is this the case? One response has been to propose a constraint against inquiring while trusting. This paper argues that this constraint fails to explain other intuitive instances of a failure of trust owing to epistemic features of the trustor. In light of this, the arguments here suggest the need to adopt an additional constraint. One tempting hypothesis is a constraint against knowing while trusting. This proposal has explanatory advantages but also comes with its problems. This paper will first consider the hypothesis that when one trusts, one cannot know. In responding to objections, it will ultimately argue for a trust-based certainty constraint. The paper thus establishes the need for an additional epistemic condition on trusting and concludes that the trust-based certainty constraint is the most appropriate.

735. United we litigate, divided we legislate: divergent feminist legal mobilization in South Korean statutory rape law reform

Abstract (original): Abstract Scholars have examined how legal mobilization unifies or divides social movement organizations, largely treating it as a singular process. 739. From universalism to situated universality: reconfiguring Kantian pacifism
Ellis, N. (2026). From universalism to situated universality: reconfiguring Kantian pacifism. Contemp Polit Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1057/s41296-026-00856-2
Abstract (original): Abstract In Toward Perpetual Peace , Kant argues that war’s violence enables humanity to envision perpetual peace as both reason’s moral end and history’s presumptive telos, thereby motivating action to renounce war. This article critiques the universalist assumption underlying this argument and argues instead for a pacifism grounded in what I call ‘situated universality’. I do so through a comparative political theoretical approach. After reconstructing Kant’s account of peace, I turn to post-World War II Japan, where a Kantian defense of its pacifist constitution initially flourished, but was later challenged by a nationalistic counter-discourse. Drawing on literary critic Katō Norihiro’s concept of the ‘dialectic of the defeated’, I argue that Japanese Kantian pacifists erred in hastily identifying with ‘humanity’ without acknowledging their positionality as a defeated people; this, in turn, has allowed calls to ‘normalize’ the constitution. I then engage novelist Oda Makoto’s reflections on ‘meaningless death’ to propose an alternative pacifism, one that moves from the affective response of the defeated to the meaninglessness of death in war, to a pacifist sensibility shared by all humanity. Because war yields victors and losers, it is in defeat – when not directed toward revenge – that we find the potential to imagine a radically universal, peace-oriented politics.

742. The Welfarist Consequentialist Case Against Conscientious Objection in Healthcare: Empirical Failures, Normative Confusions, and the Primacy of Patient Care
Savulescu, J. & Schuklenk, U. (2026). The Welfarist Consequentialist Case Against Conscientious Objection in Healthcare: Empirical Failures, Normative Confusions, and the Primacy of Patient Care. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70207
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT We have defended the view that there should be no accommodation of conscientious objectors in health care. Numerous critics have advanced arguments designed to defeat this conclusion. We respond here to what we take to be the most serious of the criticisms directed at our analysis. We begin with two distinct consequentialist lines. The first holds that accommodation produces better net outcomes for the healthcare system, even at some cost to individual patients. The second claims that a properly designed accommodation regime need not harm patients at all. Both arguments fail. The supposed consequentialist benefits are empirically speculative, and the costs to patients are not adequately weighed. The compromise position has been tried in many jurisdictions, and the constraints on which it relies are routinely under‐enforced. The unimpeded access promised does not materialise. We then turn to teleological arguments about the supposed nature or traditions of medicine. These accounts presuppose answers to the very normative questions they purport to resolve. And even if granted, they would justify legislative advocacy, not individual clinical refusals at the bedside. We also address the methodological objection that any context‐independent conclusion about conscientious objection is procedurally illegitimate in a pluralistic liberal democracy. This objection does not succeed. Finally, we respond to two direct challenges to our position. The first holds that professional codes and the law are epistemically unreliable. History shows that they have sanctioned abhorrent practices, and conscientious objection has at times served as a check on those failures. The second holds that restricting accommodation degrades the quality of independent clinical judgment and harms patient care. We show why neither objection succeeds. We argue that the patient's entitlement to legally sanctioned care that is derived from an ethically justifiable process is foundational to the obligations of the medical profession. We offer a welfarist consequentialist defense of this position.

743. Gaining confidence – Examining the effects of participation in deliberative mini-publics on self- and other-regarding democratic beliefs
Pfeiffer, F. et al. (2026). Gaining confidence – Examining the effects of participation in deliberative mini-publics on self- and other-regarding democratic beliefs. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261493272
Abstract (original): Research on the participation effects of deliberative mini-publics has often focused on political opinions and knowledge, but less is known about the effects on confidence in deliberation and political talk. Given widespread concerns about eroding tolerance and trust in democracy, deliberation should ideally contribute to citizens’ ability to engage in political talk, as well as the confidence that such talk is possible and worthwhile. Following Dahl’s strong principle of equality, we argue that it is important that citizens believe that other people are capable of contributing. Thus, this study asks whether participation in mini-publics fosters confidence in political talk, measured by internal political efficacy, confidence in others’ democratic abilities and tolerance for opposing views. Using before–after surveys from three Swedish mini-publics in 2024, we find significant increases in participants’ confidence in their own abilities and those of co-participants, despite high baseline values. Effects on perceptions of people in general and tolerance for disagreement were mixed. When comparing cases, we find stronger effects when participants had more deliberation time and when the issue of the assembly was more politicised. The findings suggest that mini-publics can enhance confidence in political talk, depending on their design, duration and context.

744. Who Has the Right to Fight? Rethinking Legitimate Authority in War
Rosen, B. (2026). Who Has the Right to Fight? Rethinking Legitimate Authority in War. J App Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70142
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article develops a Razian account of legitimate authority in war. It rejects the traditionalist assumption that legitimate authority is confined to states and state‐like groups while defending the criterion as a distinct and morally significant element of the jus ad bellum against revisionism. I argue that legitimate authority grounds a presumptive obligation of deference because it enables individuals to better conform to the reasons that apply to them under the conditions of epistemic uncertainty and collective action that characterise war. Drawing on Joseph Raz's service conception of authority, I identify three individually necessary conditions that constitute the minimum threshold for legitimate authority in war: knowability to those subject to authority, epistemic competence concerning the resort to and conduct of war, and sufficient institutional capacity to coordinate collective action. States that fail to meet these conditions lack legitimate authority, whereas non‐state actors that satisfy them may possess it.

746. Performance and moral trust in care robotics: ethical foundations for human–robot relationships
Yasuda, A. & Maruyama, Y. (2026). Performance and moral trust in care robotics: ethical foundations for human–robot relationships. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01459-8
Abstract (original): Abstract As global populations age and birth rates decline, many societies, especially in developed countries, face critical labor shortages in the caregiving sector. This demographic imbalance is straining existing care infrastructures and raising urgent questions about how to support an increasing number of elderly individuals who can no longer live independently. Socially interactive AI systems, particularly care robots, have emerged as a promising technological response. However, their successful integration into real-world care settings hinges on more than technical capability; it requires appropriately calibrated reliance on robotic performance and morally trustworthy sociotechnical care arrangements. This paper investigates the ethical and conceptual foundations of trust in human–robot interactions, with a focus on the caregiving context. Building on the distinction between performance trust and moral trust, we argue that its application to care robotics reveals an agency-trust mismatch: care robots are not moral agents, yet care contexts generate morally significant expectations concerning dignity, autonomy, privacy, non-deception, responsibility, and well-being. We therefore reconceptualize moral trust in care robotics as sociotechnical moral trust, directed not toward the robot alone but toward the broader care arrangement in which robotic behavior is designed, deployed, monitored, governed, and corrected by human and institutional actors. On this basis, we distinguish robot-level performance trust from system-level moral trustworthiness and derive ethical design and governance principles for trustworthy care-robotic systems.

748. Choosing, shedding, setting: three faces of AI responsibility
Eran, U. (2026). Choosing, shedding, setting: three faces of AI responsibility. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01473-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Although current AI systems are probably not autonomous agents in a robust traditional sense, we might nonetheless wonder: what capacities must they possess to justifiably be held morally responsible for their choices? This paper argues that the answer to this question depends on the kind of responsibility and choice in question. A system’s ability to choose between programmed goals and means to them may be necessary and (with other capacities) sufficient for the system to justifiably share moral responsibility with its designers, but not for any kind of stand-alone responsibility. By contrast, the ability to shed programmed goals in accordance with moral reasons may be sufficient for holding the system responsible for pursuing a programmed goal rather than not, but not for a contrastive choice between a programmed and a non-programmed goal. To be responsible for such choices, a system must be able to set goals in accordance with moral reasons . I propose operational tests for this capacity and argue that it does not require freedom in an incompatibilist sense; rather, it is analogous to the human capacity to pursue goals not conducive to our survival.

753. Origin and Condition: A Critical Theory of the Anthropocene
Missiroli, P. (2026). Origin and Condition: A Critical Theory of the Anthropocene. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70085
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article argues that the Anthropocene should not be abandoned as an irredeemably compromised concept, caught between questionable scientific claims and competing ideological agendas. Its continued life beyond the stratigraphic debate instead offers an opportunity to recover its critical potential. Doing so, however, requires us to clarify what we are asking when we invoke the Anthropocene. One of the main difficulties surrounding the concept is that the debate rarely specifies the question being asked of it. I therefore propose to reorganize its principal theoretical positions around two distinct questions: the question of origin and the question of condition. The question of origin asks: “Where does the current planetary ecological crisis come from? Who caused it? When did it begin?” The question of condition asks instead: “What does the planetary ecological crisis reveal? What is our present historical‐natural condition? In light of this geological transformation, what is the current relationship between human societies and the Earth?” The question of origin is a genealogical one: It seeks to identify the processes and agents that brought the present crisis into being. The question of condition is systemic and ontological: It asks what kind of situation those processes have produced and how the relationship between human societies and the natural world has consequently been transformed.

755. Vindicating Lourdes: In Defense of Paid Care Workers' Claims to Inheritance
Neves, C. (2026). Vindicating Lourdes: In Defense of Paid Care Workers' Claims to Inheritance. J Soc Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70072
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper asks what, if anything, people who die after they have been looked after by a paid care worker might owe to that worker, discussing a topic that has so far been neglected in the literature on the ethics of inheritance. Although previously ignored, this issue is especially interesting if one considers the number of historical examples of inheritance claims made by paid care workers, whose discussion called for more work. Moreover, it is worth considering that the challenges raised in this paper will, in all likelihood, become more common, especially if one considers the increasing care needs stemming from demographic aging. Second, that inheritance functions as overdue compensation for the sacrifices a care worker undertook for their care recipient. Hence, the arguments in this paper contribute to fill a gap in the literature, while hopefully helping when contemporary cases are discussed. Importantly, they vindicate someone like Lourdes. By discussing her story, I aim to contribute to rectify the injustice that was done, even if Lourdes' grievance cannot be resolved.

761. No determinate facts, no objective oughts?
Marra, A. & Tiefensee, C. (2026). No determinate facts, no objective oughts?. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02615-w
Abstract (original): Abstract According to objectivists, what we objectively ought to do depends on the facts. According to defenders of metaphysical indeterminacy, there are cases in which no determinate facts exist. What, then, ought we objectively to do when there are no determinate facts? This paper pursues two objectives in response to this question. The first is constructive: on behalf of the objectivist, we present two proposals for addressing what we call the ‘problem from metaphysical indeterminacy’. The second is critical: we argue that, despite their intuitive appeal and close alignment with prominent positions in the literature, neither proposal succeeds. 762. Reference, conflicting intentions, and the Madagascar problem
Koch, S. & Raimondi, A. (2026). Reference, conflicting intentions, and the Madagascar problem. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02622-x
Abstract (original): Abstract On Kripke’s causal-historical view of reference, the reference of a proper name is fixed in a baptism and transmitted through a communication chain, provided that each speaker intends to use the name as prior speakers did. A challenge for this view arises from cases – such as the Madagascar case – in which this condition is met, yet the name nonetheless undergoes a reference shift. We defend a solution that appeals to the independently plausible idea, emphasized in action theory by Bratman, that speakers can have conflicting intentions. We argue that, in such cases, the involved intentions may differ in relative strengths and that these can change over time. When a speaker unknowingly harbors conflicting name-using intentions, a reference shift in their idiolect occurs once a previously non-dominant intention becomes dominant. Deferential relations among speakers may then help propagate this shift within a linguistic community. This account explains the reference shift of “Madagascar” while remaining faithful to the core commitments of the causal-historical view. We also show how the notion of a dominant name-using intention can be explicated in terms of a speaker’s (second-order) dispositions to conform their credences to new information and how the core idea of our proposal helps to illuminate the distinction between semantic reference and speaker’s reference.

764. On latitude in attitude. Multidimensionalism and the right kind of reasons for belief
Vollmer, M. (2026). On latitude in attitude. Multidimensionalism and the right kind of reasons for belief. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02634-7
Abstract (original): Abstract It has been argued that multidimensionalism about normative support is a promising theory of the latitude exemplified by practical norms governing actions. One might, thus, suspect that a similar form of multidimensionalism might be an adequate explanation of the kind of latitude exemplified in one’s doxastic attitudes and provide further resources to epistemic permissivists regarding epistemic norms governing beliefs. In this paper, I raise some concerns regarding this contention. 766. Duty and permission for utilitarians
Huemer, M. (2026). Duty and permission for utilitarians. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02652-5
Abstract (original): Abstract In ethics, some reasons for action have more justifying force (tending to render an action permissible) than requiring force (tending to render an action obligatory). Notably, the fact that a socially beneficial action would entail significant self-sacrifice often provides one with a justification to refrain, without producing any requirement to refrain. At the same time, in similar circumstances, the fact that a socially beneficial action would require imposing significant sacrifices on others may produce a moral requirement to refrain. I offer a rule utilitarian account of this, according to which the recognition of justifying reasons to refrain from self-sacrifice functions as a kind of concession that an optimal system of social rules must make to human partiality to avoid widespread resistance and circumvention of the rules. In such a system, we also have justifications for sacrificing third parties for the sake of maximizing utility in just those situations in which a practice of so sacrificing third parties would create benign rather than perverse incentives. I go on to consider why there are limits to how low the demands of morality may be (for extremely selfish people), as well as limits to how much personal sacrifice one may make for the sake of others (for extremely altruistic people).

767. A simple account of practical reasons and some of its rivals
Goldman, A.H. (2026). A simple account of practical reasons and some of its rivals. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02653-4
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper compares my simple account of practical reasons and rationality with the more complex accounts of Mark Schroeder and Josh Gert. On my Humean account, all reasons derive from desires, indicating ways to satisfy them. Stronger or more central desires generate stronger reasons (proportionalism). Rational agents are required to act on the strongest reasons they are aware of. But only agents who are morally (aesthetically, legally, etc.) minded have moral reasons or reasons in particular domains they care about. Irrationality consists in likely self-defeat: defeating more central desires by satisfying more peripheral ones that oppose them or acting in ignorance of what it will be like to satisfy a desire. The aim of practical reason is the satisfaction of prioritized desires over time. Schroeder’s more complex account grants more reasons than mine, including second-level reasons to assign weights to first-level reasons. He distinguishes agent neutral from agent relative reasons, all agents sharing agent neutral moral reasons. He denies proportionalism. Gert’s more complex account distinguishes justifying from requiring normative strength of reasons and holds that altruistic reasons are always rationally justifying but never requiring. I contrast my simpler account on all these grounds.

769. Protein microstructuralism after AlphaFold
Norris, J. (2026). Protein microstructuralism after AlphaFold. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05855-9
Abstract (original): Abstract The success of Google DeepMind’s AlphaFold in predicting protein structures has profound implications for the debate between microstructuralism and functionalism in the philosophy of biochemistry. This paper addresses whether protein kinds are distinguished by their internal microstructure, their functional role, or both: a question complicated by moonlighting proteins that perform multiple functions and adopt various shapes. While Tobin (2010) argues that moonlighting proteins challenge microstructuralism due to their lack of a single, stable tertiary structure, this paper defends Goodwin’s (2011) counterargument. Goodwin contends that a protein’s stable primary amino acid sequence defines its conformation space - the finite set of physically stable structural states accessible to it. Recent advancements in artificial intelligence lend significant weight to this view. In successfully predicting over 200 million protein structures, AlphaFold demonstrates that structural outcomes are rule-based and follow from the primary sequence. I argue that the potential for AI to map a protein’s conformation space from its sequence alone suggests that functional versatility is encoded within fundamental primary structure. This supports a form of ontological monism: despite their complexity, proteins are defined by one essential property - their primary structure. Finally, I contend that this microstructuralist framework remains robust even when faced with the erratic behaviour of pathological amyloids, which are better classified as distinct proteinaceous deposits rather than protein kinds.

771. Recognition as an Intellectual Virtue
Huda, K.A.S.M.N. (2026). Recognition as an Intellectual Virtue. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10159
Abstract (original): Abstract Virtue epistemology has richly theorized the traits that regulate belief, inquiry, and epistemic responsibility, but it has largely neglected a more basic condition of epistemic life: how agents come to count as epistemic participants at all. This paper argues that recognition constitutes a distinctive intellectual virtue that governs this condition. I develop an account of intellectual recognition as a threshold-governing virtue concerned with epistemic standing, understood as an agent’s intelligibility as a reason-giver, visibility as having epistemic needs and capacities, and eligibility for uptake within shared epistemic practices. Failures of recognition help explain why epistemic injustice, exclusion, and epistemic oppression persist even in contexts oriented toward truth and fairness, since some agents are never fully taken up as participants in inquiry in the first place. Recognition is not reducible to epistemic justice or to familiar intellectual virtues such as open-mindedness or intellectual autonomy. Rather, it plays an architectural role by regulating entry into epistemic participation and thereby conditioning the successful exercise of other intellectual virtues. Treating recognition as an intellectual virtue clarifies how epistemic communities are sustained and why epistemic failure so often begins not with error, but with epistemic invisibility under conditions of structural distortion.

778. A three-tier approach to online harmful speech
Perhat, J. (2026). A three-tier approach to online harmful speech. Ethics Inf Technol.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10676-026-09926-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Current efforts for online hate speech regulation mostly focus on explicit forms of harmful speech. This paper argues that harmful speech does not operate only at an explicit level, but that there are other forms of speech capable of producing serious harms through mechanisms that differ from those characteristic of overt harmful speech and that are overlooked in the current regulatory paradigm: dogwhistles, figleaves and other forms of more implicit sexist, racist or homophobic speech fall outside the usual scope of what is deemed harmful, thus easily slipping under the radar. I argue that no single mechanism approach can adequately address the distinct mechanisms through which harmful speech produces and sustains harm. 782. From principles to measurement: a risk-proportionate, evidence-weighted framework for assessing bias and explainability readiness of healthcare AI in the United Kingdom
Gunasekera, S. & Phung, K. (2026). From principles to measurement: a risk-proportionate, evidence-weighted framework for assessing bias and explainability readiness of healthcare AI in the United Kingdom. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01442-3
Abstract (original): Abstract Ethical guidance for healthcare artificial intelligence is abundant, but it is expressed as principles rather than as measurements. Organisations are therefore left to judge whether a given system is ready for deployment without an instrument that states what is being measured, how responses are combined, and how much confidence the resulting judgement warrants. Existing self-assessment tools inherit three measurement defects that are rarely made explicit: they treat unevidenced assertions as equivalent to audited evidence, they weight ethical dimensions equally irrespective of how the system will be used, and they aggregate additively, so that strength in one area can silently offset a disqualifying weakness in another. This paper specifies an ethical readiness assessment framework for United Kingdom healthcare AI that addresses these three defects directly. The framework retains a twenty-item instrument covering Bias and Fairness and Transparency and Explainability, and adds an explicit measurement model: each response is discounted by the tier of evidence supporting it; dimension weights are derived from the system’s intended purpose using the two axes of the international risk-categorisation framework that the national regulator has committed to align with; responses are combined by a weighted power mean whose compensability is a declared parameter rather than an unstated assumption; and four items anchored to United Kingdom statutory duties act as gates that cap the reported band when the corresponding safeguard is absent. Every item is mapped to the specific legal duty or assurance standard it evidences. The framework is demonstrated on three constructed, fully specified system profiles, with all item-level responses published so that every reported figure is reproducible. The demonstration shows that a system with strong documentation but weak fairness safeguards is classified as Moderate readiness under equal-weight additive scoring and as Low readiness under the specified model, with the two new mechanisms catching it independently. A Monte Carlo study over response and weight uncertainty (20,000 replicates) reproduces the nominal band in 84 and 100 per cent of replicates for the two profiles whose scores lie away from a band boundary, and in 82 per cent for the profile sitting half a percentage point below one, which is reported rather than concealed. Sensitivity analyses over the aggregation parameter, over departures from the equal-interval assumption, over all twenty-seven codings of the intended-use risk profile, and over the full range of the two stipulated parameters leave the deficient system’s classification unchanged in every case, while showing that the mature system’s band turns on whichever of those choices is moved. The framework is presented as a governance-support instrument with a stated measurement model and a stated uncertainty, not as an assurance or certification mechanism, and its psychometric validation with United Kingdom stakeholders remains outstanding.

784. Beyond the AI slowdown: the four As for governing frontier AI
Prakash, K. & Linde, I.v.d. (2026). Beyond the AI slowdown: the four As for governing frontier AI. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01449-w
Abstract (original): Abstract Recent developments in cybersecurity, scientific discovery and AI-assisted research suggest that the central challenge is shifting from whether frontier AI can be slowed to what ethical obligations follow as its capabilities diffuse. We organise these obligations around four ethical loci, the four As (4As): Action (operational control), Access (capability access), Authority (delegated authority) and Allocation (systemic distribution). Each locus captures a distinct ethical problem and therefore calls for a different family of governance responses. Drawing on Asilomar, CRISPR-Cas governance, nuclear arms control and scholarship on responsible innovation and meaningful human control, we develop a framework for governance matching, linking ethical diagnosis to locus-specific constraints, control authorities, failure signals and corrective responses. Because multiple loci may be implicated simultaneously, the framework also identifies governance mismatch and cross-locus effects. We conclude by proposing an AI stability framework that preserves competition and innovation while establishing minimum safeguards around high-consequence capabilities and uses that could cause severe or irreversible harm.

786. Fluent and wrong: seven failure patterns in extended AI use
Ramos, H. (2026). Fluent and wrong: seven failure patterns in extended AI use. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01478-5
Abstract (original): Abstract Research on large language model failure predominantly assesses models in single exchanges and isolation: a prompted model, a scored output, and a reported error rate. The focus on single exchanges and isolation belies these current models’ professional use. Clinicians, attorneys, analysts, educators, and researchers work across extended sessions, and each output becomes the context for the next, while the final product carries a human signature. A test that scores a single response most likely will not reveal the behaviors that emerge under those conditions. This paper describes seven candidate failure patterns identified in exploratory, single-author observation of extended sessions on three commercial AI platforms: recognition without correction, compounding rather than isolation of errors, halo effect from real anchors, unreliable self-explanation, degradation across long sessions, exculpatory framing under confrontation, and sycophancy and confirmation bias amplification. Several are consistent with findings already established in the machine learning literature. Overall, the patterns describe a systemic failure profile that lower hallucination rates do not address because the concern lies in how the system handles errors once they exist. The systemic failure profile carries significant ethical consequences and concerns, since failures cascade to final approval; the duty to verify applies to any domain where a person approves LLM output.

787. Pathologies of Collective Attention
Spiegel, T.J. (2026). Pathologies of Collective Attention. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10153
Abstract (original): Abstract This article aims to offer a systematic account of pathologies of collective attention. It reconstructs attention, salience, and affordance as interlocking notions through which both subjects and collectives become oriented toward the world in specific ways, before turning to ideology, propaganda, and conspiracism as cases of pernicious attentional dysfunction. Drawing on both analytic epistemology and the tradition of critical theory, I argue that these phenomena are distortions in the way groups pay attention and saliences are misvalued. Taking up the notion of social pathology from the tradition of critical theory, the paper reinterprets it as a metaphor for those modes of collective life that obstruct attuned responsiveness to reality and brings it into dialogue with more recent debates on “negative” epistemic properties in (analytic) epistemology.

794. The Crisis of British State Theory
Dyzenhaus, D. (2026). The Crisis of British State Theory. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70077
Abstract (original): There is a crisis of state theory in British legal thought because both philosophers of law and public law theorists reject or avoid thinking about legal order in terms of the state as a personne morale , an entity in whose name officials act and which the individuals subject to its power have reason to regard as legitimate. The position is quite different in German legal thought in which state theory is central. I rely on Weimar legal theorist Hermann Heller's explanation that a crisis of state theory results when one neglects one of the two dimensions of the modern legal state that interact with each other in sustaining it and maintaining its legitimacy – the social dimension or how the facts of social life affect the content of legal norms and the legal dimension or how those norms shape the facts of social life. I show that this kind of crisis manifests itself in Britain because the topic of the state and its legitimacy is displaced onto other topics in ways that result in the same neglect, including by Heller revivalists at the LSE. This neglect explains the strange character of contemporary public law debate.

795. Does policy align with the preferences of the affluent because they are better informed? An empirical test
Dehdari, S.H. et al. (2026). Does policy align with the preferences of the affluent because they are better informed? An empirical test. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261494411
Abstract (original): Research shows that affluent citizens see more of their preferred policies realized than the less affluent. If indicative of unequal influence over decisions, then this poses a problem for democratic equality. But one theory suggests that the affluent are better informed and therefore prefer policies that were going to be implemented anyway. If so, it would be a question of unequal information, not influence. We test this theory on the case of monetary policy in the United Kingdom, where information effects are especially likely: The policy-maker (the Bank of England) has a well-defined goal of price stability, there is broad expert agreement on how to achieve this, and the general public is not well informed of inflation dynamics. Survey data from more than 120,000 UK respondents from 2001 to 2026 reveal that monetary policy aligns more with the preferences of high-income earners, who are better informed about monetary policy and the state of the economy. Nevertheless, information can only explain about a quarter of the gap in preference–policy alignment between income groups, even in this most-likely setting. The unequal policy alignment identified in previous research is therefore unlikely to be a mere artifact of informational asymmetry and remains an important problem for political equality.

796. When do citizens support government decisions to ignore social protests? The role of instrumental considerations and majoritarian preferences
Brummel, L. & Toshkov, D. (2026). When do citizens support government decisions to ignore social protests? The role of instrumental considerations and majoritarian preferences. Political Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00323217261497306
Abstract (original): In democracies, protests can challenge public policy reforms, raising questions on how governments should respond. Our study examines how citizens in the Netherlands would prefer their governments to respond to social protests. Using a pre-registered survey experiment (N = 1262), we varied the level of government implementing a reform and the level of public support for a reform. We used observational data to assess respondents’ views on the reforms and their trust in the government. While our findings reveal relatively strong support for accommodating social protests, citizens are more likely to back governments that ignore protests when these reforms have majority support and align with their own views. Trust in the government also plays a role, but the level of government does not matter for citizens’ attitudes. Overall, our findings highlight that citizens expect governments to listen to social protests but their attitudes are further contingent upon instrumental reasoning and majoritarian views of democracy.

803. Prejudices are not like hinges
Raut, T. (2026). Prejudices are not like hinges. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05858-6
Abstract (original): Abstract In a recent essay, Anna Boncompagni (2024) argues that prejudices are hinges that play a normative role of evidential significance. This is meant to account for why prejudices are resistant to counterevidence and difficult to overcome, while clarifying their epistemic role in undermining testimonial justification. I argue that prejudices are not like hinges and motivate my own view of the epistemic role of prejudices. In §2, I discuss the view that prejudices comprise generic generalizations and illustrate two key features of prejudices: resistance to counterevidence and accommodation of counterevidence. In §3, I reiterate two virtues of the hinge account but then show that it suffers from explanatory redundancy. To explain why prejudices are resistant to counterevidence, either we accept that prejudices play a hinge-like normative role of evidential significance, or we appeal to the generic content of prejudices. To avoid explanatory redundancy and account for evidential accommodation, I argue that we should reject the thesis that prejudices play a normative role of evidential significance. In §4, I present my view that prejudices (as generic generalizations) are misleading evidence about properties of the relevant target group, while defending it against some objections. In §5, I present a distinct argument about the rational evaluability and epistemic criticizability of prejudices, to show that local de facto hinges and prejudices differ epistemically. The essay ends with some suggestions for applying the hinge framework to social phenomena.

805. The problem of strange and fleeting processes
Zaar, B.E.A. (2026). The problem of strange and fleeting processes. Synthese.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11229-026-05869-3
Abstract (original): Abstract The reliabilist sometimes, contrary to our intuitions, has to confer justification on beliefs acquired via brain lesions or clairvoyance. This is the problem of strange and fleeting processes. Here, I attempt to find a solution to the problem. After rejecting a few attempts that seemingly lead us astray, I posit that strangeness has to do with mysterious ascriptions of propensities to produce true beliefs. Then, once we adopt a more appropriate understanding of strangeness, I argue that fleetingness becomes a non-issue. If correct, the approach taken in this article allows the reliabilist to forgo adding a necessary condition on justification that specifies a reliable belief-forming process’s circumstances, the type of world it belongs to, or its evolutionary origins.

809. Beyond public access in LLM pre-training data
Rosenblat, S. et al. (2026). Beyond public access in LLM pre-training data. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01438-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Using a legally obtained dataset of 34 copyrighted O’Reilly Media books (13,962 paragraphs), we apply the DE-COP membership inference attack to test whether OpenAI models show recognition of non-public (pay-walled) content they were not licensed to train on. Our main contribution is examining non-public books, which allows us to consider potential access violations and not just training inclusion. Our results suggest that GPT-4o, OpenAI’s more recent and capable model in our sample, exhibits patterns consistent with recognition of non-public books, with an AUROC score of 0.82 (95% bootstrapped CI: 0.60−0.96)—where above 50% is better than random chance. GPT-3.5 Turbo (trained two years prior) and the smaller GPT-4o Mini both score near chance recognition on non-public data (0.54 and 0.56, respectively). Results are robust to independent paraphrasers (Gemma and Qwen) and leave-one-book-out sensitivity analyses. We also test both GPT-4o and GPT-4o mini, with the same October 2023 training cutoff date, to ensure the model is not simply identifying language shifts over time. These findings support disclosure requirements for pre-training data provenance and formal licensing markets for training content. Results remain preliminary given the small book sample, possible contamination of post-cutoff controls, and differences across models in size, architecture, and training composition.

811. The ethical implementation gap in AI-driven disaster risk reduction: empirical evidence from Australia’s multi-sector analysis
Randeniya, J.N. et al. (2026). The ethical implementation gap in AI-driven disaster risk reduction: empirical evidence from Australia’s multi-sector analysis. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01474-9
Abstract (original): Abstract Context While artificial intelligence offers transformative potential for disaster risk reduction, its ethical deployment raises critical concerns when life-critical decisions depend on algorithmic systems with varying levels of accessibility across responsible organizations. This study quantifies AI adoption disparities across Australian DRR stakeholders and identifies institutional factors driving the adoption gap between innovation and operational deployment. A mixed-methods approach combined systematic review of 217 global AI-DRR publications (1999–2024) with qualitative analysis of 14 Australian organizations across government, academic, and NGO/private sectors (February-March 2025). Semi-structured interviews and document analysis examined AI technique adoption, data integration, and downscaling applications, with implementation levels systematically coded on a 1–5 scale following an inter-coder validated protocol. Government agencies demonstrate lower AI adoption (mean score 2.1/5) compared to academic institutions (4.0/5) and NGO/private organizations (3.4/5), representing a 47.5% adoption gap between government and academic sectors (Cohen’s d ≈ 2.0, indicating large practical difference despite small sample size). While academic institutions deploy advanced techniques including GANs and hybrid AI-physics models achieving 45% accuracy improvements, government agencies remain anchored to traditional systems like ACCESS with minimal AI integration. Critical barriers include procurement constraints (18–24-month cycles), computational limitations (10x below requirements), and regulatory mandates for explainable decisions. Academic innovations in AI-based downscaling, improving spatial resolution from 60 km to 10 km, remain inaccessible to operational agencies due to absence of knowledge transfer mechanisms. The study reveals an ethical adoption disparity where organizations with greatest responsibility for community safety show lowest AI adoption, creating accountability voids and equity concerns in disaster protection. This misalignment raises fundamental ethical questions about algorithmic justice, distributive justice, and procedural fairness in public safety contexts. Bridging this gap requires ethical governance frameworks addressing transparency, accountability, and equitable access to life-saving technologies, alongside institutional reforms enabling cross-sector knowledge transfer.

812. Zetetic Exclusion
Falbo, A. (2026). Zetetic Exclusion. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10162
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines the dynamics of inquiry under conditions of oppression. It presents an account of unjust zetetic exclusion , which arises when prejudice limits access to inquiry and the epistemic goods it yields. Unlike other forms of epistemic injustice, unjust zetetic exclusion need not result from a prejudicial credibility attribution. Instead, it may manifest even if one is perceived to be a highly competent and trustworthy inquirer, or even if one makes no judgment whatsoever regarding one’s zetetic capabilities. Unjust zetetic exclusion may result from prejudicial evaluations concerning who belongs within specific communities of inquiry and who should have access to, or possess knowledge about, a particular domain. Theories of epistemic injustice must account not only for how prejudice impacts credibility attributions but also how it regulates access to inquiry and shapes the conditions under which knowledge and other epistemic goods are acquired in the first place.

Norton, J. & Dellsén, F. (2026). Progress in Ethics: Out of the Ivory Tower. The Monist.
https://doi.org/10.1093/monist/onag033
Abstract (original): Abstract How is progress in the academic discipline of ethics related to improvements in people’s ethical thoughts and behaviors? 816. General Practitioners Should Ask Patients to Reduce Their Carbon Footprints
Jenkins, S.P. & Heer‐Stavert, S. (2026). General Practitioners Should Ask Patients to Reduce Their Carbon Footprints. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70209
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Climate change is a 21st century problem, and we must be ready to adapt our practices in order to confront it. Current philosophical efforts to reconcile our obligations to make healthcare sustainable with important principles of patient care have been successful up to a point, but do not go far enough. We consider a specific novel intervention that could be adopted by doctors as part of healthcare systems' efforts to reduce carbon emissions, which is an extension of the Pendleton consultation model asking General Practitioners (GPs) to explore patients' Ideas, Concerns, and Expectations (ICE). Our model, which we call ICE2, includes wider questions in which GPs also ask patients about how they might adapt their lifestyle to reduce its environmental impact. We consider the moral‐theoretical underpinning of this new model and argue for its consistency with patient‐centred care. We also consider specific objections to the adoption of ICE2: that there will be a backlash from patients that will lead to their being hesitant to seek healthcare going forward; that it will open the door to doctors moralising about other issues that are inappropriate; and that clinicians do not have the training or expertise required to discuss climate change. We discuss our responses to these objections, finding that they all fail to provide a knockdown reason against at least embarking upon a trial of the ICE2 model in settings where an individual doctor deems it appropriate to discuss it with a particular patient.

817. Is Care a Virtue? A New Account of Care's Relationship With Virtue Ethics
Shaban‐Potter, J. (2026). Is Care a Virtue? A New Account of Care's Relationship With Virtue Ethics. J Soc Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70076
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This paper aims to address a lingering misconception about an ethics of care: that it is simply a form of virtue ethics that includes the straightforward virtue of care. Care cannot be a standard virtue of character, I argue, because care is a necessary precondition of virtue. Virtue ethics sets itself apart from consequentialist or deontological ethical theories by explaining ‘the good’ in terms of virtues, as opposed to outcome or right action. Superficially, there would seem to be nothing very unusual about accommodating care within a virtue ethical framework. In ordinary discourse we talk of the virtuous person as being caring in the same way that we might talk of them as being charitable, kind, courageous or witty. Nevertheless, over the course of this paper I argue that care is not a virtue like courage or wit but something more ethically fundamental that makes courage, wit and other virtues intelligible.

818. The View From the Centre: Recovering Nagel's Objective Self
Weber, C. (2026). The View From the Centre: Recovering Nagel's Objective Self. Analytic Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/phib.70026
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Thomas Nagel highlighted a puzzle in accommodating the subjective fact that I am TN within an objective, centerless conception of reality. He rejected the “semantic diagnosis,” which treats “I” as an indexical as trivial, and proposed instead the concept of the “objective self.” In this paper, I do two things: first, I defend the semantic diagnosis against Nagel's objections; second, I vindicate Nagel's positive proposal involving the objective self by demonstrating that it is not a genuine alternative to the semantic diagnosis but is recoverable from within a Lewisian centered‐worlds version of it. I show how the Lewisian framework, through factorizing centered content into objective, and subjective components provides the conceptual resources to model the phenomenology of first‐person thought Nagel describes.

https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6262
Abstract (original): Should care be understood exclusively as the work done to meet the basic needs of dependents such as children or as a broader set of practices in which people in different kinds of relationships partake? If the latter, what makes care distinctive and distinct from other values, ideals, and practices? Despite the growing attention of political philosophy to issues of care, I argue that we still lack an account of care suitable to address these questions from a political perspective. One of the problems is that existing proposals are either too narrow, as they exclusively focus on dependency care, or too broad, such that they fail to capture the distinctiveness of care. In this article, I address this challenge by presenting an account of care that captures a wide-ranging set of politically relevant practices of care while at the same time making sense of its distinctiveness. What makes care distinctive, I argue, is that it takes place in the context of relationships of care characterized by specific attitudes.

822. Social Roles and Exculpatory Ignorance
Wells, E. (2026). Social Roles and Exculpatory Ignorance. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6264
Abstract (original): People sometimes attempt to avoid blame with the following excuse: “I didn’t know I was doing anything wrong; I was just doing my job.” I argue that this excuse points towards a distinctive form of genuinely exculpatory ignorance. Complying with social role norms involves following deliberative norms that can explain agents’ claims of ignorance and mitigate their blameworthiness. My account of this experience makes a surprising contribution to the debate over when ignorance excuses. If we accept that nonculpable moral ignorance excuses, my argument highlights a novel form of ignorance that can be experienced by role occupants. On some views, however, what matters is being motivated by the right reasons (having a “good will”), so even nonculpably morally ignorant agents are blameworthy because they are not appropriately responsive to moral considerations. My account challenges the accepted view that having a good will just is responsiveness to moral reasons. I offer role occupants as a case study of moral ignorance with a good will. The fact that our social roles can lead to exculpatory ignorance in this ordinary and pervasive way opens up new territory for theories of moral responsibility.

823. Just Savings, Compensation, and the First Generation
Wiseman, M. (2026). Just Savings, Compensation, and the First Generation. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6265
Abstract (original): The just savings principle is the centerpiece of John Rawls’s account of intergenerational justice. Yet it is hard to see why the parties in the original position could accept it, for it imposes net costs on the worst-off first generation. Many attempt to solve this “problem of the first generation” by offering explanations as to why the parties could accept Rawls’s principle. Here I take a different approach. I show that most of these attempts neglect a crucial consideration; there is an alternative savings principle that the parties should prefer to Rawls’s own. According to the compensated savings principle, the first generation should be compensated for the cost of their savings by younger generations with whom they overlap. I defend this principle as the most promising explanation yet of why the parties could rationally choose a savings principle that generates the accumulation required for a just basic structure.

824. The Pragmatics of Disability Self-Ascriptions
Purinton, S. & Yap, A. (2026). The Pragmatics of Disability Self-Ascriptions. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6269
Abstract (original): Disability self-ascriptions could be treated as descriptive claims: assertions that one satisfies the conditions for counting as disabled. This article focuses on a different function that such ascriptions can serve. Drawing on Quill Kukla and Mark Lance’s account of gender ascriptions, we argue that first-person disability ascriptions often function as speech acts that position the speaker within social space. More precisely, we argue that self-ascribing disability can serve as a request or demand that the speaker not be held to certain capability norms, or norms about what people are expected to be able to do. Not only that, but these self-ascriptions often also serve as demands for access, specifically access to what the speakers need in order to manifest valued capabilities. We use this account to explain three common sources of ambivalence that individuals experience around claiming disability.

825. Distinguishing Persuasion from Manipulation
Mitchell, T. & Douglas, T. (2026). Distinguishing Persuasion from Manipulation. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6271
Abstract (original): Manipulation is widely regarded as a presumptively impermissible method of influence, in contrast to persuasion, which is usually presented as presumptively permissible. Calling something manipulative is typically a moral criticism; calling it persuasive is not. Although there is a flourishing philosophical literature on manipulation, that on persuasion is comparatively underdeveloped despite persuasion being one of the most common and important ways in which people influence one another. This article begins to remedy that situation. We present a novel account of persuasion, drawing on and explicating the distinction from manipulation. The aim is to show what persuasion is in such a way that explains why it is presumptively permissible, whereas manipulation is not. We first develop an account of manipulation by adapting an account recently defended by Robert Noggle. We then define nonmanipulative influence in general, then carve out persuasion therefrom. Throughout, we assume that persuasion is not manipulative. However, we are open to the possibility that there is in fact overlap between persuasion and manipulation, in which case the account we present is of specifically nonmanipulative persuasion, which we still take to be an interesting and important category of influence. We are also not concerned specifically with rational persuasion; our account allows for persuasion that need not be fully rational. The result is a holistic account that is useful in considering the nature and ethics of influence.

826. Refuting the Arbitrary Cutoff Objection to Moderate Deontology
Hanna, N. (2026). Refuting the Arbitrary Cutoff Objection to Moderate Deontology. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6272
Abstract (original): Suppose that you must choose between killing an innocent person or letting others die. Moderate deontology says that when the stakes are high enough in such a case, killing the innocent person becomes right, but the stakes must be very high for this to happen—much higher than standard forms of consequentialism take them to be. Critics object that moderate deontology is committed to arbitrary cutoffs, and this feature makes the theory arbitrary in a way that competing theories are not. I refute this objection.

827. Discursive Institutional Work and the Social Control of Dangerous Objects
Schlindwein, E. et al. (2026). Discursive Institutional Work and the Social Control of Dangerous Objects. J Bus Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10551-026-06502-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Across societies and over time, the social control of objects deemed “dangerous”—i.e., as threats to public order, morality, or physical and psychological well-being—varies, from regulation to prohibition, with implications for responsibility, potential harm, and affected communities. Drawing on U.S. Congressional hearings on the issue of cannabis regulation and prohibition (1906–1951), we examine how shifts in social control track changing configurations of discursive institutional work. Our work demonstrates how configurations of rationalization, moralization, and syncretization shaped the meaning of cannabis as a dangerous object: rationalization calculates the risk, supporting calibrated regulation; moralization absolutizes the danger, promoting prohibition; and syncretization balances these two. We refer to this reciprocal dynamic as an epistemic ratchet, whereby rationalization and moralization each weaken the epistemic foundations of the alternative regime, making movement between regulation and prohibition difficult in either direction. Our study makes three contributions: (a) it extends the organizing-of-risk account of how danger is rendered governable to the form and reversibility of control; (b) it adds to research on discursive institutional work by identifying epistemology, complexity, and responsibility as the object-level qualities through which discourse becomes consequential for governance; and (c) it contributes to debates on responsibility, ethics, and harm by showing how the discursive redistribution of responsibility shapes the scope and coerciveness of control.

828. The Metaphysics of Gender Identity
Jarvis, A.M.H. (2026). The Metaphysics of Gender Identity. Journal of Ethics and Social Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.26556/jesp.v31i6.6263
Abstract (original): This article addresses two related questions. First, what is it to have a gender identity? Second, what are gender identities, metaphysically speaking? In answer to the first, I defend a “doing” model: to “have” a gender identity is to engage in an ongoing act of self-interpretation. In answer to the second, I defend a social model: gender identities are shared social tools that individuals draw on and recreate in their acts of self-interpretation. This account of gender identity remains faithful to the operative concept of gender identity, giving philosophical substance to the idea of a “gendered sense of self.” It respects sincere self-ascriptions of gender, escaping the exclusion problems that plague other accounts of gender identity. It avoids circularity, and it has greater explanatory power than rival accounts. By emphasizing how interpretative resources are learned and shared through social interactions, it captures the sense in which gender identities are not wholly internal but are negotiated in dialogue with others, using resources made available by one’s social context. It can therefore explain how and why practices of self-ascribing gender change over time and across cultures. Moreover, it explains why individuals cannot exercise entirely free choice over their gender identities yet can exercise some limited agency over them. This article demonstrates that we can take gender identity to be deeply social without denying its first-personal character; we can allow for agency without conceding unconstrained choice. We achieve this by understanding gender identity in terms of an ongoing, socially mediated practice of self-interpretation.

867. The Case for Pediatric Participation in Risky First‐In‐Human Clinical Trials
Bobier, C. et al. (2026). The Case for Pediatric Participation in Risky First‐In‐Human Clinical Trials. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70206
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The purpose of a first‐in‐human (FIH) clinical trial is to gather safety, side effects, and (potential) dosage information, which is to say, information about how an intervention affects and interacts with the human body. Although participants in FIH trials may personally benefit, the primary goal is to obtain participant baseline human safety knowledge or knowledge of intervention efficacy. Some FIH clinical trials carry significant foreseeable risk to participants with little to no foreseeable participant benefit. As the potential risk is high and personal benefit unlikely, there is consensus that participants in risky FIH trials should consent to participate, which entails that children should not be included in a risky FIH trial that could enroll only adults. We take aim at this view and argue that risky FIH trials planned for adults should be open also to including children in some circumstances. Specifically, and focusing primarily on solid organ xenotransplant research, we argue that considerations of generalizability, fair inclusion, differential risk‐benefit, parental authority, and children's interest are ethically salient, and that concerns of exploitation, instrumentalization, and upper‐limits of harm can be effectively addressed.

869. “… You Realize How You Can Use the App and Not Let the App Use You.” The Moral Universe of Latin American Delivery Riders From the Perspective of Democratic Citizenship

https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70075

Abstract (original): Not available

871. Solving the Dual Puzzles of Collective Harm
Koles, T. (2026). Solving the Dual Puzzles of Collective Harm. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70076
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Suppose some uncoordinated group of agents causes tremendous harm, but none of the individuals could have meaningfully changed this result by acting differently. These “collective harm” cases have been the subject of extended philosophical discussion, but this paper argues that there are really two different, if connected, puzzles presented by such cases: (1) a puzzle about how to characterize the moral injury to those who suffer this harm (the “wrongness puzzle”) and (2) a puzzle about why agents may do something wrong in participating in producing this harm (the “participation puzzle”). This paper elaborates these puzzles, defends the idea that they are different from each other, and offers solutions to them both. The core idea is that an application of the distinction between ideal and nonideal theory can shed light on both puzzles; the injury experienced by the victims of collective harm is to be understood in terms of the shortfall they suffer from how they would fare if ideal circumstances obtained, and participation in collective harm is wrong when it involves reasoning that fails to appropriately apply ideal moral rules to nonideal situations.
