著者未収録 (2026). Issue Information. Analytic Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/phib.70027
Abstract (original): Not available

873. “Private Rights” in Public Law
Bradley, J. (2026). “Private Rights” in Public Law. Michigan Law Review.
https://doi.org/10.36644/mlr125.2.private
Abstract (original): Private rights have returned to play a central role in public law. In the last few years alone, courts have invoked private rights to elaborate the elements of Article III standing, interpret the scope of 42 U.S.C. § 1983’s cause of action, articulate the limits of the administrative state, and give meaning to enumerated constitutional rights. But unlike private law—which has confronted a similar fall and rise of private rights with wide-ranging discussion over their meaning—public law has not been subjected to a fulsome debate about what exactly private rights are. Both proponents and critics of the return of a private rights model of public law adjudication share a basic understanding of private rights rooted in William Blackstone’s eighteenthcentury writings. But Blackstone’s view fails to capture the full significance of private rights in the common law. Overemphasizing it muddles the stakes of the return of private rights in public law.

This Article articulates the historical and doctrinal limits of the default Blackstonian view of private rights. Blackstone’s conception of private rights was based on an underlying view of liberty as noninterference that was controversial even when he was writing in the 1760s. Instead, the dominant view of liberty in the common law at the time was the republican view of liberty as independence. That view of liberty supported a distinct republican conception of private rights, which has coexisted in the common law tradition alongside the Blackstonian conception.

After outlining the oft-elided republican conception of private rights and tracing its influence on legal discourse, rhetoric, and case law, this Article illustrates its possible implications for public law doctrine. Recognizing the distinction between these two conceptions of private rights casts new light on Article III standing, damages, Section 1983, and adjudication outside Article III courts. This distinction also provides a corrective to the scholarly debates over private and public rights: Just as it is a mistake to suppose there was a single unified historical conception of private rights, it is also a mistake to dismiss private rights altogether as inherently confused or obfuscatory.

874. Tribal Federalism: Expanding the Indian Gaming Paradigm
Crepelle, A. (2026). Tribal Federalism: Expanding the Indian Gaming Paradigm. Michigan Law Review.
https://doi.org/10.36644/mlr125.2.tribal
Abstract (original): Federalism has long been celebrated for its ability to foster policy innovation, transforming states into “laboratories of democracy.” Yet, federalism discourse has largely neglected Indian tribes, the third sovereign in the American constitutional order. Tribes regularly implement novel policies. However, tribal policy innovation is hindered by uncertainty over whether tribes or states govern Indian country activities.

The most influential framework for determining the scope of tribal versus state regulatory power in Indian country is derived from the Supreme Court’s opinion in California v. Cabazon Band of Mission Indians—the case that launched Indian gaming. Cabazon held that tribes cannot permit an activity if the surrounding state prohibits it, but they can permit an activity if the surrounding state regulates it. For example, tribes in California can authorize gaming because California regulates it. However, Utah’s gaming ban prevents tribes within its borders from permitting casinos. The distinction between regulatory and prohibitory is often ambiguous; thus, state specificity invites endless litigation that stifles tribal policy experimentation.

To remedy this uncertainty and fully integrate tribes into the federal system, this Article proposes a “Cabazon-plus” paradigm. Under Cabazon-plus, if any state regulates an activity, all tribes would have exclusive regulatory authority over that activity. This paradigm provides the jurisdictional certainty vital for tribal self-determination and policy innovation. Furthermore, this Article argues Cabazon-plus will ignite tribal economies and benefit state economies, validating tribal sovereignty as a feature—not a bug—of a dynamic federal system.

875. Unintended murder
Al-Obaidi, S. (2026). Unintended murder. Journal of Legal Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.4337/jlp.2026.02.01
Abstract (original): A murder is an intentional killing, but some killings happen by mistake or accident. Although mistakes and accidents are unintended parts of an action, some jurisdictions prosecute unintended homicides as murder crimes in at least three patterns of cases. Together, these propositions present a puzzle that undermines a fundamental principle of criminal responsibility: An agent must unify the elements of a crime as a matter of their agency for criminal responsibility to attach. I examine five insightful ways to interpret this principle in the literature, and I argue that none can account for all three case patterns. Primarily, I offer a solution to the puzzle based on the intentional causation relation. According to this solution, although intentionally acting does not involve the intent to cause every change one makes as part of the action, it does involve intentionally causing all such changes, including the mistakes or accidents.

Abstract (original): This paper addresses a question that has been overlooked by the growing body of literature on private law formalism: how are formalist private law categories constructed? 882. Food, Families, and Parental Perfectionism: A Rejoinder to Clayton

https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70143

883. Forced Labour for Liberals: Mandatory Service, Social Equality, and Freedom of Occupation

https://doi.org/10.1002/japp.70145
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT In recent years, many political philosophers have made the case for thinking that achieving a fully just society will require some variety of mandatory civilian service. However, there is widespread agreement that mandatory service schemes are in significant tension with commitments to freedom of occupation. Correspondingly, support for mandatory service schemes is also taken to be in deep tension with the liberal tradition, with its emphasis on the fundamental importance of protecting basic liberties. 886. When principles become policy: the institutional translation of responsible AI in national AI ​strategies

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01477-6
Abstract (original): Abstract This study examines how Responsible AI (RAI) principles are translated as they move from ethical discourse into governance, standards, and national policy contexts. Drawing on comparative analysis of influential AI ethics guidelines, policy syntheses, and technical glossaries, the research traces how widely cited principles are reformulated across domains, using transparency and fairness as cases of how institutional translation operates in practice. It shows that while these principles originate as broad normative commitments, they become progressively institutionalized as policy categories, procedural requirements, and measurable technical properties. The findings highlight that principles most amenable to standardization and measurement tend to dominate policy implementation, while more expansive ethical concepts are less visible. By mapping these shifts across institutional settings, the paper contributes to a deeper understanding of how AI ethics is reconfigured in practice and offers insights for policymakers seeking to balance technical feasibility with normative ambition in the governance of AI.

892. On Associating Extinction with Death: Fatal Implications for Conservation?

https://doi.org/10.1093/monist/onag025

895. Section 35 of the Scotland Act 1998 and Executive Control of Devolved Legislation
Nyatanga, D. (2026). Section 35 of the Scotland Act 1998 and Executive Control of Devolved Legislation. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70078
Abstract (original): This article examines the first use of section 35 of the Scotland Act 1998, which prevented the Gender Recognition Reform (Scotland) Bill from receiving Royal Assent. It argues that the 2023 Order is constitutionally significant because it operationalised a previously unused form of executive control over devolved legislation. Section 35 permits intervention where the Secretary of State has reasonable grounds to believe that modifications made by a devolved Bill would adversely affect the operation of reserved law. It therefore separates legislative competence from legislative effectiveness: a Bill may fall within devolved competence yet be prevented from becoming law following a prospective ministerial assessment. Drawing on the statutory framework, the Statement of Reasons, and the Court of Session's judgment, the article characterises section 35 as a preventive executive power constrained by statutory conditions, parliamentary annulment, and judicial review, but one which affords substantial latitude to predictive ministerial judgement. It develops this arrangement as a statutory form of conditional autonomy, building on earlier accounts of politically conditioned devolution, and situates the provision within a broader pattern of UK executive constitutionalism. Its longer‐term significance will depend on whether section 35 remains exceptional or becomes a more regular instrument of territorial governance.

896. Participation Without Adaptive Preferences
Mallon, R. (2026). Participation Without Adaptive Preferences. J Soc Philos.
https://doi.org/10.1111/josp.70073
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Feminism opposes the unjust inequality and subjugation of women. Core feminist projects morally critique people, institutions, social practices, norms, and behaviors that contribute to or constitute the subordination of women. But such projects confront individual women and groups of women who participate in and endorse the practices being critiqued. A common response to this tension is to posit adaptive preferences in the form of “deformed desires” among participating women. But a standing alternative holds that participating women engage in trade‐offs among goods in nonideal situations. Here, I extend this “tradeoffs” alternative by arguing that simple ideas from game‐theoretic models support the possibility of trade‐off explanations that respect and illuminate the agency of participating women while allowing for feminist critique. And trade‐off accounts can avoid substantive assumptions regarding human flourishing or the nature of the good. Both features make trade‐off accounts normatively preferable to adaptive preference accounts of participation in unjust social arrangements.

897. The Invention of Liberalism
Dijn, A.d. (2026). The Invention of Liberalism. Political Theory.
https://doi.org/10.1177/00905917261501468
Abstract (original): Today, it is commonplace to claim that liberalism created the modern world. This paper examines how and why the rise-of-liberalism narrative first emerged by examining textbooks and surveys on the history of political thought published in Britain and the United States between 1890 and the 1990s. In doing so, it shows how, in the interwar period, a new conception of “liberalism” was invented, imagining it as a secular emancipatory movement that shaped the modern world in its image. Second, this paper highlights how this master narrative was created by liberalism’s self-professed enemies—by Marxists and conservative thinkers—as much as by its friends, to serve different polemical purposes. 898. Ethics for a Large Sentient World: Insects, Ahimsa, and Gandhi’s Progressive Ahimsaism
Birch, J. (2026). Ethics for a Large Sentient World: Insects, Ahimsa, and Gandhi’s Progressive Ahimsaism. Journal of Practical Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.3998/jpe.10745
Abstract (original): Positions that link rights to sentience face an insect challenge: insects are plausibly sentient, yet recognizing individual insects as rights-bearers would problematize public health, veterinary, and agricultural benefits obtained by harming them. The most obvious responses seem inadequate. Indian animal ethics, which has always taken the idea of insect sentience seriously, is a promising place to look for further options. Of particular relevance is the idea of ahimsa (nonviolence, noninjury), but ahimsa in its ancient form has metaphysical commitments that secular animal ethics should not accept. More promising still is the progressive ahimsaism developed by Gandhi in the 1920s, on which duties of nonmaleficence can be overridden by duties of care, provided the duty of care is adopted, interpreted, and exercised in accordance with the guiding ideal of ahimsa. This article provides an exposition and analysis of the view and relates it to the insect challenge.

899. In Defence of Robust Rights for All Sentient Creatures
Pepper, A. (2026). In Defence of Robust Rights for All Sentient Creatures. Journal of Practical Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.3998/jpe.10746
Abstract (original): Jonathan Birch's 'Ethics for a Large Sentient World' presents a novel approach to the challenges associated with granting sentient insects direct moral concern. The Gandhian-inspired ethic that Birch develops takes insect sentience seriously while maintaining that insects have no right not to be harmed when harming them is necessary to secure human and nonhuman animal health and agricultural benefits. This paper offers a critical response to Birch's framework. I begin by arguing that Birch misinterprets the challenge facing a robust rights-based theory. I then show that his Gandhian alternative is not a genuine departure from a robust rights-based view but rather a specific, and ultimately unappealing, version of it. I show that Birch's view is unattractive because it holds that duties of care can function as exceptions to rights, which has problematic implications for all sentient beings, including humans.

900. Ethics for a Tragic World: Humanity, Insects, and Doing Good
O'Brien, G.D. (2026). Ethics for a Tragic World: Humanity, Insects, and Doing Good. Journal of Practical Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.3998/jpe.10747
Abstract (original): In his ‘Ethics for a Large Sentient World: Insects, Ahimsa, and Gandhi’s Progressive Ahimsaism’ Jonathan Birch attempts to provide a solution to one of the most difficult problems in animal ethics: the insect challenge. If insects are sentient, and if sentience is sufficient for the possession of robust rights not to be harmed, then much of our behavior is seriously wrong since it harms insects on a vast scale. Birch’s solution is based on Gandhi’s idea of Progressive Ahimsaism (PA). In this paper I argue that PA is unsuited to answer the challenges of animal suffering. First, though it emphasizes avoiding wrongdoing, the moral ideal proposed by PA makes it difficult to do positive good, tends toward antinatalism, and offers no pathway out of natural suffering. Second, PA’s duty of care model is incompatible with largescale beneficent action and precludes us from moving toward a genuinely harm-free world. Finally, I conclude by briefly suggesting that we could solve these problems by seeing humanity as a collective agent with a moral duty to care for all sentient beings and individual human actions as justified insofar as they are compatible with this mission.

901. The ‘Insect Challenge’ – A Response to Birch
Hurn, S. & Badman-King, A. (2026). The ‘Insect Challenge’ – A Response to Birch. Journal of Practical Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.3998/jpe.10748
Abstract (original): The recognition of invertebrates as ‘plausibly sentient’ raises significant ethical questions in contexts where they are harmed. In response to Jonathan Birch’s presentation of Gandhi’s ahimsaism (nonviolence) as a potential means of navigating this ‘insect challenge’, we draw on fieldwork conducted at the Skanda Vale ashram to provide a comparative critique of, and potential alternative to, ahmsaism. For the community at Skanda Vale, the ethos of nonviolence cannot be separated from the wider metaphysical commitments of foundational South Asian religious traditions. Within such a context we come to question the prioritization of sentience as a marker of responsibility toward other living beings. Rather than seeking to justify harms to sentient others, we argue for an expanded circle of compassion that is more in line with traditional uses of ahimsa.

902. How to Live Nonviolently in a Large Sentient World: Replies to Pepper, Hurn and Badman-King, and O’Brien
Birch, J. (2026). How to Live Nonviolently in a Large Sentient World: Replies to Pepper, Hurn and Badman-King, and O’Brien. Journal of Practical Ethics.

The framework condemns many aspects of modern industrialized economies, not just their treatment of nonhuman animals.

905. Is Data Collection from Internet Users a Form of Exploitation?
Magnell, E. & Malmqvist, E. (2026). Is Data Collection from Internet Users a Form of Exploitation?. Ethical Theory Moral Pract.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10677-026-10589-1
Abstract (original): Abstract Whenever you use apps, websites, or digital infrastructure owned by large platform companies, these companies collect data from your activity. They then use the data for various highly profitable purposes, e.g., by designing targeted advertising and selling it to third parties. This paper argues that such data collection should be considered a form of wrongful exploitation. We begin by defending the simple and relatively uncontroversial idea that to exploit is to extract excessive advantage from vulnerability. We then argue that platform companies’ data collection practices are exploitative in this sense. Many internet users are vulnerable because they have limited capacity to make an informed and voluntary decision about exchanging access to their data for access to the companies’ services, or because they have no reasonable alternative to doing so. Moreover, platform companies take advantage of these vulnerabilities to extract profits that they could not otherwise have extracted. The paper ends by discussing what governments and other third parties should do about this form of exploitation. We distinguish preventive measures, which remove or mitigate users’ vulnerabilities, from prohibition and regulation, which leave these vulnerabilities intact but block companies from taking advantage of them.

906. The genesis of inner speech: forward models or constructive imagination?
Gauker, C. (2026). The genesis of inner speech: forward models or constructive imagination?. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02625-8
Abstract (original): Abstract Inner speech is often said to be a by-product of motor control, the product of a forward model driven by an efference copy of a cancelled motor command to speak. The conscious auditory imagery of inner speech is said to arise when the product of this forward model becomes conscious. This conception of the genesis of inner speech founders on some basic questions about the consciousness of inner speech and about the interaction between inner speech and episodes of imagination. Further, the empirical evidence cited in support of this theory falls short. An alternative starts with a general conception of constructive imagination, which is our capacity to produce mental images (visual, auditory, etc.) in the course of practical problem-solving. Inner speech may be conceived as a species of constructive imagination in which we construct auditory images of episodes of speech. Inner speech is an imaginative representation of speech events that do not exist, which also represents the objects and events that the represented episodes of speech would represent if they existed.

907. Quo vadis, reasons first? From weight monism to weight pluralism to thresholds
Schmidt, E. (2026). Quo vadis, reasons first? From weight monism to weight pluralism to thresholds. Philos Stud.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s11098-026-02646-3
Abstract (original): Abstract In this paper, I discuss and explore two responses to problems that arise for a simple, weight monist version of reasons first in the practical domain. These responses—weight pluralism and threshold reasons first—attempt to provide a more nuanced account of reasons and all-things-considered deontic statuses. The first problem for weight monism is that it lacks the resources to predict the intuitively correct deontic statuses for the agent's options in cases of supererogation, of practical latitude, and of moral dilemmas. The second problem is that weight monism cannot distinguish requirements from oughts. I sketch how weight pluralists try to do justice to the problem cases, as well as to distinguish requirements and oughts, by introducing several independent dimensions of a reason's weight. However, as I argue, weight pluralism faces problems of its own—it cannot uphold the entailment from requirement to ought (on some variants); it problematically casts ought as an evaluative notion (on another variant); and it undermines the action-guidingness of reasons. In the end, I explore a more sophisticated weight monist alternative, threshold reasons first, that holds some promise to avoid all of these problems.

910. When prediction is not explanation: AI and the ethical transformation of medicine
Cati, M.M. (2026). When prediction is not explanation: AI and the ethical transformation of medicine. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01469-6
Abstract (original): Abstract Artificial intelligence (AI) is evolving from a collection of discrete applications towards a technology increasingly embedded in the infrastructure of medicine. This development may improve diagnosis, prediction, monitoring, and the use of clinical information, but technological adoption is advancing faster than the governance structures needed to accompany it. At the level of the individual patient, this transformation creates a specific ethical problem. Patient information enters computational systems and emerges as probabilities, classifications, predictions, or recommendations that may influence care. The black box provides a metaphor for the intervening computational process when clinically relevant elements cannot be sufficiently examined. Yet opening the black box does not resolve the entire problem: an intelligible and validated prediction does not itself establish that a proposed intervention will benefit a particular patient. I propose a Patient-Level AI Code of Conduct governing the transition from AI output to clinical action. The Code requires examinability, validation, patient-level applicability, independent clinical justification, and meaningful human responsibility before an AI output materially influences care. Its purpose is to preserve a fundamental distinction as AI becomes increasingly infrastructural to medicine: computational capability can inform clinical decisions, but it cannot by itself justify action on a patient.

912. Taking the wheel: user-side responsibility and the ethics of SAE level 2 driving automation
Mukherjee, S. (2026). Taking the wheel: user-side responsibility and the ethics of SAE level 2 driving automation. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01489-2
Abstract (original): Abstract Scholarship on responsibility and autonomous technology has extensively examined responsibility gaps, distributed agency, and the obligations of developers, manufacturers, regulators, and human operators. Yet the prospective normative position of users across repeated engagement with partially automated systems, and its relationship to responsibility after harm, remains comparatively underdeveloped. This paper argues that users of SAE Level 2 partial driving automation systems occupy a normatively distinctive position that generates two forms of obligation which have not been adequately theorized. Drawing on Conradie and Nagel’s [1] framework of taking versus holding responsibility and the concept of extended responsibility developed by Mason [2], this paper makes two-part argument. First, users of SAE Level 2 systems bear prospective responsibilities arising from their continuing supervisory role, including sustained supervisory engagement, reasonable trust calibration, appropriate knowledge-seeking about system limitations, and readiness for control intervention. Second, where harm occurs and the user’s involvement is morally significant but the conditions for ordinary blameworthiness remain uncertain, users may nevertheless be positioned to take extended retrospective responsibility. Extended responsibility therefore supplements rather than replaces ordinary responsibility.

913. Beyond behavioural markers: causal-representational corroboration in AI sentience assessment
Li, Y. (2026). Beyond behavioural markers: causal-representational corroboration in AI sentience assessment. AI Ethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01495-4
Abstract (original): Abstract Current generative AI systems increasingly elicit attributions of understanding, emotion, and even consciousness. This creates an evidential problem for AI sentience assessment, because the behavioural and linguistic markers that motivate these attributions may be generated by multiple underlying processes. One source of uncertainty is the gaming problem: AI systems may satisfy sentience indicators because they have learned the relevant behavioural regularities. A further observer-side concern is that anthropomorphic or socially responsive behaviour may influence how strongly these markers are weighted. This paper treats these as distinct sources of evidential uncertainty and focuses on the problem of evidential certification. Behavioural similarity and verbal self-report are not evidentially significant merely because they resemble human or animal expressions of sentience. Their evidential value depends on whether they can be connected to internal processes that causally explain why the relevant outputs are produced. Cross-validating multiple behavioural markers remains unreliable in the LLM case, since models may learn not only individual markers but also their patterns of co-occurrence. Moreover, even under controlled training conditions, verbal self-report remains vulnerable to alternative computational explanations. Since assessments of AI sentience may bear on questions of welfare and moral status, errors in evidential attribution can have ethically significant consequences. In response, I propose the Causal-Representational Corroboration Standard (CCS). The CCS directly evaluates whether behavioural or verbal markers are supported by a stable and causally relevant internal organization whose evidential role is not exhausted by training pressure that directly selects for the assessed marker. The corroborated mechanism becomes relevant to sentience if it also participates in an affectively structured organization and satisfies functional or architectural conditions independently motivated by a plausible theory of consciousness. Therefore, the CCS provides a more disciplined basis for managing the ethical risks of both over-attributing and under-attributing sentience to AI systems. Keywords: AI sentience; Causal-representational corroboration; Behavioural markers; Large language models; Gaming problem

914. The Virtues (and Vices) of Contrarianism
Gurrola, J. & Moller, D. (2026). The Virtues (and Vices) of Contrarianism. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10166
Abstract (original): Abstract We offer a theory of contrarians such as Socrates, Martin Luther, and Thoreau, and explain how contrarianism can function as an epistemic virtue. On our view, contrarians don’t just dissent from orthodoxy, but from a specific ingroup, and in a specific domain. Contrarians must also have some independent perspective that animates their dissent rather than parroting others, and they must express their dissent publicly, which will often produce alienation or reprisals. Finally, contrarianism isn’t bloodless inquiry; contrarians are animated by contempt for conformism and herd-behavior. The virtues of contrarianism include the ability to disrupt epistemic bubbles and echo-chambers that have formed among our ingroup, and contributing an adversarial element to our social epistemology, familiar from the legal system. But our account also has the resources to explain how contrarianism can spill over into a vice, since it is a trait like kindness or skepticism that requires judicious application.

915. (Re)writing the fiction: how Dutch sovereign citizens narrate legality
Jonker, A. & Siegel, I. (2026). (Re)writing the fiction: how Dutch sovereign citizens narrate legality. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10123
Abstract (original): Abstract How should sociolegal scholarship understand sovereign citizens who contest state authority while remaining deeply invested in law? This article examines how Dutch sovereign citizens construct, enact, and negotiate understandings of legality in lived experience and institutional encounters. Drawing on in-depth interviews with four participants and six courtroom observations, and combining legal consciousness scholarship with narrative and relational perspectives, we identify three interrelated narrative stances through which sovereign legality is constructed and negotiated: legitimacy under contestation, re-authoring the normative order, and strategic navigation of institutional reality. These stances show that sovereign legality is neither a coherent alternative doctrine nor a straightforward rejection of state law. Rather, participants reinterpret legal authority, relocate legality in alternative normative foundations such as consent, natural law, and humanity, and adapt claims and strategies in response to institutional constraints. We conceptualize this configuration as counter-nomic legal consciousness: a narrative and relational mode of legal meaning-making through which individuals contest state authority while remaining invested in law’s language, procedures, and symbolic force. This study of Dutch sovereign citizens thus extends legal consciousness scholarship by showing how sovereign legality cuts across conventional distinctions between compliance and resistance and serves interpretive, practical, and affective functions.

916. The ambivalence of sexual consent: a socio-legal inquiry
Naaman, N. & Lazar, R.L. (2026). The ambivalence of sexual consent: a socio-legal inquiry. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10125
Abstract (original): Abstract Consent – the organizing principle of legal responses to sexual harms – rests on a yes-versus-no binary. Yet, ambivalence is pervasive in sexual life, often eluding binary simplicity. This article assesses the implications of ambivalence for sexual consent jurisprudence. Drawing on interviews with Israeli gay and bisexual men regarding their experiences in (semi-)public sexual venues, it examines a context in which sexual norms have historically developed outside dominant cultural scripts, where ambivalence is particularly salient. Through the men’s testimonies, the conditions under which ambivalence leaves individuals uncertain of how to name sexual harms, identify boundary violations, or assign responsibility are mapped. Grounded in these findings, alongside sociological theories of ambivalence and socio-legal theories of sexual consent, the article adopts dialectical sensibility to capture what binary consent models tend to flatten: the simultaneous presence of agency/coercion, empowerment/vulnerability, and pleasure/danger within a single sexual interaction. Ambivalence in these “gray zones” is therefore reconceptualized here as neither anomaly nor individual failure but as a structuring – and even productive – feature of sexual life, complicating theories that cast ambivalence as primarily suppressive. Finally, the analysis underscores the limitations of prevailing consent paradigms and surfaces new questions about how criminal law might respond to the indeterminate realities of sex.

917. Fragile rights: rights consciousness in hospitals in times of crisis
Sudai, M. (2026). Fragile rights: rights consciousness in hospitals in times of crisis. Law & Society Review.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lsr.2026.10127
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper examines rights consciousness (RC) in hospitals during emergencies, drawing on interviews with bereaved relatives and healthcare workers from isolated COVID-19 wards in Israel. It reveals that neither group framed visitation as a formal legal entitlement but rather as a privilege, gift, or luxury. While visitation is a routine and expected practice in ordinary times, the crisis exposed its fragility, transforming it into a discretionary allowance whose availability depended on institutional priorities and frontline discretion. Family responses ranged from compliance to selective resistance, with rights awareness often emerging retrospectively and collectively. The analysis advances few theoretical insights. First, it conceptualizes visitation as a “fragile right” robust in everyday practice yet weakly anchored in law and easily erased in crisis. Second, it shows how “de facto rights” can re-emerge from within institutions through professional ethics, norms, and informal organizational diffusion. Third, it demonstrates that RC is relational and spatial, suppressed inside hospitals by clinical hierarchies, yet reconstructed outside them through collective comparison and shared experience. These dynamics suggest that strengthening such fragile rights may depend not only on legal reform but on bottom-up participation of patient/family in hospital policymaking during crisis, which can cultivate rights awareness from within care itself.

918. Crime does not pay, but does tort?
                    Lewis-Ranwell v G4S Health Services (UK) Ltd
                    [2026] UKSC 2
Burin, A.K. (2026). Crime does not pay, but does tort?
                    Lewis-Ranwell v G4S Health Services (UK) Ltd
                    [2026] UKSC 2. Legal Studies.
https://doi.org/10.1017/lst.2026.10140
Abstract (original): Alexander Lewis-Ranwell killed three people while suffering a psychotic episode. He brought a claim against several defendants who failed to detain him prior to this tragic incident. Following the UK Supreme Court’s recent decision in Lewis-Ranwell v G4S Health Services , 1 his claim in negligence was barred for illegality. The case is significant because it is the first time the UK Supreme Court has confronted the effect of an acquittal for insanity on a tort claim for damages arising out of the crime. 2 Its answer to the question posed in the title of this case note – even if crime does not pay, does tort law? – was thus ‘no’.

920. REVISITING MURDER TARIFFS: MINIMUM TERMS, REVIEWABILITY AND COMPARATIVE LAW

https://doi.org/10.1017/s0008197326101767
Abstract (original): Abstract Since the enactment of Criminal Justice Act 2003, Sched. 21, the average minimum term for murder in England and Wales has risen by more than 60 per cent. Very lengthy determinate terms are now the norm, subject only, in effect, to the Secretary of State’s discretion to release on compassionate grounds. This article asks whether there should be a mechanism for revisiting such terms, and, if so, on what principles. 921. CAUSATION IN DISCRIMINATION LAW: THE TORTIOUS “BUT-FOR” TEST
Taylor, A. & Elphick, L. (2026). CAUSATION IN DISCRIMINATION LAW: THE TORTIOUS “BUT-FOR” TEST. Camb Law J.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s000819732610186x
Abstract (original): Abstract Discrimination law is notoriously difficult to categorise. Depending on the context, discrimination law can be classified as human rights law, constitutional law or labour law. Notably, though, the UK Supreme Court has drawn significantly on the language of torts in its discrimination decisions. One prominent example is its use of the “but-for” test to determine the causative element of direct discrimination. This article explores the implications of relying on these tortious principles in discrimination jurisprudence. We argue that while the court’s use of the “but-for” test has been useful to claimants by removing the need for malice or intention to prove discrimination, it conflates factual causation with legal causation and misunderstands the analysis required to determine causation in a direct discrimination claim. As a result, the “but-for” test weakens the coherency of discrimination law, ignores its unique conceptual challenges and muddies its factual and normative inquiries.

922. Shadow legal integration: migrant legal integration, Russian style
Urinboyev, R. & Aidarkhankyzy, A. (2026). Shadow legal integration: migrant legal integration, Russian style. International Journal of Law in Context.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s1744552326100731
Abstract (original): Abstract This article examines how Uzbek migrants become incorporated into Russian society under conditions of legal precarity, discretionary enforcement and pervasive informality. Drawing on multisited ethnographic fieldwork conducted between 2014 and 2026 among Uzbek migrants in Russia and their families and communities in Uzbekistan, it develops the concept of shadow legal integration to explain how social and economic incorporation can deepen without a corresponding movement towards legal security. The article argues that repeated encounters with migration authorities, police, employers, intermediaries and migrant networks generate practical legal knowledge that migrants test, revise and circulate over time. This process of legal adaptation enables migrants to navigate the interaction between formal rules and informal practices while rem aining vulnerable to detention, deportation and administrative uncertainty. Ethnographic analysis of deportation, migration bureaucracy, legal brokerage, document economies and changing migration governance after 2022 further demonstrates that such competence is not a fixed repertoire but requires continual recalibration as legal and enforcement environments change. Shadow legal integration thus identifies how learning to navigate the intersection of formal and informal practices can itself become a mode of incorporation. The article contributes to migration and socio-legal scholarship by showing that migrant integration and legal security need not develop in parallel.

926. Against the expressivist interpretation of Nietzsche’s lightning-flash analogy
Wood, W. (2026). Against the expressivist interpretation of Nietzsche’s lightning-flash analogy. Philos Q.
https://doi.org/10.1093/pq/pqag079
Abstract (original): Abstract This article argues that Nietzsche’s rejection of the doer/deed distinction in the famous lightning-flash passage in On the Genealogy of Morality 1:13 is more radical than Robert Pippin’s influential expressivist interpretation allows. Nietzsche’s target is not merely the causal conception of agency, but a metaphysical picture according to which a bearer of a capacity subsequently actualizes it in action, and thus ultimately the ontological distinction between potentiality and actuality itself. The conventional distinction between strength and expression presupposes a bearer of a capacity that can remain unactualized; Nietzsche’s criticism of precisely this distinction, together with his identification of force with activity and his rejection of the capacity-bearing ‘substratum’, is also directed against the metaphysical structure required by the potentiality/actuality distinction itself.

929. Clarifying the Distinction Between Inclining Moral Bioenhancement and Prohibitory Moral Bioenhancement, and Their Effects on Freedom
Lee, D. (2026). Clarifying the Distinction Between Inclining Moral Bioenhancement and Prohibitory Moral Bioenhancement, and Their Effects on Freedom. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70204
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT The project of Moral Bioenhancement (MBE)—biomedical interventions causing individuals to have better moral beliefs, motivations, or behaviours—is controversial, particularly as MBE relates to human freedom. Proponents argue that MBE is utility‐promoting and compatible with freedom. Conversely, critics charge that MBE—particularly when it bypasses or replaces rational moral deliberation, or when it is imposed compulsorily—threatens freedom of thought and action. I argue that much of the disagreement on this topic is due to conflation and obfuscation, resulting in conceptual confusion. Accordingly, I draw a distinction between two types of MBEs: those that incline individuals to moral action, and those that prohibit immoral action, by invoking the higher‐order desires of Harry Frankfurt's freedom of the will. Importantly, this distinction runs parallel to, rather than tracking, the familiar distinction between voluntary and compulsory MBE. Ultimately, I argue that inclining MBE interventions may not infringe, and may even enhance, freedom by allowing individuals to translate sincerely held moral beliefs into desired moral behaviours, enabling freedom as moral self‐mastery. Conversely, prohibitory MBEs necessarily restrict freedom, though this does not by itself entail that they are impermissible in all circumstances.

930. Algorithmic Bioethics: A Defense and Demonstration
Yeo, S.L. et al. (2026). Algorithmic Bioethics: A Defense and Demonstration. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70211
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT In this paper, we argue for the adoption of algorithmic bioethics , which uses algorithms, in the form of decision trees, to articulate, understand, apply, and evaluate bioethical arguments. Algorithms bring many benefits to the study of bioethics: they clearly, precisely, and transparently articulate the decision procedure and facts relevant to an ethical decision; furthermore, they can be used to systematically discover, map out, and compare different ethical decision procedures concerning a practical issue. We then also outline and tackle potential limitations of algorithmic bioethics, and explore future directions for using decision trees in bioethics. Throughout, we illustrate our points using a case study of ethical decision trees, created with the help of large language models, about conscientious objection in medicine.

931. Substitutional Quantification and Arithmetical Truth
Warren, J. (2026). Substitutional Quantification and Arithmetical Truth. Noûs.
https://doi.org/10.1111/nous.70072
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT In the mid‐20th century, there was a debate about whether the quantifiers in first‐order logic should be understood objectually or substitutionally. This debate typically focused on ontology and assumed that we could use only one of these two types of quantification. After Kripke and others influentially argued that the two kinds of quantification were logically equivalent, defenses of substitutional quantification were rare. Following Gottlieb and Field, I argue that Kripke was wrong and that substitutional consequence is language‐bound and non‐compact. While of no help with ontology, I also argue that so‐understood, substitutional quantification can be applied to help explain our grasp of a determinate notion of arithmetical truth. To argue for this, I give an account of the metasemantics of the substitutional quantifiers and prove that adding a single, very natural, mixed axiom to weak theories of arithmetic results in a categorical theory. This approach promises to explain arithmetical truth on a less controversial basis than appeals to either second‐order logic or the omega rule, thus making progress on the project of explaining our grasp of arithmetic in a naturalistically acceptable fashion.

933. Bullshit as Counterfeit Evidence
Berkovski, Y.S. (2026). Bullshit as Counterfeit Evidence. Ratio.
https://doi.org/10.1111/rati.70048
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Frankfurt's idea that bullshit is “fake,” “counterfeit,” and “phony” has been overshadowed in the later debate by his claim that the bullshitter is indifferent to truth. The former insight deserves to be taken more seriously. I begin by asking whether bullshit is a fake assertion and argue that it isn't. What makes it bullshit, rather, is that the speaker's performance is meant to raise the audience's credence in some proposition h by resembling genuine evidence for h , while simultaneously concealing that it lacks the features of genuine evidence. Bullshit, on this analysis, is best understood as a communicative act that the speaker intends to serve as “counterfeit evidence.” I then use the ideas of relevance theory to distinguish bullshit from plain lying. Since deception is a corollary of counterfeiture, it is also an intrinsic element of bullshit. This helps to contrast the present view with other accounts in the literature.

935. The Body Problem: Cautionary Notes on the Embodied Self
Loued-Khenissi, L. (2026). The Body Problem: Cautionary Notes on the Embodied Self. Neuroethics.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s12152-026-09683-z
Abstract (original): Abstract Recent years have seen a decisive turn in neuroscience toward embodiment, shifting focus from a brain-bound view of the mind to a more holistic one where the body is treated as constitutive of self and consciousness. While this turn has yielded valuable insights through studies on interoception, multisensory integration and bodily ownership, it also carries significant theoretical and ethical risks. This essay identifies the body problem: the risk of collapsing selfhood into bodyhood, with troubling consequences when applied to bodies that are marginalized or that otherwise do not meet the status quo. Tracing historical debates on mind–body relations, clarifying contemporary notions of the self and examining empirical findings on embodiment, it is argued that strong formulations of the embodied self risk reviving old reductions whereby “less body” implies “less self,” not because such formulations explicitly assert the inference, but because they leave it available for others to make. Such reductions threaten to undermine the value of persons whose bodies diverge from normative assumptions. Rather than rejecting embodiment research, this essay calls for caution against its uncritical extension, underscoring the need to guard against theoretical slippage that may entail harmful social consequences.

https://doi.org/10.1007/s43681-026-01480-x
Abstract (original): Abstract Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) can expose interpretable features in language-model activations, but public SAE resources such as GemmaScope and Llama Scope are trained on generic web text rather than cybersecurity evidence. CyberLens is a purpose-trained SAE and heuristic taxonomy-mapping pipeline for cybersecurity interpretability of an open-weight language model. English-only, phishing-heavy corpus; one completed layer-8 width-4,096 SAE whose observed $$L_0=286$$ greatly exceeds the target 20; heuristic, unverified labels; keyword-based outcomes; a placeholder-heavy 60-prompt intervention set; no evidence about frontier models or effective cyber-content suppression. SAE weights, feature dictionary, loader, and an interactive demonstration are released for reproducible small-model research; scaling to larger models remains future work.

938. A Modal Explanationist Solution to the Gettier Problem
Zhang, Y. & Zhao, H. (2026). A Modal Explanationist Solution to the Gettier Problem. Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10165
Abstract (original): Abstract The Gettier problem suggests that the core challenge for theories of knowledge lies in eliminating knowledge-undermining epistemic luck. In response, two major approaches have emerged in contemporary epistemology: modalism and explanationism. However, both approaches have limitations and exhibit an interesting theoretical symmetry: while modalism effectively handles environmental luck, it struggles with certain cases of intervening luck; explanationism faces the reverse difficulty. This symmetrical limitation suggests that any theory of knowledge relying on a single dimension is likely to be inadequate. To address this, this paper presents a new, integrated approach – Modal Explanationism (ME). ME requires that a belief counts as knowledge just in case it is explained by the truth of the believed proposition in the actual world, and that this explanatory connection is preserved across a range of nearby possible worlds. It is argued that ME offers a more successful solution to the Gettier problem than either modalism or explanationism alone.

939. Gender Markers in Passports and the Digital Racial Borders of the European Union

Abstract (original): Abstract The Article analyzes how gender markers on passports are used to bolster the digital racial borders of the European Union (EU). 940. Pronouns and Linguistic Amelioration

https://doi.org/10.1017/hyp.2026.10106
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper aims to advance current debates on gendered language reform by making explicit the assumptions in much of the philosophical literature, and by proposing a reorientation of how we theorize about pronoun reform and linguistic amelioration. While sympathetic to the normative aims of prominent proposals, I caution that prescriptions grounded in any single language risk overstating language-specific features and blurring the sources and dynamics of gender essentialism. 943. A Scene of Contention at the Limits of Sovereignty: Derrida, Rancière, and the Deconstructive Politics of Human Rights
Basturk, E. (2026). A Scene of Contention at the Limits of Sovereignty: Derrida, Rancière, and the Deconstructive Politics of Human Rights. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70087
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article develops a critical account of human rights through an encounter between Jacques Derrida and Jacques Rancière. Rather than treating their approaches as complementary components of a single theory, I argue that their disagreement reveals two irreducible dimensions of the politics of rights: the situated enactment of equality and the temporal non‐closure of every such enactment. Rancière shows how rights become political when those excluded from an existing distribution invoke their egalitarian inscriptions to stage a dissensus and enact themselves as political subjects. Derrida, through the distinction between law and justice and the temporality of the à venir, shows why no institutional or political articulation of equality can determine in advance the subjects and forms through which future claims may emerge. This reading rejects both the interpretation of deconstruction as an indefinite deferral of political action and the claim that Rancière fails to recognize the recurrence of exclusion through the politics/police distinction and le tort. Bringing these positions into tension, I argue that human rights require determinate legal and political forms while remaining contestable by claims those forms cannot anticipate. A deconstructive politics of human rights thus emerges in the interval between enactment and non‐closure: Equality must take political form without any particular form becoming its final horizon.

944. Sovereignty by Omission: Rethinking Agamben's State of Exception
Adami, M. (2026). Sovereignty by Omission: Rethinking Agamben's State of Exception. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70088
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article revisits Agamben's state of exception. Homo Sacer and State of Exception frame camps as spaces where law is suspended and life reduced to “bare life.” This decisionist paradigm underestimates the institutional and economic complexity of refugee management. This article proposes the concept of sovereignty by omission: Exclusion produced not by a decisive suspension of law but by legal ambiguity, bureaucratic deferral, and systemic inaction. Engaging Schmitt, Benjamin, Foucault, and Butler, the article situates omission within debates on sovereignty and rights and develops the argument through the case of Gaza. The argument rests on systematic analysis of legal, monitoring, and advocacy documents published between October 2023 and August 2025, each coded for acknowledgement, assignment, and execution. Gaza's blockade, siege, and overlapping legal regimes, invoked yet unenforced, exemplify omission, even as cultural and political life persists. Camps and enclaves are thus fractured fields of governance where abandonment and resilience coexist; sovereignty today manifests less in decision than in omission.

945. Reconceptualizing “Religious Revolutions”: An Introduction to Daryush Shayegan
Gaudin, C. (2026). Reconceptualizing “Religious Revolutions”: An Introduction to Daryush Shayegan. Constellations.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1467-8675.70089
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT Daryush Shayegan (1935–2018), a major yet underrecognized Iranian philosopher, offers a compelling framework for rethinking the Islamic revolution and broader religious‐political dynamics. This article introduces his thought to Anglophone audiences, challenging traditional interpretations of the 1979 revolution. Rather than a return to tradition, Shayegan views it as a modern event, in which religious symbols act as unconscious psychological defenses against change. Through a process he terms “ontological shift,” political and religious realms become intertwined, each repressing the other. These hybrid religious‐political symbols, detached from their original contexts, gain power through misrecognition. The study also extends Shayegan's insights beyond Iran, arguing that various fundamentalisms and populisms reflect similar reactions to existential disruption. It concludes by examining the notable academic oversight of his incisive analyses, highlighting the research trajectories that his rediscovery might generate.

946. The Embryo at the European Court of Human Rights
Scott, R. (2026). The Embryo at the European Court of Human Rights. Mod Law Rev.
https://doi.org/10.1111/1468-2230.70075
Abstract (original): Having a child by in vitro fertilisation (IVF) entails the creation of in vitro embryos, their transfer to a woman's uterus, a successful pregnancy, and live birth. Under the European Convention on Human Rights, using IVF principally engages the right to respect for private and family life under Article 8(1). The approach of the European Court of Human Rights (ECtHR) to the embryo is part of its jurisprudence regarding prenatal life, which it has considered under Article 8(2), including regarding the right to life under Article 2. Since an embryo currently needs a woman's uterus to have any chance of born life, questions about what legal status and claims to life it may have, if any, involve consideration of a woman's autonomy and bodily integrity interests under Article 8(1), and thus the legal relationship between the embryo and the woman – the central focus here. This paper principally identifies and analyses several problematic features of the ECtHR's jurisprudence regarding prenatal life as this relates to a woman, with particular reference to the embryo. It also considers the implications of a heightened concern for the embryo in a minority of States’ IVF laws, and in debates about heritable human genome editing.

947. Reconsidering the Role of Ethical Theories in Bioethics: Ethical Theories as Heuristic Descriptions of Moral Grammars
Christensen, A.S. (2026). Reconsidering the Role of Ethical Theories in Bioethics: Ethical Theories as Heuristic Descriptions of Moral Grammars. Bioethics.
https://doi.org/10.1111/bioe.70208
Abstract (original): ABSTRACT This article focuses on the question of how to understand the role and use of ethical theories in bioethics. Work in bioethics often involves uses of ethical theory, but methodological developments such as the empirical turn and the growing insights into the plurality and contextuality of moral considerations challenge a traditional view of ethical theories as providing moral input into the value‐neutral descriptions offered by empirical studies. Nevertheless, bioethics is characterised by a relative neglect of questions related to the use of ethical theories. The article develops a number of desiderata that any understanding of the role of ethical theories should be able to address; to accommodate moral plurality and contextuality, to motivate choices and uses of specific theories, to minimise risks of theory‐driven attention and blindness, to allow for theoretical plurality, to address relations between theories and theoretical integrity. It then presents some challenges for existing accounts of theory‐use in bioethics, thus paving the way for an alternative understanding of the role of ethical theories in bioethics. The suggestion presented here is that ethical theories should be understood as descriptions or representations of moral grammars , implicit normative structures of thinking and action that constitute moral considerations. Furthermore, in relation to problems in bioethics, it is suggested that ethical theories are used as heuristic tools to illuminate and clarify moral grammars as they appear in specific bioethical poblems or studies.

948. Modal Fragility and Counterfactual Causation
Stradis, A. (2026). Modal Fragility and Counterfactual Causation. Erkenntnis.
https://doi.org/10.1007/s10670-026-01174-0
Abstract (original): Abstract One of today’s foremost counterfactual analyses draws many causal links where it shouldn’t—not only towards the future, but also towards the past. The root of these problems is that unlikely events are too modally fragile to ground reasonable causal claims. However, I will argue that these issues arise just as readily for likely events, spelling double trouble for this analysis. By recentring this approach around probabilities instead, causation tracks structure whose modal robustness justifies its name, sidestepping the old problems and the new.

954. Price Personalization in the Age of AI: Complicating the Defense
Robson, G. (2026). Price Personalization in the Age of AI: Complicating the Defense. Business Ethics Quarterly.
https://doi.org/10.1017/beq.2026.10112
Abstract (original): Given the rapid rise of AI and its capacity to help firms customize consumer prices, price personalization has become a hot topic at the intersection of business ethics and technology studies. This article challenges the argument that price personalization would increase social welfare, addressing the claim that firms should charge more to people with greater willingness-to-pay (WTP), and less to people with lower WTP, because this will boost social welfare. I develop two reasons for doubt. First, preferences that influence WTP often do not conduce to consumers’ well-being. So, adjusting prices to preference-based WTP will not necessarily enhance social welfare. Second, firms’ long-term strategic agendas will often disfavor increased price personalization. If “ought implies can,” firms can morally be expected to do only what they reasonably can in competitive conditions.

956. Process Reliabilism is Permissive (and Plausible)
Powell, J. (2026). Process Reliabilism is Permissive (and Plausible). Episteme.
https://doi.org/10.1017/epi.2026.10167
Abstract (original): Abstract The debate between permissivists and impermissivists – which concerns the question of whether there is at most one epistemically rational doxastic attitude to adopt toward a proposition given a body of evidence – has been a focus of discussion in the literature. However, this debate has occurred almost exclusively within an internalist context. I aim to change that by introducing an externalist perspective into the debate. In particular, I will consider what a process reliabilist should think about the debate. Since process reliabilists do not typically talk about evidence, I will show how the debate can be translated into process reliabilist terms. In light of this translation, I will argue that process reliabilists should be permissivists. I will also argue that process reliabilism is a plausible version of permissivism because it can successfully respond to common objections to permissive views.

957. Can We Logically Tell Good from Bad Reasoning?
Zhang, L. (2026). Can We Logically Tell Good from Bad Reasoning?. Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1017/s0031819126101594
Abstract (original): Abstract The possibility of distinguishing good from bad reasoning – the precondition of any normative role for logic – has been challenged by a cluster of skeptical arguments in recent philosophy of logic. While much attention has focused on the gap between logical knowledge and ordinary reasoning highlighted by Harman and Russell, this paper identifies three additional arguments against logical normativity that have received insufficient scrutiny: one concerning the inherent limitations of formal evaluation methods, one concerning the pragmatic indeterminacy of illogical behavior in natural language communication, and one rooted in the early-twentieth-century doctrine of the ‘feeling of logicality’ directly opposed by Charles S. Peirce. The paper pursues two sequential aims: first, to show that skepticism about the logical normativity of reasoning is deeper and more historically entrenched than contemporary discussions suggest; second, to reconstruct Peirce’s theoretical framework – centered on the distinction between logica utens and logica docens , the tripartite account of good reasoning, and the method-based criterion of validity – and to demonstrate that it provides a unified response to all four skeptical challenges.

960. Polyvocal constitutionalism
Dixon, R. (2026). Polyvocal constitutionalism. International Journal of Constitutional Law (I-CON).
https://doi.org/10.1093/icon/moag108
Abstract (original): Abstract It is now widely understood that constitutions are interpreted and enforced by a broad range of actors—not simply courts. Much of constitutional theory has come to embrace this form of (what I call) “constitutional polyvocality,” or the idea of “polyvocal constitutionalism.” At the same time, there are several different versions of polyvocal constitutionalism. For instance, one set of polyvocal theories argues for collaboration among different constitutional actors and the importance of norms of comity, respect, and restraint in mediating any conflicts that arise in a process of polyvocal constitutional construction. Another set of ideas focuses on notions of accountability and the importance of judicial and fourth-branch impartiality, independence, and authority in the resolution of certain constitutional disputes, even in a polyvocal world. A final set of ideas focuses on the idea of contestation and disagreement among constitutional actors as potentially generative forces in a democratic constitutional system and as helping underpin the legitimacy of its outputs. Some theories also seek to balance these competing ideas in an open-ended way, whereas others seek to provide a more structured or “tiered” approach to balancing competing polyvocal constitutional ideas or values. This article explores these different understandings of the idea of polyvocal constitutionalism and how it helps us understand and situate a range of recent works in comparative constitutional theory. It further highlights the strengths and weaknesses of these different theories, especially their capacity to respond to the current political moment—in which many previously stable democracies are experiencing “abusive” constitutional change.

961. Creating Normativity and Responding to It: How Metaethical Constructivists Must and Can Have It Both Ways
Schneider, L. (2026). Creating Normativity and Responding to It: How Metaethical Constructivists Must and Can Have It Both Ways. European Journal of Philosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/ejop.70137
Abstract (original): Abstract This paper pursues two aims. First, it develops a novel objection to metanormative constructivism, which I term the Duality Objection . Second, it proposes a dual‐role account of practical reason and argues that it can meet this objection. Constructivists like Korsgaard and Street maintain that normativity is constructed within agents' first‐personal practical standpoints, shaped by their evaluative input. Valuing is prior to, and metaphysically grounds, value. However, a basic feature of our normative experience is that our evaluative judgements also respond to and track pre‐existing normative facts. Thus, evaluative commitments appear to play two seemingly contradictory roles – creating and responding to normative reality. This tension forms the basis of the Duality Objection. I respond in two steps. First, I show how constructivists can reconcile their metaphysical commitments with agents' normative experiences by distinguishing between first‐ and third‐personal levels of explanation. Second, I develop a Korsgaardian dual‐role account of practical reason , which elucidates the passive‐responsive and active‐creative roles that practical reason plays within our first‐personal agential standpoint. Making sense of “responsive” normative experience within a constructivist framework has a further upshot. It allows us to reject recent “arguments from first‐personal deliberation and experience” by metanormative realists (cf. Enoch 2007, 2011; Kirwin 2024).

962. Rethinking the Concept of Consciousness
Yücesan, E. (2026). Rethinking the Concept of Consciousness. Metaphilosophy.
https://doi.org/10.1111/meta.70054
Abstract (original): Abstract Today, research on consciousness spans various fields, including neuroscience, philosophy, psychology, physics, and computer science. These studies focus on demonstrating the effects of consciousness rather than defining its essence. While consciousness is often used as an overarching concept in thought processes, its nature remains unclear and is expected to be more precisely defined in the future. Some theorists propose that consciousness resides within neurons or the cytoskeleton. Examining the history of science reveals similar concepts that were once widely accepted but later proven nonexistent. This paper argues that consciousness is akin to aether and phlogiston—once considered scientific truths but ultimately discarded. While debates persist on whether consciousness is a fundamental reality or an illusion, the paper asserts that consciousness is merely a term, neither real nor illusory. As our molecular understanding of brain function deepens, the concept of consciousness, like aether and phlogiston, will eventually fade from scientific discourse.

965. Understanding Broken Colors
Kulvicki, J. (2026). Understanding Broken Colors. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.7799
Abstract (original): Ulrike Kern (2016) unpacks the history of a concept, broken colors. Though it’s been in use since the early seventeenth century, and is even used today by painters, critics, and art theorists when discussing color, it hasn’t much influenced philosophy. Instead, it evolved independently, alongside the early modern philosophical discussions of color. This paper has two goals. First, work out a philosophical reconstruction of the concept whose early history Kern so ably traces. So, this paper is a philosophical contribution to the history of ideas. The paper’s second goal is to show how broken colors contribute to two contemporary issues. First, they help us understand the depiction of things as colored, and second, they can deepen our understanding of the nature of chromatic experience. Broken colors are thus relevant to contemporary philosophy of art and perception. Though there are historically informed contemporary accounts of color on the market—Adams (2016) stands out—they typically address the history of philosophical discussions of color. And there is comparatively little work in the philosophy of art about the depiction of things as colored.

966. A Function-Based Account of Fittingness
Forcehimes, A.T. (2026). A Function-Based Account of Fittingness. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.8494
Abstract (original): Can we offer a constitutive account of fittingness? Offering such an account has proven difficult because of the great diversity of fit-assessable responses—e.g., believing, desiring, fearing, blaming, apologizing, thanking, and laughing. Yet, when surveying such examples of fit-apt  responses, what's striking is that invariably each response-type has a job, a goal, an end, a telos. Believing, desiring, fearing, blaming, apologizing, thanking, and laughing are all plausible candidates for having proper functions. The account I aim to defend runs with this idea. Put roughly, fittingness is the relation that holds between a response and its object just when and because the object has the properties that an object of that response needs to possess for the response to non-deviantly fulfill its function.

967. Essence and Grain
Ditter, A. (2026). Essence and Grain. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.8528
Abstract (original): The concept of essence is a paradigm case of a putatively hyperintensional concept in metaphysics, a concept that can draw distinctions between necessarily equivalent propositions. Yet as several authors have recently emphasized, fine-grained distinctions in metaphysics come with a substantial risk of inconsistency, due to the Russell-Myhill paradox and its variants. This concern is especially pressing for hyperintensionalists about essence because the hyperintensionality of essence has been motivated primarily by examples. This paper presents a novel, more systematic case for the hyperintensionality of essence that shows that the relevant distinctions can be systematically modeled in a natural theoretical framework. I start by offering a general heuristic to motivate a range of examples of fine-grained essentialist distinctions. I then present a higher-order logical framework for regimenting essentialist statements and provide a general argument for hyperintensionality on the basis of minimal assumptions about the logic of essence. Finally, I address the question of consistency by developing a semantic framework that demonstrates that the relevant fine-grained distinctions can indeed be consistently drawn.

968. Escape the Presentism–Eternalism Dilemma: Towards A Common-Sense A-Theory
Jackson, A. (2026). Escape the Presentism–Eternalism Dilemma: Towards A Common-Sense A-Theory. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.8658
Abstract (original): I propose a novel A-theory that attractively articulates our common-sense, pre-relativistic conception of time. This A-theory posits fundamental facts about how things were at a specific past times. It treats time’s passing by positing fundamental facts like: four hours passed from 8am today until noon. It allows fundamental cross-temporal relationships. I show that the presentism–eternalism dilemma does not embarrass the proposed A-theory. There are two notions of existence in play: existence at a time and ‘∃-existence’. Neither features in the fundamental facts. I define them up in a way that vindicates the claims of common sense. Past objects count as metaphysically ‘gone’, but there are fundamental facts about how they were. My account rejects presentism and eternalism (properly defined). I defend other aspects of the proposal elsewhere.

969. Seeing what to do: norms, scripts, and social affordances
Berio, L. & Musholt, K. (2026). Seeing what to do: norms, scripts, and social affordances. Philosophers’ Imprint.
https://doi.org/10.3998/phimp.8887
Abstract (original): Human cognition and behavior are guided by a myriad of norms which enable us to successfully navigate our social environment and our interactions with others. Many of these norms might be hard for us to articulate (e.g. how far to sit or stand from a stranger on the bus), and some of them differentiate between different hierarchies and roles, reflecting patterns of injustice. This paper addresses the fact that social norms, including gendered, ableist, adultist, and racist norms, influence our perception of action possibilities, or affordances, making some actions possible for some agents and not for others. In particular, we propose an account of these interactions in terms of scripts, that is, expected sequences of actions that shape the landscape of the possible ways to interact with one another. Scripts can be learned implicitly, they are dynamic and flexible, and can regulate complex interactions by prescribing permissible actions and responses in specific situations. A script-based account can explain the way norms regulate which actions we perceive as possible in a representationalist way, and can help us explain how norm change occurs.
